Foreword

The Andhra Pradesh State Council of Higher Education, in line with the  direc-
tions of the Hon'ble Chief Minister of Andhra Pradesh, Introduced English Medium
in all Degree programmes from the academic year 2021-22, As part of
empowering the students joining Degree programmes from vernacular medium,
the AP State Council of Higher Education is bringing out podcasts, video casts
and notably the bilingual text Books. These bilingual books are writhen in
English, with the gist of the content in Telugu to enable the students to
comprehend the content in their mother tongue. The bilingual text book is
envisioned on the core concept of Outcome Based Education, highlighting the
learning outcomes for every chapter, These are better called as bilingual
resources rather than textbooks, as the APSCHE has developed a template for the
bilingual textbooks designing them with concepts and frameworks going beyond
the usual reading material.

Furthering the detailed description of the topics, as per the common syllabus of
the Redesigned Curricular Framework for Cholce Based Credit System, the
bilingual text book contains Glossary, where certain important terms which the
student might be unfamiliar with are identified and explained in one or two
sentences, which is not a mere dictionary meaning. Links to online videos or
audios which will be useful for further reading and understanding of the topics are
given under the Interactive links. To foster further reading, information on online
resources, articles or another text book pertaining to the content are provided. To
make the text book more of a resourceful book, Curricular Activities, wherein
sugpested activities that could be taken up in realization of the outcomes are
provided for the benefit of students. To help the students to assess understanding
of the content, Self Assessment instruments are provided. For Advanced
Learners, caters to the needs of advanced learners providing them with additional
material about the topics. Finally, for every chapter References are provided.

--- PAGE 007 ---
HISTORY & CULTURE OF ANDHRA
(FROM 1512 TO 1956 AD)

Learning Outcomes:
After successful completion of this course, the student will be able to:

>

>

Interpret social and political and cultural transformation from medieval
tomodern Andhra

Relate key historical developments during medieval period occurring
in coastal Andhra and Telangana regions and analyze socio - political
andeconomic changes under Qutb Shahi rulers

Understand gradual change, or change in certain aspects of society
inAndhra, rather than rapid or fundamental changes

Explain how the English East India Company became the most
dominantpower and outline the impact of colonial policies on different
aspects in Andhra

Outline the issues related to caste, women, widow remarriage, child
marriage, social reforms and the laws and policies of colonial
administration towards these issues
Take pride in the non-violence struggle for Indian Independence
and relatethe importance of peace in everyday life
Apply the knowledge of the regional history to understand the
regional, linguistic and other cultural aspirations of the present day
society
Visualize where places are in relation to one another through map
pointing

SYLLABUS

Unit-I Andhra through 16" & 19" Centuries AD: Evolution of

CompositeCulture - The Qutb Shahis of Golkonda -
Administration, Society & Economy - Literature &
Architecture; Advent of European and settlements in Andhra-
Occupation of Northern Circars and Ceeded Districts - Early
revolts against the British

Unit-II Andhra under British rule : Administration — Land Revenue

Settlements-Society-Education-Religion-Impact of Industrial
Revolution on Economy-Peasantry & Famines-Contribution
of Sir Thomas Munroe & C. P. Brown-Impact of 1857 Revolt
in Andhra.


--- PAGE 008 ---
Unit - III Social Reform & New Literary Movements: Kandukuri

Veeresalingam, Raghupathi Venkata Rathnam Naidu, Guruzada
Apparao, KomarrajuVenkata Laxmana Rao; New Literary
Movements: Rayaprolu Subbarao, Viswanatha
Sathyanarayana, Gurram Jashua, Boyi Bheemanna, Sri Sri.

Unit - I1V Freedom Movement in Andhra (1885-1947): Vandemataram

Movement-Home Rule Movement in Andhra-Non-
Cooperation Movement -Alluri Seetarama Raju & Rampa
Revolt (1922-24)-Civil Disobedience Movement -Quit India
Movement.

Unit- V Movement for separate Andhra State (1953) and AP (1956):

Causes-Andhra Maha Sabha-Conflict between Coastal
Andhra & Rayalaseema-Sri Bagh Pact-work of various
Committees-Martyrdom of Potti Sriramulu-Formation of
separate Andhra State (1953); Movement for formation of
Andhra Pradesh (1956): Visalandhra Mahasabha-Role of
Communists-States Reorganization Committee- Gentlemen’s
Agreement-Formation of Andhra Pradesh.

Unit-I
Andhra through 16" & 19" Centuries AD:

1.1 Learning Outcomes

1.2 Introduction

1.3. Evolution of Composite Culture

1.4 The Qutb Sahis of Golconda

1.5 Administration

1.6 Society & Economy

1.7 Literature & Architecture

1.8 Advent of European and Settlements in Andhra

1.9 Occupation of Northern Circars and Ceded Districts

1.10 Summary

1.11 Model Questions and Answers (MCQ)

1.12 References

1.1: Learning Outcomes :

After successful completion of this unit, the student will be able to

> Know the Qutb Shahis of Golkonda and their administration.

> Understand the socio-economic, political, and literary developments
under Qutb Shahis of the Golconda kingdom.

>» Learn about the evolution of composite culture in Andhra Pradesh.

> Elaborate on the art and architecture developments under Qutb
Shahis.

> Know the history of the Europeans in Andhra and how they settled
in Andhra.

>» Visualise the occupation of the Northern Circars and how they
occupied the ceded districts in Andhra Pradesh.

>» Explain the early revolts against the British in Andhra Pradesh.

1.2: Introduction :

The disintegration and dismemberment of the Tuglug Empire saw
the rise of many kingdoms, and the Bahmani Sultanate of Deccan was
one of them. Allauddin Hasan Gangu, one of the nobles in the court of
Muhammad-bin-Tugluq, founded the Bahmani kingdom in Deccan in
1347 C.E. The Bahmani sultans and the Vijayanagara kings fought wars
for a couple of centuries to establish their hold over the Tungabhadra
doab, the Krishna-Godavari delta, and the Marathwada country. Firoz
Shah was the greatest sultan of the Bahmani dynasty. He ruled the Bahmani

--- PAGE 014 ---
kingdom from 1397 C.E. to 1446 C.E. The Bahmani kingdom gradually
expanded and reached the height of its power and territorial limits during
the prime ministership of Muhammad Gawan. He was the prime minister
of Muhammad Shah III. Due to the jealousy of nobles, Muhammad
Gawan was executed in 1482 C.E. This was the starting point for the
downfall of the Bahmani kingdom. Soon the Bahmani kingdom was
divided into five principalities, viz., Golconda, Bijapur, Berar, Bidar, and
Ahmadnagar. The Bahmani kingdom acted as a cultural bridge between
the North and south, but it was disappeared by the end of the fifteenth
century. This paved the way for the rise of the Qutb Sahis of Golconda.
The Qutb Shahi dynasty played a very important role in the mediaeval
history of the Deccan. They not only maintained the balance of power in
the Deccan but also checked for a long time, with the assistance of the
other Deccan powers, the penetration of Mughuls in the Deccan and
south India. The Qutb Shahis belonged to a Turkish tribe known as
Karakanelu. They were the original inhabitants of Persia and Trans-
Oxiana. They ruled their principalities with Tabries and Hamdam as their
capitals. Kara, Yusuf, Sikander, and Jahan Shah were the remarkable
personalities of this tribe. Akunelu was the other important Turkish tribe
that was ruling some parts of Persia and Transoxiana. There was no love
lost between these two Turkish tribes. Finally, the latter drove the former
from Persia and Transoxiana. Thus, Quli Qutb, a member of the
Karakanelu tribe, left West Asia and came to India in search of fortune.
Fortunately, he came to Deccan, the land of fortune hunters, and got a
job in the court of Muhammad Shah III (1482 C.E. to 1518 C.E.), the
Bahmani sultan of Bidar.

Muhammad Shah III was an imbecile sultan. He spent much of
his time in the company of buffoons and fiddlers. It may be stated here
that 18 rulers ruled the Bahmani kingdom. Five of them were murdered;
two of them died of intemperance; three of them were deposed; and two
of them were blinded. Thus, there was neither peace nor solidarity in the
Bahmani kingdom. Added to this, the tyrannical nature of their rule and
their incompetency sounded the death knell of the Bahmani kingdom.
The Qutb Shahi kingdom rose out of the ruins of the Bahmani kingdom.
The incompetency of Muhammad Shah II], the cliques in the court, and
the activities of the power-hungry Amir Ali Barid, ‘the cunning fox of
Deccan’, set in motion the forces of disintegration, which were

systematically exploited by Quli Qutb Shah to assert independence and
to carve out a kingdom of his own. During the reign of Muhammad Shah
IH, Quli Qutb Shah became the governor of the Telangana region. By
sheer merit and hard work, he won the admiration of his overlord. In the
meantime, the Bahmani kingdom was occupied by the Barid Shahis of
Bidar, and accordingly, Quli Qutb Shah, like the rulers of Bijapur, Berar,
and Ahmadnagar, declared him independent and started ruling his kingdom
under the name of Quli Qutb Shah.

Andhradesa is available only in the European travellers’ accounts,
like those of Tavernier, Thevenot, Bernier, and Abbe Carre, all French;
Thomas Bowry, English; and Niccolao Manucci, an Italian. All of them
belonged to the second half of the seventeenth century, visited the
Golconda kingdom, and have given us first-hand information, though
scanty, of the conditions prevailing here, as well as of their own activities
and those of other European settlers.

1.3: Evolution of Composite Culture:

During the 17" and 18" centuries, many people from Central
Asia, Persia, and northern India migrated to Andhra Pradesh. The Qutb
Shahi rulers were great admirers of Persian art and literature. They too
welcomed the influx of Iranian scholars, poets, and architects from time
to time and offered them high posts, both in military and civil administration.
The composite culture of Andhra Pradesh has a captive nature. It has a
magnetic property that attracts a lot of people, and the inquisitiveness
increases the more the person tries to get into the knowledge of Andhra
culture and traditions. Our Andhra Pradesh region has immense diversity
in lifestyle, traditions, customs, outlook, religion, region, caste, and much
more. People across the country are surprised to understand the unity in
our thinking and affection for the motherland with such a vivid and varied
lifestyle among the citizens. This is the beauty of the composite culture of
India. From its historic evolution and development, the Andhra region
has undergone several changes since its foundation as the capital of the
mediaeval kingdom of Golconda within the sixteenth century to its present
standing as the metropolis of a contemporary state. Not only Hinduism
influenced Islam, but Islam too influenced Hinduism.

The Golconda, under the influence of Deccan, Persian, and Telugu
cultures, synthesised and evolved its own Andhra culture. Despite this
heterogeneity, the culture that bound society was based on religious

tolerance, broad-mindedness, and a humane outlook. The Muslims and

Hindus borrowed heavily from each other’s cultural traditions. However,

it was Ibrahim Quli Qutb Shah, who had a long stay at the Vijayanagara

court during his exile, who picked up Indian socio-cultural norms and
traditions. The rulers-more so, those of Bijapur and Golconda-
encouraged this attitude, and the nobles and Sufis supported it. Thus, the
literature, architecture, social and religious rituals and ceremonies of the
period show a happy fusion of foreign and local traditions. In the age of

Quli Qutb Shah’s rule, Persian social norms and traditions became

Indianized to a great extent. It was a period of soft blending of Persian

cultural trends with Indian social norms and traditions to pave the way

for the new composite culture, which attained its fullest fruition during the
reigns of Abdullah Qutb Shah and Abul Hasan, the last ruler of the dynasty.

1.4: The Qutb Sahis of Golconda: Quli Qutb Shah

(1518-1543 C.E.):

Sultan Quli Qutb Shah, the founder of the dynasty, was a royal
family of Hamdan. He served the Bahmanis faithfully was appointed
governor of Telangana in 1492 C.E., and received the title ‘Qutb Shah’
after the dynasty had attained independence. He declared independence
after the death of his patron king, Muhammad Shah, in 1518 C.E. Sultan
Quli controlled the province of Telangana for nearly 60 years, first as a
general or governor and later as an independent king. During this period,
he extended the territory up to the seaport of Masulipatnam, or
Machilipatnam. He was contemporary to Sri Krishnadevaraya,
Achuyutadevaraya of the Vijayanagara rulers, Babur, the founder of the
Mughal dynasty, and Sher Shah Suri.

Achievements of Quli Qutb Shah :

1. Quli Qutb Shah fortified the fort of Golconda and named it
Muhammadnagar in honour of his patron, Muhammad Shah III.

2. After consolidating his position in his kingdom, he set himself the
task of expanding his position at the expense of his neighbours. In
this connection, he came into conflict with the Vijayanagara emperor,
Sri Krishnadevaraya, who unsettled the settled political setup of the
Vijayanagara Empire. It was during this time that Quli Qutb Shah
resolved to implement his policy of expansion. He first defeated the
Velama chiefs and occupied the Rachakonda and Devarakonda
areas. Shitab Khan, also known as Sitapathi, was the most powerful

--- PAGE 020 ---
ruler of the Warangal region. He was also a friend and ally of the
Gajapatis of Orissa. Quli invaded Warangal, defeated Shitab Khan,
and occupied that region.

3. Quli Qutb Shah then proceeded to the coastal Andhra, defeated the
feudatories of the Gajapati king, and annexed Rajahmundry, Eluru,
and the other regions.

4. Quli Qutb Shah also decided to wage war against the sultans of
Bidar and Bijapur. In these wars, he faced success and occupied
Kovilakonda and Medak. These conquests made Quli Qutb Shah
the master of the entire state of Telangana and coastal Andhra
Pradesh. The region located between Krishna and Godavari came
directly under his control.

He was a contemporary of Babar (1526-1530 C.E.) and
Humayun (1530-1556 C.E.). These two Mughal emperors had no
opportunity to divert their attention towards the Deccan. He achieved
the political unification of Andhra Pradesh to some extent. He fortified
the fort of Golconda by raising massive walls and a moat around it. This
became one of the strongest forts in the Deccan. Quli Qutb Shah ruled
for thirty-one years and lived up to ninety years. He had four sons, viz.,
Hyder Quli, Qutubuddin, Jamshed Quli, and Ibrahim Quli. Hyder Quli
predeceased, and hence Quli Qutb Shah proclaimed his second son,
Qutubuddin, as his successor. But Jamshed Quli murdered his father and
elder brother and usurped the throne.

Jamshid Quli (1543-1550 C.E.):

Jamshid reigned for seven years until 1550 C.E. but remained
maligned by all for his patricidal crime. He failed to gain the goodwill of
his subjects on account of his despicable acts and rude behaviour, but he
became unpopular among his subjects and people, for he had murdered
his father and elder brother, although he was a good soldier and a man of
learning. His youngest brother Ibrahim, with the help of Ali Barid, the
sultan of Bidar, attacked Golconda. Jamshid, with the support of Sultan
Burhan of the Nizam Shahi dynasty of Ahmadnagar, defeated Ibrahim
and his allies. After this defeat, Ibrahim retired to Vijayanagara, where
he was received with sympathy by Aliya Ramaraya, the Vijayanagara
ruler. Ibrahim stayed there for seven years and learned the intricacies of
statecraft.

11


--- PAGE 022 ---
The kingdom of Golconda was surrounded by four more Muslim
kingdoms, i.e., Bidar, Berar, Bijapur, and Ahmadnagar. Jamshid’s
uncommon tact and diplomacy not only increased his power but also
successfully maintained the balance of power in the Deccan. In the first
instance, allying himself with the sultans of Ahmadnagar and Bidar,
Jamshid defeated the sultan of Bijapur and occupied Medak. In the
second place, he successfully checked the rise of Ahmadnagar and Bijapur
by allying for some time with the sultan of Bidar and with the sultans of
Bijapur and Bidar.

Jamshid, though he came to the throne after much bloodshed,
reserved and promoted the prestige of the dynasty. He died in 1550
C.E. The nobles of the court were divided into two groups. One group
supported the claims of Subhan Quli, the son of Jamshid, whereas the
other group extended a welcome to Ibrahim, who was in the court of
Ramaraya, to come to Golconda and to occupy the throne. Ibrahim,
who got the support of the nobles and people, came to Golconda,
captured the throne, and started his reign in 1550 C.E.

Ibrahim Qutb Shah (1550-1580 C.E.):

Ibrahim Qutb Shah was the last son of Quli Qutb Shah. He is
popularly known to the poetic world as ‘Malkibharam’. He was the real
architect of the Golconda kingdom. He ruled the kingdom for thirty years,
from 1550 to 1580 C.E., and consolidated it. Ibrahim stayed in the court
of Sadasivaraya, the Vijayanagara emperor, for seven years. Ramaraya,
the prime minister of Sadasivaraya, treated him with cordiality and
decorum. Ibrahim maintained friendly relations with Ramaraya when he
became the sultan of Golconda. Besides, Ibrahim and the sultan of
Ahmadnagar supported Ramaraya against the sultan of Bijapur. Ibrahim
married the daughter of Hussain Nizam Shah I of Ahmadnagar and took
a leading part in forming an alliance of the Deccan Sultans against
Vijayanagara. But Ibrahim changed his attitude towards Vijayanagara
for the following reasons:

1. The changing alliances of Ramaraya with one Muslim ruler against
the other annoyed Ibrahim Qutb Shah.

2. Ramaraya gave asylum to a certain Jagadekarao, who was the enemy
of Ibrahim.

3. Ibrahim, who apprehended danger from the rising power of Bijapur,
allied with the sultan of Ahmadnagar. Ramaraya, who disliked this

--- PAGE 024 ---
alliance, invaded the kingdom of Golconda and occupied the fort of
Panugal and other regions.

Due to these reasons, Ibrahim severed his relations with the
Vijayanagara and turned every stone to create a grand alliance of the
sultans of Deccan against the Vijayanagara. He accomplished this task,
and the result of this alliance was the battle of Tallikota, fought in the year
1565 C.E. In this battle, the Vijayanagara forces were routed by the
combined armies of the Deccan sultans. Hussain Nizam Shah of
Ahmadnagar murdered Ramaraya on the battlefield. The Muslim forces
occupied Kondavidu, Machilipatnam, and the coastal region. The
territories came under the control of Ibrahim. The forces of Ibrahim not
only destroyed the temples at Ahobilam but also looted the treasury of
the temple and occupied the Udayagiri and Penugonda regions. Ibrahim
thus dealt a mortal blow to the prosperous Vijayanagara Empire.
Other measures of Ibrahim :

1. Quli Qutb Shah and his successors were interested in the expansion
and consolidation of the kingdom, and hence there was no time for
them to introduce any sound administrative measures. Thus, Ibrahim
took concerted measures to perfect the administration. He established
central and provincial administrative systems and introduced perfect
rapport between these administrative wings. Able, honest, and
efficient people were appointed to look after the administration. He
also introduced spies to supply the required information to the central
and provincial administrators.

2. The kingdom of Golconda was infested with robberies. They proved
to be a source of hindrance to the people and the trade and
commerce of the country. He punished them very heavily and thereby
restored law and order.

3. Ibrahim developed communication facilities. This, in turn, paved the
way for the development of trade and commerce.

4. Ibrahim took a lot of interest in the welfare of the people. In this
connection, he excavated many canals and constructed tanks and
dams. The tanks at Ibrahimpatnam and Hussainsagar were excavated
during his time. These activities paved the way for the development
of agriculture.

He also laid out many beautiful gardens. Ibrahim Bagh and Pool
Bagh were laid by him. He built many towns, mosques, magnificent

--- PAGE 026 ---
palaces, and bridges. The present bridge lies across the river Musi
in the city of Hyderabad and was constructed by him.
Muhammad Quli Qutb Shah (1580-1612 C.E.)

Ibrahim was succeeded by Muhammad Quli. He came to the
throne at the very tender age of fifteen. He was a man of perseverance
and assiduity. He mastered them within no time and started ruling the
kingdom with political foresight and breadth of judgement. During his
time, the kingdom of Golconda embraced the whole of Andhra Pradesh.
His reign was peaceful and prosperous. Muhammad Quli was also a
great poet, writer, and builder. The layout of the city of Hyderabad was
a great achievement of his regime. The city was laid out in 1591 C.E.
with fine taste and geometrical accuracy and was embellished with
magnificent buildings, straight roads, and other civic amenities such as
banking transactions. For this purpose, he invited several Persian families
to settle down in Hyderabad as well as in Machilipatnam. Mohammad
Quli was a scholar and a great poet. He composed numerous poems in
Deccan Urdu on various topics of literary, political, and social character.
Mir Mamin Astrabadi, a great scholar and philosopher, was in his court.
Telaganarya dedicated his work, Yayaticharitra, to him. Muhammad
Quli died in 1612 C.E. and was succeeded by his nephew and son-in-
law, Sultan Muhammad Qutb.

Muhammad Qutb Shah (1612-1626 C.E.)

Muhammad Qutb was the son-in-law of Muhammad Quli. He
was a great scholar and a patron of men of letters. He, like his
predecessors, was a great builder. He was kind and humane and led a
very pious life, fully devoted to religious practises. He followed his uncle
in promoting various activities in the fields of learning and architecture.
He was responsible for laying the foundation of the Macca Masjid, which
was completed by his successors. His queen, Hayat Bakshi Begum, also
did some service for the diffusion of education and learning in the Golconda
kingdom.

The Mughals at this time slowly penetrated the Deccan. Akbar
made attempts through diplomacy to establish his control over the Deccan.
Except for Khandesh, no other Muslim state in the Deccan was prepared
to accept the authority of the Mughals. Muhammad Quli received the
Mughal envoy and sent presents to Akbar, but he refused to become a
vassal of the Mughals. Muhammad Qutb was a contemporary of Jahangir.

--- PAGE 028 ---
Jahangir also showed interest in the affairs of the Deccan. At this time,
the kingdom of Ahmadnagar, under the able guidance of Malik Amber,
not only recovered the lost possessions but also expelled the Mughals
from that quarter. Muhammad Qutb extended his support to Ahmadnagar;
Jahangir conducted two major campaigns against Ahmadnagar and faced
success. Prince Kurram, who later became Shahjahan, defeated Malik
Amber in the battle of Balaghat. Finally, Mughal authority was established
over Berar, Ahmadnagar, and Balaghat. After this, Jahangir got the
subordinate alliance of Bijapur and Golconda. In one of the Farmans,
Jahangir addressed the Sultan of Golconda as his son. Muhammad Qutb
ruled for fourteen years and died in 1626 C.E. He was succeeded by his
son, Abdullah Qutb Shah.

Abdullah Qutb Shah (1626-1672 C.E.):

Abdullah Husain Qutb Shah came to the throne at a very tender
age. During his boyhood, the administration became rotten, cliques in
the court became frequent, and the people were very agitated. When he
grew up and assumed control of the administration, he showed more
interest in leading a life of ease and luxury than looking after the interests
of the people and kingdom. Incompetent and selfish officials started
exploiting their power for the promotion of their interests. Thus, the
administration was paralytic and decadent. Added to this, the Mughals
cast their eyes upon the Muslim kingdoms of the Deccan.

Shahjahan was the Mughal contemporary of Abdullah Qutb Shah
of Golconda. The Mughal emperor realised, after taking into consideration
the activities of Malik Amber, that there could be no peace for the Mughals
in the Deccan as long as Ahmadnagar remained an independent state.
This was a major departure from the policy followed by Akbar and
Jahangir. Thus, the end of the Nizam Shahi dynasty and the annexation
of Ahmadnagar into the Mughal Empire were achieved by Shahjahan in
1635 C.E. Then Qutb Shahi, the ruler, acknowledged the supremacy of
the Mughals and even struck coins in the name of Shahjahan. The ruler
of Bijapur refused to submit to the authority of Shahjahan, and the result
was that his territory was invaded by the Mughals. According to this, the
sultan of Bijapur refused to submit to the authority of Shahjahan, and the
result was that his territory was invaded by the Mughals. According to
this, the sultan of Bijapur was forced to conclude a treaty with the Mughals.
The Byapur Sultan became a tributary of the Mughals. Then Aurangzeb

--- PAGE 030 ---
was appointed governor of Deccan, and he was in that post for a long

time.

Aurangzeb, as the viceroy of Deccan, invaded Golconda in the
year 1637 C.E. Abdullah Qutb did not pay the stipulated tribute as per
the treaty of 1636 C.E. with the Mughals. Secondly, the Qutb Shahi
sultan, without the permission of the Mughal emperor, conquered some
parts of Karnataka. Aurangzeb demanded that Abdullah pay a heavy
amount in the form ofa fine for his act of defiance. Finally, the refractory
activities of Mirjumla precipitated the matter. Mirjumla, at the instance
of Abdullah, conquered the Karnataka region. During these campaigns,
he amassed a lot of wealth, and with it, he bought the nobles and
commanders of the army. Finally, he defied the authority of the sultan.
This annoyed the sultan of Golconda, and he tried to chastise him. But
Mirjumla sought the help of Aurangzeb against his patron, Abdullah
Qutb.

Aurangzeb, who had been determined to chastise the Shiva
Sultan, invaded Golconda. Abdullah could not withstand the onslaught
of the Mughal forces. He at last sought the help of Shahjahan. When
victory was in sight, Shahjahan ordered Aurangzeb to stop the seizure of
the Golconda. Accordingly, a treaty was concluded between Aurangzeb
and Abdulah Qutb. The provisions of the treaty are as follows:

1. The Sultan of Golconda agreed to pay 16 lakhs of rupees as war
indemnity. He also agreed to pay tribute.

2. Abdullah Qutb agreed to offer his daughter in marriage to Prince
Muhammad, the son of Aurangzeb.

3. Theconfiscated property of Mirjumla by the sultan was restored to
her. Thus Abdullah Qutb became the subordinate of the Mughal
emperor Shahjahan.

Abul Hasan (1672-1687 C.E.):

Abdul Hasan was the last Qutb Shahi sultan. He was popularly
known as Zanishah in the Deccan. He was the son-in-law of Abdullah
Qutb. He was an Arabian who came to India and ultimately became the
sultan of Golconda. He was an excellent administrator. He followed the
policy of religious toleration. The appointment of Akkanna and Madanna
as commander and prime minister and his policy of cooperation and
friendship with the rising Maratha ruler, Shivaji, speak volumes of his
enlightened rule. But Aurangzeb and the Mughal historians brand him as

a pleasure hunter and an incompetent ruler. One need not give much
credence to these biassed opinions and writings.

Abul Hasan maintained cordial relations with Shivaji. He invited
Shivaji to Hyderabad and accorded him a rousing reception. He
supported Shivaji by sending troops and offering money for his southern
campaigns. Through these acts, Abul Hasan got some important regions
like Bellary and Gandikota. But very soon he was involved in a titanic
struggle against the Mughals. Aurangzeb decided to see the end of the
Muslim kingdoms of the Deccan. In the first instance, he invaded Bijapur.
He not only defeated Adil Shahi, ruler of Bijapur, but also annexed the
Bijapur kingdom into the Mughal Empire. Thus, Bijapur disappeared
from the political map of the Deccan. Then he decided to see the end of
Golconda. The following are some of the causes of the attack on the
Golconda by Aurangzeb:

1. Abul Hasan of Golconda was a Shiva, whereas Aurangzeb was a

2. The Sultan of Golconda was a friend and ally of Shivaji, who was
the arch-enemy of Aurangzeb.

3. The appointment of Akkanna and Madanna as commanders and
prime ministers by Abul Hasan incurred the displeasure of Aurangzeb.

4. Finally, Abul Hasan supported the cause of Byapur when Aurangzeb
attacked it.

Aurangzeb dispatched a huge army under the command of his
son, Muazam. The fort of Golconda became a target of attack. The
siege of Golconda continued for some months. At last, the emperor
Aurangzeb himself came to conduct the siege operations, but these
operations failed. Then Aurangzeb had recourse to bribery and gained
admittance through the treachery of one of the officers of the garrison
who opened the gates of the fort. Abul Hasan was captured and made a
prisoner. The kingdom of Golconda was annexed into the Mughal Empire
in 1687 C.E. The Qutb Shahi rulers ruled the Golconda kingdom for
two centuries. In 1687 C.E., their glory and grandeur sank into the sea
of Mughals. But the cultural contribution of the Qutb Shahis was
everlasting.

1.5: Administration:

The kingdom was divided into many administrative divisions.
Tarafs (provinces), Sarkars (districts), Paraganas (talukas), and villages
were the principal administrative divisions of the kingdom. T7arafdar was

23


--- PAGE 034 ---
the head of a Taraf. Sarkars were kept under the control of Fauzdars.
They were helped by Havaldars. The latter were also in charge of ports.
But the principal poets were kept under the control of officers like Shah
Bandar. The Paraganas were kept under the control of several officers
like Deshpande, Deshmukh, Thanadar, Majumdar, Kulkarni, etc.
The village administration was kept under the control of Muqaddam,
Karanam, etc. Besides, the village elders association, in the form of
Panchyat, was in charge of the general administration of the village.
Revenue Administration:

The main source of income for the state was land revenue. The
tax farming system was the principal feature of the revenue administration.
According to this system, the right to collect land revenue in a particular
area was auctioned in public. Thus, the highest bidder was empowered
to collect the land revenue of a particular area. He was expected to pay
that fixed amount in the auction to the state. He was allowed to collect
from peasants whatever he desired to collect. This ultimately paved the
way for peasant exploitation. The oppressive policy followed by the tax
farmers led to the mass migration of peasants to a place of safety on
many occasions. Besides land revenue, customs dues also formed another
important source of revenue for the state.

Defense Administration:

The Qutb Shahi rulers never maintained a standing army. They
depended on the feudal levies supplied by the nobles and the Jagirdars.
The main wings of the army were the infantry, cavalry, and artillery. The
cavalry and artillery wings were managed by the Mughals, Persians, and
English. But during the time of Abul Hasan, certain changes occurred in
the army organisation. The sultan depended more on the standing army
than the feudal levies supplied by the nobles and Jagirdars. The Qutb
Shahis honoured and valued the skill and talent of many foreigners and
rewarded them by elevating them to exalted positions and ranks, offering
attractive service terms and conditions, including high salaries. The
foreigners contributed immensely to the mighty strength of the Golconda
army. It is also clear that the Qutb Shahi army was composed of
multiracial, multilingual, and multiethnic elements and rulers and accorded
importance to merit and capacity.

Judicial Administration:

The Qutb Shahis sharply deviated from the judicial administration

introduced by the Bahmanis. The judicial administration of the Qutb Shahis

was based on Shariat (Islamic law), royal farmans, and local traditions
based on Hindu law. The decision of the Sultan was final. Next to the
sultan, the judicial powers were enjoyed by the Shariat Panch. He was
the chief justice. He must be one of the ministers. Owazi was next to
him. Every city was provided with a court and a judge. They were under
the control of Shariat Panchi. In every paragana, Qwazi, Havaldar,
and Mazlis were to act as judges. Mazlis was in charge of civil cases.
Thanedar was on the lowest rung of the judicial ladder.

Special officers called Mahasibs or Mustasibs were in charge of public
morals. They discharged more or less the same functions as the Mustasibs
introduced by Aurangzeb in the Mughal Empire. The punishments were
very severe during the time of the Qutb Shahis. Traitors and revolters
were punished with death sentences. For theft, amputation of limbs was
the punishment. The Qutb Shahis were impartial in executing the judicial
administration. Sultan Muhammad Quli established ‘Aman Mahal’, the
Hall of Peace. This enabled the sultan to personally know the grievances
of the people. This shows that the Qutb Shahis were in touch with public
opinion. However, it is evident from the accounts left by Boswell that in
most of the cases, the lower officials arbitrarily decided the cases and
thereby did untold damage to the rights of the people.

One healthy policy that was followed by the Qutb Shahi rulers in
the administrative organisation was the appointment of non-Muslims to
posts of prominence. Quli Qutb, though he was the enemy of the rulers

of Vijayanagara, allowed Ramaraju to occupy a position of prominence
in the administration. Similarly, Jamshid patronised Jagdekarao and gave
hima post of importance. During the time of Ibrahim Qutb Shah, Murahari
Rao became a very popular personality. During the time of Abdullah
Qutb, Brahmins occupied important positions in the administration. The
prime minister of Abul Hasan was Madanna. Akkanna was made an
officer in the army. Gopanna was made the revenue officer in charge of
the Badrachalam region.

1.6: Society and Economy: Social Structure:

In the Qutb Shahi dynasty, Muslims, Hindus, and Christians lived
together. The Qutb Shahi sultans belonged to the Shia sect of Muslims.
Shia had taken high positions in the administration of Qutb Shahis.
Brahmanas occupied a prominent position in society. They acted as
ministers, commanders, and tax farmers. They were supposed to be the
custodians of knowledge and learning. They were also experts in astrology
and astronomy. Vaisyas monopolised trade and commerce. The
European trading companies used to purchase goods through them. They
were supposed to be experts in accounting and mathematics. Sudras
were the agriculturists. Besides, they acted as servants to the nobles in
the cities and also as soldiers. They formed the bulk of the population.
The other important social class was the untouchables, who lived outside
the villages. They were the tanners. They eat the flesh of dead animals.
They were shoemakers, shoe polishers, and all sorts of manual labourers.
Heavy punishments were inflicted on them. They were the most unclean
lot.

Women position:

During the Qutb Shahi period, women’s positions were not fine.
She was only valued in domestic life. It is known from the testimony of
foreigners that the women of this period, particularly Hindu women,
enjoyed a considerable amount of social freedom. Muslim women
followed the paradha system. Child marriages were in vogue. The position
of widows was deplorable. The reason was the prevalence of the Satis
system. They were not allowed to enjoy any socio-economic freedom.
They were ill-treated in society in various ways. The contemporary Telugu
author Ponnaganti Telaganarya, in his work Yayathi Charitra, informs
us about the position of women.

--- PAGE 040 ---
The courtesans enjoyed considerable importance and significance in
society. According to Tavernier’s works, there are nearly twenty thousand
courtesans in the Golconda kingdom. They became a source of revenue
for the state, for they paid taxes to the state. They were expected to
dance in the court regularly. They maintained shops as adjuncts to their
houses, where all kinds of intoxicating drinks and drugs were available in
abundance. The state used to levy taxes on these shops. Thus, the
courtesans became a potential source of income and a perpetual source
of enjoyment for the people in the Golconda kingdom. The Devadasis
also enjoyed a high social status.

Economic Prosperity:

It is evident from the accounts left by the European officers,
travellers, and merchants that agriculture was flourishing in the Golconda
kingdom. The sultans of this period took particular interest in improving
irrigational works. There were places where one to three crops were
cultivated. The coastal area was full of fertile lands, and their rice was
grown in plenty. The agricultural surplus gave tremendous impetus to the
development of trade, commerce, and industry. Muhammad Quli
maintained friendly relations with the Shah of Persia. This enabled him to
bring some people interested in doing trade from Persia and provide
facilities for them to settle in cities like Hyderabad and Machilipatnam.
He encouraged trade guilds. At this time, the English East India Company
was trying to establish trade centres in Andhra Pradesh. Muhammad
Quli permitted them to establish trade centres at Machilipatnam in 1611
C.E.

Many of the cities and towns located in the Golconda kingdom
in general and the coastal cities in particular were famous for producing
different products and articles. Machilipatnam was famous for producing
different products and articles. Machilipatnam was famous for producing
the finest quality of muslin. Paritala, Malapalli, and Golapalli were famous
for diamond mines. It is evident from the account given by the French
traveller Tavernier that, in the diamond mines of Kolluru, the famous
Kohinoor diamond was discovered. Its weight was 500 carats.
Banaganipali, Munimadugu, and Timmapuram, located in the present
Kurnool district, were famous for producing diamonds. A French doctor
by the name of Tavena states that, in possessing quality diamonds, the
Qutb Shahis had neither rivals nor equals in the world.

31


--- PAGE 042 ---
The fine quality of muslin cloth, kalamkaris, and diamonds that were
available like air and water in the Golconda kingdom became a temptation
for the European merchants. Thus, the Portuguese, the Dutch, the English,
and finally the French established their trading centres in the Golconda
kingdom. The Qutb Shahi kings also permitted the European companies
to establish their trading centres in their kingdom. Thus, the Qutb Shahis
contributed their might to the development of trade and commerce. The
artisans and handicraft industries of Andhra witnessed hectic commercial
activity during this period.

Religious Policy:

The Qutb Shahis followed a policy of religious toleration. They
knew pretty well that their strength lay in the willing cooperation of the
natives, who were mostly Hindus. They gave Hindus a position of
prominence in the administration and did not restrict their freedom of
worship. Abul Hassn gave munificent grants for the maintenance of the
Rama temple at Bhadrachalam. The Qutb Shahis even gave agraharas
to the Brahmanas. This practise was followed by their subordinates. It is
interesting to note that in some of the Muslim buildings, like the Mazzids,
Hindu decorative motifs like the purnakumbha are found very often.
The Qutb Shahi rulers gave importance to Hindu customs and traditions.
The Sultan Muhammad Quli composed many verses exposing the
similarities that are noticed at the time of marriages performed by Muslims
and Hindus. They also gave importance to some of the superstitious
beliefs entertained by the Hindus. For example, the Qutb Shahi Sultans
believed in astrology, the eclipses of the Sun and Moon, auspicious times,
and the impact of planets on the lives of human beings. Sati was a social
evil that prevailed in the Golconda kingdom. There are instances to prove
that sati was not a voluntary act but one committed by force. The Qutb
Shahis tried to stop this social evil. But they never made it law on account
of the fear of revolt by the Hindus, who gave a lot of importance to that
custom. There are some instances where a woman committed sati when
even the local subedar refused permission for her to do so.

1.7: Literature and Architecture: Literary activities:

The Qutb Shahis were proficient in the Persian language.
Muhammad Quli was an expert in Persian. But the Urdu language brought
a lot of credit to the Qutb Shahis. It is an admixture of Persian, Sanskrit,
and Marathi words and expressions. The Deccan became the cradle of

the Urdu language. Hence, it was called Deccan Urdu. As a protest
against the north Indian Persian language and to maintain its linguistic
identity, the Deccan Sultans developed Urdu poets. During the time of
Abdullah, Urdu reached new heights. Further, he was himself a great
poet in that language. The Sufi saints also helped spread Urdu in the
Golconda kingdom. They spread their gospel in easily understandable
Urdu. Urdu more or less became the language of the masses.

Ibrahim, it may be remembered, spent seven years in the court
of the Vijayanagara kings. It gave a fertile opportunity for him to come
into close contact with the men of letters and also witness personally the
patronage extended by the Vijayanagara kings for the promotion of
learning and education. The impact of these factors was that Ibrahim not
only developed proficiency in Telugu literature but also praised and
patronised many Telugu poets. Ponnaganti Telaganarya, the author of
the Yayaticharitra, and Addanki Gangadhara Kavi, the author of Zapathi
Samharnopakhyanamu, were patronised by him. ‘Malkabhiram,”
referred to by the Telugu poets, was none other than Ibrahim Qutb Shah.

The Qutb Shahis also patronised Telugu, Urdu, Sanskrit, and
Persian. It is very interesting to note here that Muslims during this time
acquired mastery over Telugu and Sanskrit. Shay Akbar was one of
them. He translated Sringaramanjari, written by Rangasai, into Sanskrit.
Calligraphy had assumed a special significance under the Deccan rulers.
The buildings at Bijapur and Golconda still stand as a testimony to it.

Calligraphy was used by the calligraphers, who were mostly Persians.
For example, the inscription on the detached gateway of Jami Masjid,
according to Dr. Yazdani, presents perhaps the finest example of the
‘Nastaliq’ in Deccan, while on the same mosque; the Quran verses around
the prayer niche are a good specimen of the ornamental ‘Naskh’. Hussein
Sagar was constructed by Hussein Shah Wali, a famous Sufi saint and
son-in-law of Ibrahim Quli Qutb Shah; in 1562 C.E. Muhammad Quli
was a great builder. He was responsible for the planning and building of
the modern city of Hyderabad. This was originally named Bhagyanagar
after the name of the Sultans’ dancer-concubine, Baghmati. The most
important buildings of the city, viz., Charminar, Jami Masjid,
Chandanamahal, and countless monuments that are scattered throughout
the environs of the city of Hyderabad, were the works of this great sultan.
He constructed a dam across the Musi River and thereby provided a
water supply to the inmates of the city. He raised beautiful gardens and
fruit-bearing trees around the city of Hyderabad. He was thus not only
responsible for the construction of the city of Hyderabad but also for its
beautification. The Qutb Shahi tombs of Sultans were very popular for
their architectural beauty and were located in Banjara Darwaza near
Golconda.

The patronage extended to men in letters recetved a check. The
English East India Company got the required freedom to extend its
commercial activities. As long as Abul Hasan and his Prime Minister
Akkanna were at the helm of affairs, the company was kept in check.
After 1687 C.E., the activities of the company increased, and their
ambition grew by leaps and bounds. The administration was paralysed.
The Mughul viceroys, particularly after the death of Aurangzeb, became
selfish, power-hungry, and literally neglected maintaining law and order.
The later Mughal emperors were not powerful enough to control them.
Thus, one of the Mughal subedars by the name of Nizam-ul-Mulk defied
the authority of the Mughals and established the independent state of
Hyderabad in 1724 A.D. He was the founder of the Asaf Jah dynasty.
1.8: Advent of Europeans and Settlements in Andhra Pradesh

India’s trade links with Europe can be traced back to the ancient
days of the Greeks. This trade was carried out along several routes. One
was by sea along the Persian Gulf, and from there overland through Iraq
and Turkey, and then again by sea to Venice and Genoa. The second

--- PAGE 048 ---
one was via the Red Sea and then overland to Alexandria, Egypt, and
then by sea to Venice and Genoa. The third, less frequented, overland
route lay through the passes of the north-west frontier of India, across
Central Asia and Russia, and to the Baltic.

The most important feature of the overseas and overland trade
was that the Asian part of the trade was carried on mostly by Arab
merchants and sailors, while the European trade was monopolised by
the Italians. The occupation of Constantinople by the Ottoman Turks in
1453 C.E. brought certain startling changes. The old trading routes
between the East and the West came under Turkish control. However,
the merchants of Venice and Genoa monopolised the trade between
Europe and Asia and refused to let the new nation states of western
Europe, particularly Spain and Portugal, have any share in the trade
through the old routes. The West Europeans were determined to maintain
trade links with the East. The demands for spices were pressing, and the
profits to be made in their trade were inviting. The fabulous wealth of
India was an additional attraction. Thus, the West European states and
merchants began to search for a new and safer sea route to India and the
Spice Islands (Indonesia). In this connection, the Europeans wanted to
break the Arab and Venetian trade monopolies, bypass the Turkish
hostility, and open direct trade relations with the East.

The initiative was taken by Portugal and Spain in their geographical
exploration and discoveries. In 1494 C.E., Columbus of Spain set out to
reach India but discovered America instead. In 1498 C.E., Vasco da
Gama of Portugal discovered a new, all-sea route from Europe to India.
This is called the Cape route. These two discoveries revolutionized the
history of the world. Adam Smith wrote later that the discovery of America
and the Cape route to India were “the two greatest and most important
events recorded in the history of mankind”.

The Portuguese first settled in Calicut. Zamorin, the ruler of
Calicut, extended the required support to the Portuguese. Within a very
short time, the Portuguese established their trading centres at Cochin,
Goa, Diu, Daman, etc. In this connection, they were helped by the
superiority of their armed ships. Besides, they could take advantage of
the mutual rivalries of the Indian princes to strengthen their position.
Slowly, they gained control over the Malabar Coast. Then they destroyed
the trade monopoly enjoyed by the Arabs on the Malabar Coast. They

--- PAGE 050 ---
also succeeded in seducing many trade concessions from the Mughal
emperors. Francisco de Almeida was the first governor of the Portuguese
possessions in India. He believed that the Portuguese should maintain
supremacy at sea and concentrate their attention more on trade and
commerce. They should not think of establishing a territorial empire in
India. This policy enunciated by Almeida is called the “blue water policy’.
But this policy was reversed by Alfonsco de Albuquerque, the successor
of Almeida. It was during his time that Goa was conquered by the sultan
of Bijapur. Further, the Portuguese established their dominance over the
entire Arabian coast, from Ormuz in the Persian Gulf to Malacca in Malaya
and the Spice Islands in Indonesia. But very soon they had lost not only
their trade monopoly but also disappeared from Asia. The following are
some of the factors that were responsible for the failure of the Portuguese
in India:

1. The Portuguese, from the beginning, combined the use of force with
trade.

2. The Portuguese, with the fond hope of creating a territorial empire in
India, neglected trade. Besides, they intervened in the internal politics
of the native states. This ultimately weakened their economic
strength.

3. Philip Il, the king of Spain, conquered Portugal and annexed it into
Spain. He was interested in Brazil but not in India.

4. The Portuguese were intolerant and fanatical in religious matters.
They indulged in forceful conversions, offering people the alternative
of sword and Christianity. Their approach in this respect was
particularly hateful to the people of India, where religious tolerance
was the rule. They also indulged in inhuman cruelties and lawlessness.
All these acts alienated the people.

5. The last straw in the air was the advent of the Dutch as rivals and
competitors to the Portuguese in the Eastern trade.

Arrival of the Dutch :

In 1594 C.E., Philip I, the ruler of Spain and Portugal, issued
an edict preventing the entry of Dutch ships to Lisbon and other ports of
his empire. This action of Philip II denied the Dutch access to spices, so
they decided to seek out the eastern products for themselves. By that
time, the Dutch were in a position to venture into the eastern trade. A
Dutch traveler named Van Linschoten spent nine years in Southeast Asia

and returned home in 1592 C.E. The northern Circars region of the
Andhra coast was referred to as the Gingelly coast by the Dutch. It has
been pointed out that “the Dutch, who replaced the Portuguese, whose
trade was very irregular in Andhra, at first entered into the hinterland of
Andhra and established factories at the weaving centers. The hinterland
of Andhra was converted into a great commercial zone, which helped
them continue for a long time until the end of the eighteenth century. The
Dutch East India Company (VOC) had established seven trading centers
on the Andhra coast and entered into trade agreements with local rulers,
i.e., Qutb Shahis, in the first decade of the 17th century. Their factories
were quite prosperous since they were involved in the trade of interior
coastal Andhra. The Dutch wanted to monopolise the spice trade, so
they concentrated their attention on the East Indies rather than India. In
India, it established factories at Surat, Machilipatnam, and Petaploli. The
other Dutch settlements were at Nagapattanam, Narasapuram,
Bheemunipatnam, Chinsurah, etc.

1.9: Occupation of Northern Circulars and Ceded Districts:

The rivalry between Anglo and French companies led to three
wars called the Anglo-Carnatic wars. The Anglo-French war in Europe,
which was known as the War of Austrian Succession, was the precipitating
cause of the First Anglo-Carnatic War in India.

Anglo-Carnatic War I (1744-1746 C.E.)

The Carnatic was the southeast coastal region where Madras
was located and was bounded by the Mysore Kingdom on the west. It
was ruled by a Nawab who had come from Hyderabad State, but it was
almost independent. Arcot was its capital. Dost Ali, the Nawab of Arcot,
died in a war with the Marathas, and his son-in-law Chandasaheb was
captured. Safdar Ali, who was the son of Dost Ali, became the Nawab
of the Carnatic. However, the Nizam intended to claim superiority over
the Carnatic. However, the Nizam, who intended to claim superiority
over the Carnatic, dethroned Safdar Ali and installed his follower
Anwaruddin on the throne. The British attacked the French fleet at
Pondicherry. Dupleix, who was then the French Governor, occupied
Madras, where the British had built Fort St. George. The British took
the help of Anwaruddin, the Nawab of Arcot, and fought against the
French. However, they were defeated in the battle at Santhome in 1746
C.E. The battle of Santhome was significant because it was the first time

that the European war between France and Britain came to an end. So,
as part of a peace treaty, Madras was returned to the British by the
French.

Anglo-Carnatic War II (1748-1756 C.E.):

In this war, both the French and the British involved the local
rulers to maintain their supremacy. Chanda Saheb, who was the son-in-
law of the previous Nawab of Arcot, i.e., Dost Ali, was released. At this
time, the first Nizam of Hyderabad, Asaf Jah I, died in 1748 C.E. So,
Nasir Jung, son of Asaf Jah, and Muzaffar Jung, grandson of Asaf Jah,
were the competitors for the throne of Hyderabad. The British made
Nasir Jung the Nizam. The French were then on friendly terms with
Chanda Saheb and Muzaffar Jang. Together, they killed Anwaruddin,
the Nawab of Arcot, and then made Chanda Saheb the Nawab of Arcot.
The French then killed Nasir Jung as well and made Muzaffar Jung the
Nizam of Hyderabad. Muzaffar Jung then gave the French some territories,
including Machilipatnam, and a large amount of money. The French stayed
in Hyderabad to protect Muzaffar Jang. However, Muzaffar Jang was
killed by his own people in 1751 C.E. by Marquis de Bussy; the French
Governor installed Salabat Jang, brother of Nasir Jung, on the throne.
The prestige of the French in India increased to a great extent because of
these actions. Not able to digest the growing popularity of the French,
the British East India Company wanted to put an end to it. So, they
attacked Arcot, killed Chanda Saheb, and made Mohammad Ali, son of
Anwaruddin, the Nawab of Carnatic. Robert Clive, who fought a heroic
battle at Arcot, was called ‘The Hero of Arcot’. The defeat at Arcot was
the beginning of the downfall of the French in India.

Anglo-Carnatic War III (1756-1763 C.E.):

In 1756 C.E., the “Seven Years War’ in Europe between the
French and the British had its impact on India. The French government
sent Count de Lally to India as the military general. He first captured the
area between Pondicherry and Madras. Later, he determined to attack
Madras as well. So he asked Bussy to come from Hyderabad to assist
him in Madras. Though Bussy knew that his departure from Hyderabad
would help the British, he was ordered by Lally to leave Hyderabad
would help the British, he was ordered by Lally to leave Hyderabad. So,
having no option, Bussy left Hyderabad for Madras. Taking advantage
of this, Robert Clive, who was the Major-General of the British Army,

bought Salabat Jung, the Nizam of Hyderabad, under his control. Salabat
Jang then gave all the northern coastal districts of Andhra Pradesh up to
the Krishna District to the British. Lally’s attempt to capture Madras
ended in failure. The British army defeated the French in the battle at
Wand Wash in 1760 C.E. Pondicherry was occupied by the British.
Lally and Bussy surrendered to the British. Thus, French power in India
diminished considerably. They stayed in India until 1815 C.E. Count de
Lally was kept as a prisoner in England for two years. He was then sent
back to France, where he was executed for losing to the British. However,
he was honoured posthumously by the French.

All the lands in northern Caims were divided into two categories:
Zamindari and Haveli, or government. The land assessment was 1/Sth of
the produce. The Zamindars were notorious for their oppression of the
peasantry. They also collected land customs on all articles of commerce
like food, cattle, salt, etc. These taxes, known as “sunkams,” were
collected at short intervals. They hampered the growth of industry and
commerce. Besides, there were other imposters under various names. A
loom tax, or moturpha, was also levied. The Circar region produced rice
in abundance. Its forests produced a fine variety of teak, which was
used in shipbuilding. The shipbuilding industry at Corinka was able to
withstand the competition from outside. The fishing industry was also
highly developed. The inland commerce and maritime exports of the Circar
region were to the tune of 75 lakhs a year.

1.10: Summary:

In conclusion, we may state that the rule of the Qutb Shahis of
Golconda may be considered a memorable epoch in the history of Andhra
Pradesh. After 1687 C.E., the Deccan in general and that of Andhra
Pradesh in particular came under the control of the Mughals. The Mughals
ruled the Deccan and Andhra through their viceroys. The end of Qutb
Shahi’s rule had yielded certain important results. The Qutb Shahi dynasty
has been considered a “composite culture” of Hindu-Muslim socio-
religious and cultural conditions during the sixteenth and seventeenth
centuries. The Qutb Shahi rulers were particularly liberal in patronising
the Telugu poets. His court represented a true picture of what was
reserved for matters of state and administration alone, but it was extended
to religious institutions as well. Thus, the socio-cultural life of the people
during the rule of the Qutb Shahis was marked by a spirit of broad-

period of the Qutb Shahis, borrowed heavily from the Hindu style of
architecture. Hindu symbols like the pot, lotus, and elephant trunk were
used in purely religious buildings like mosques.

2.2. Andhra Region During British Rule

The Andhra region of India was under the control of the Madras
Presidency during the British colonial era. The British gained control of
the region in the mid-1 8th century after defeating the Nawab of Arcot.
They introduced various administrative and legal systems that brought
about significant transformations, including the establishment of English
education, railways, and telegraph systems. This period marked the
beginning of modern history in the Andhra region, and it brought about
fundamental changes in the socio-economic and political fields, paving
the way for modernization. However, the introduction of these changes
also led to social and economic upheavals and resistance from the people
of the Andhra region. Despite this, the British also introduced western
ideology, culture, and education, which provided opportunities for new
cultural development and social reforms. The colonial period in the Andhra
region had a profound impact on the region’s development and set the
stage for its integration into modern India.

Overall, the British period in Andhra was characterised by
economic growth and political upheaval as the region struggled to balance
the benefits of British development with the social and economic costs of
colonial rule. Due to the combination of systematic management errors
and natural calamities, consecutive droughts occurred in this age of peace.
The cottage industries of the Andhra region, which were affected by the
industrial revolution, weakened and lost their influence. Some officers of
the East India Company were not only efficient in their duties but also
committed to the development of the region. Fortunately, civil society
has emerged, and the government has started to respond to the challenges
faced by the region.

After the fall of the Vijayanagara Empire, due to a lack of political
stability, local landlords in the Rayalaseema region, as poligars and as
zamindars in Sarkar areas, became strengthened, independent, and
managed their own armies. They did not like the control and discipline
policies of the East India Company. A clash had occurred to express real

--- PAGE 070 ---
power between the East India Company and the local lords, which created
a conflicting environment.

After the East India Company had conquered the areas of Uttara
Sarkar (Northern) and Rayalaseema, they faced many obstacles when
trying to validate their authority in those areas. Sometimes the protests of
local landlords escalated into revolts. The tribal revolts were called
“‘Pithuri’. There were several factors that led to the revolts in the Andhra
region during the British colonial period. One of the primary reasons was
the exorbitant increase in land tax (pesh kush) imposed by the government
on the zamindars, which often led to financial strain and discontent among
the local rulers. Furthermore, the exact collection of the fixed ‘Peshkush’
and frequent interference in inheritance matters also caused resentment
and contributed to revolts. In addition to this, the imposition of restrictions
on the use of forest resources and the formulation of licensing policies on
the manufacture and consumption of alcohol became significant triggers
for tribal revolts. These policies interfered with the traditional way of life
of the tribes and impacted their livelihoods, leading to widespread
discontent and resistance. Overall, these factors contributed to the social
and economic upheavals in the Andhra region during the British colonial
era, and they became the driving force behind many of the revolts and
movements that sought to challenge colonial rule.

2.3. Administrative System

The British administration in Andhra was divided into two period’s
i.e (a) the East India Company administration and (b) the Queen Victoria
administration. The fact that the officers of the East India Company were
skilled in business matters but lacked experience in local conditions. The
British made persistent efforts to establish an orderly system of
administration in the areas under their control. This involved constant
changes and additions to the administration as part of their efforts to
achieve this goal. Before the East India Company acquired the power of
statehood in the northern sarkar districts, it built factories and
warehouses in Machilipatnam and Visakhapatnam and set up a Board of
Revenue to supervise them. This Board of Revenue was abolished in
1794. After the company’s authority was established in this area, the
areas under their control were divided into districts, and the district
collectors were entrusted with the administration of these districts. Since

--- PAGE 072 ---
1794, district collectors have been introduced into the system of
administration. The district collector, superintendent of police, and district
judge formed the three pillars of district administration under company
government. The post of collector is preferred as the main pillar of the
district administration. However, the main responsibility of collectors is
to collect land tax, to protect law and order, and to act as the Chief
Magistrate of the district. In 1816, the East India Company’s government
abolished judicial powers from collectors, and special officers were
appointed to administer justice.

The Sarkar areas came under British control in 1766. Chikakol
was part of Ganjam district. Non-tribals have resided in the plains, and
tribal people live in the hilly areas of this district. These hilly areas are
called agency areas. Administratively, these two areas are seen as separate.
According to the Act enacted in 1839, the Collector of Ganjam should
act as the agent of the Governor, exercise authority over the agency
areas, and manage administrative affairs. The government used to levy
taxes and get financial resources through three types of systems in the
region: Ryotwari, Zamindari, and Inamdari. Initially, the government areas
were divided into five districts. They are Ganjam, Visakhapatnam,
Godavari, Krishna, and Nellore. Similarly, all the areas of ceded districts
were organised into a single district, with Anantapur as the district
headquarters. In 1808, the ceded districts were re-divided into Bellary
and Kadapa districts. Then Kumool District (1858) and Anantapur District
(1882) were formed for some time. Chittoor district was formed in 1911.

Agency areas are also spread over Visakhapatnam and Godavari districts.
In 1766, Godavari district came under the control of the East India
Company. Rajahmundry and two collectorates in Kakinada under its
jurisdiction were merged into Rajahmundry district. Rajahmundry,
Machilipatnam, and Krishna districts were reorganised into Godavari
and Krishna districts when the rulership passed from the East India
Company to the Queen. Bhadrachalam Taluk from the Central Provinces
was removed and then added to Godavari district in 1874. Judiciary:
The District Judge was responsible for handling legal matters within the
district. Minor disputes were resolved in the Sadar Amin courts, which
were under the jurisdiction of the District Judge. Above the district courts
were the state-level courts, including the civil court known as the “Sadar

The practise of transferring scandalous officials who were deemed
incompetent in the revenue department to the judicial department was
generally frowned upon. However, corruption and inefficiency were
rampant in the judiciary system during this time.

2.4. Land Revenue Settlements

The East India Company identified the land system as a major
source of revenue for the administration of the government. Scientific
procedures have been devised for the effective collection of land tax. As
part of this, which area tax system was implemented in the area where it
gives more income, those tax systems were implemented. Companies
divided their land tax management systems into three methods. They are:
(a) permanent settlement (zamindari). (b) Ryotwari Settlement; (c) Mahal
Wari or Village-Wide Settlement Zamindari system in Uttara Sarkaru
districts; Ryotwari system in Datta mandals; and village-wise systems a
village-wise system has been introduced experimentally in Nellore district
as well. Lands in government areas were classified as Haveli (government)
Lands and Lands of Zamindars. During the period of Mughal emperors,
some land was given to farmers for agriculture by the rulers for their own
expenses. These are government lands. The rulers use the income from
them for their personal needs. In Zamindari lands, there were intermediaries
called zamindars. They collect taxes from land cultivators, take some
commission, and pay the rest to the government. There was a written
contract between the zamindars and the government. The written contract
is known as “Kaulw’’, in which there will be a fixed amount of tax.

a) Permanent Settlement:

In the beginning of the East India Company’s rule in Bengal, land
was collected and sold by auction for a period of five years. Those
who paid a fixed amount of money to the government and got the
right to collect tax were called Zamindars. These zamindars used
to collect as much tax from cultivators as possible. Due to this, the
condition of the farmers has become miserable. The company’s
government reduced the five-year term to one year. But the flaws in
this approach have not been eliminated.

In 1793, Governor General Cornwallis made this policy a permanent
settlement. With this, who had the right to collect tax become a

b)

permanent zamindari system. The main change brought about by
this system was that the zamindars, who were tax collectors, were
made the owners of the land. Moreover, it has become a right of
inheritance. By doing so, the company government can predict fiscal
collections in advance. Through this, the government gets to know
the revenue in advance and decides the expenditure accordingly. An
elite Indian class has been created that supports and depends on the
company’s government. This permanent zamindari system was
introduced in the states of Bengal, Bihar, and Orissa, as well as in
coastal Andhra districts extending from Ganjam to Nellore. The tax
paid to the government by the zamindars is known as pesh kush
(land tax). Because of this permanent zamindari system, the lives of
the cultivators depended on the benevolence of the landlords.

Ryotwari Settlement:

The Ryotwari system was devised as an alternative to this system,
as many defects of the permanent Zamindari system were exposed.
In this system, the government collects taxes directly from the farmer
who cultivates the land. That means there is no place for middlemen
in this system. This system was first introduced in the Bharamahal
area of Salem district in Tamil Nadu. While Captain Reid formulated
this system in principle, Thomas Munro further refined it and gained
long-lasting fame. As the government was dealing directly with the
farmer, this system was called the ““Ryotwari system.

In this system, the croplands in the village were carefully calculated,
and the yield was estimated. The names of the farmers and their
cultivating land boundaries were recorded. The farmer has rights
over the land as long as he pays the prescribed tax. A farmer can
pass his land on to his offspring or sell his land. In addition to the
increase in revenue for the government due to the absence of
middlemen in this system, the land tax paid by the farmer is paid
directly to the government, so to some extent the wastage was
decreased. By giving the farmer ownership rights over the land he
cultivates, it creates equity in the land, thereby improving agriculture.
Thomas Munro thought ownership of agricultural land would improve
the social status of the farmer. This peasant system became so
popular that Thomas Munro, when he was Collector of Ceded

--- PAGE 078 ---
Districts, implemented it in that region, then throughout the Madras
state, and then extended it to Bombay and the Indus states.

c) Mahalwari System:

This method is also known as the Gramvari (Village) Method. In this
system, the village is taken as a unit, the tax is decided on the
agricultural lands in the village, and the responsibility of collecting
this tax is placed on the villagers. Village heads that collect tax are
exempt from it. The system is mainly implemented in the northern
parts of India and Punjab. This type of village-wise tax collection
system was implemented in 1804 for 15 years in North Sarkar,
Nellore, and Rayalaseema districts. It is the policy to seize the lands
from the zamindars who do not pay pesh kush and introduce a village-
wise tax system. In this way, the total revenue of the village is
calculated. The village headman decides who and how much the
farmers in the village should pay. Village officials will collect tax using
this method, but it is not working properly. After some time, this
system was ended and the permanent method, or Ryotwari system,
was introduced.

2.5. Society :

The society in the Andhra region had stagnated and developed
negative traits due to a lack of proper administration over an extended
period. This led to the implementation of policies that discriminated against
lower castes and women proper administration over an extended period.
However, during the rule of the East India Company, changes began to
take place in the region. These changes caused significant upheavals in
Andhra society, including the introduction of a new education system by
the English government that created a more dynamic social environment
with new values. Agricultural castes such as Reddy, Vaishya, Kapu,
Kamma, and Velama began to compete with the Brahmin community for
employment, education, and representation in local institutions. Previously,
only Brahmins held most of the jobs, from the highest to the lowest, in
the administration of the company’s government. English education in
the Andhra region contributed to an increase in the literacy rate, leading
to the emergence of a new educated middle class. As a result, many
individuals pursued careers in fields such as law, teaching, and journalism.

--- PAGE 080 ---
The Andhra region’s stable governance system, law and order, and
increased literacy rates laid the foundation for a conscious civil society.
In 1812, the Madras Literary Society was formed in Madras, with seven
members hailing from the Telugu region of Machilipatnam. Since as early
as 1821, people have been submitting petitions to the Governor of Madras
to address their grievances. These petitions focused mainly on
government-related issues, highlighting flaws in governance and suggesting
changes necessary for societal development. Progressives such as Gazulu
Lakshminarasu Chetty established the Madras Native Association in 1852
and became pioneers of the region. In 1851, a public discussion forum
was established in Machilipatnam, while the Kakinada Literary Association
was formed in Kakinada town in 1877. Additionally, reading clubs,
libraries, and widely established magazines played a significant role in
shaping the social dynamics of the Andhra region.

2.5. (a) Communications, Roads, and Transport :

The British government decided to develop the communication
system, roads, bridges, and railways as part of the programme to
strengthen the country’s security. But due to the 1857 revolt in North
India, the British government focused more on that region and
neglected the transport facilities in South India. In the region of
Andhra, the first railway line was constructed between Puttur and
Renigunta, which was completed and started by September 1862.
Between 1864 and 1869, another railway line was built connecting
Gutthi to Renigunta. In the Madras Presidency, five percent of the
government’s revenue is earmarked for the development of land and
waterways. With this, the transport system was formed in the Andhra
region.

2.6. Education :

The traditional education system was in force in the Andhra region
before the administration of the East India Company. Only Brahmin boys
were given the opportunity for higher education. In this traditional system
of education, professional communities, which are formed on the basis
of caste, are trained by their elders for that profession. In this system,
lower-caste men and women were denied education. On the other hand,

the Mohammedans used to teach the students in madrasas Arabic based
on their belief in God.

In 1813, the East India Company took over the responsibilities
of the administration of India, realized that they should also take care of
the management of education in this country and allocated some money.
In 1822, when Thomas Munro was the Governor of Madras State, he
sought an official report from the District Collectors to find out the actual
information about the indigenous education system in Madras State.
Bellary Collector Camp Bell, while submitting his report, stated that the
education system in this area was damaged due to poverty and that there
are only seven students out of every 1000 who are studying; even these
students can read verses without mistakes but cannot interpret the verses.
When Thomas Munro was the Governor of Madras, he established 300
Tahsildari Schools and 40 Collectorate Schools in the Madras Presidency
and provided funds from the Court of Directors. However, the government
issued a report on these schools in 1839, declaring that they had failed to
achieve their intended goals.

The development of education in the Andhra region was mainly
due to Christian missionary schools, government schools, and schools
established with the generosity of donors. In the year 1806, the London
Missionary Society established a school in Visakhapatnam, followed by
another two years later by a school in Bellary district. In the same way,
this society started a school for girls in Visakhapatnam. In 1813, Robert
Noble started the Church Missionary Society School at Machilipatnam,
the Native School at Machilipatnam in 1845, the George Noble Tyler
School at Narsapuram, the Maharaja School at Vizianagaram in 1857,
the District School at Rajahmundry in 1853, the Middle School at
Kakinada in 1853, the PR College at Machilipatnam in 1856, the Hindu
Anglo- Vernacular School in 1842, the Lutheran Multipurpose (L.M.P.)
School, and the Pennsylvania Synod Society built vidyalayas in Guntur,
Pattipadu, and Nallapadu in the year 1841. Madras Collegiate Institution
was established in 1841. Christian missionaries established several
educational institutions, recetving government grants. These were mainly
aimed at promoting Christianity.

Higher education was started in 1857 in the Andhra region with
the establishment of Madras University, which started teaching in 1864.

--- PAGE 084 ---
During the Queen’s administration, Nobel College was started in Bandar
in 1864, Andhra Christian College in Guntur in 1866, and a junior college
was started in Rajahmundry in 1873, which then became Government
Arts College (Degree College) in 1877. Similarly, in 1878, Hindu College
was established in Visakhapatnam, and Kallikota College was established
in 1879. New colleges were established in Parlakimidi, Kakinada, and
Vijayanagara, respectively. The establishment of colleges in the region
brought exposure to new ideas and perspectives from Western culture
among the youth, leading to a critical examination of traditional customs
and beliefs. As a result, many young people became inspired by these
new views and emerged as leaders of social and political reform
movements.

The job opportunities created by the new education system have
displaced traditional education and professions. English education is very
popular because of the job opportunities. The people of Andhra Pradesh
have started recognizing education as an important tool for social mobility.

While only primary education institutions are established in villages, higher
education institutions are established in towns. The spread of education
was mainly among the upper and middle classes. Although there is not
much difference in the percentage of lower caste and upper caste students
at the primary education level, Brahmins were dominant in higher
education.

George Noble Taylor, who was the sub-collector of Rajahmundry
Division, experimentally collected donations from the locals in 1852 and
organised this rate school with them. The English schools (rate schools)
established in Narsapur, Palakollu, etc. were functioning effectively and
attracted the people of the surrounding villages. Due to the success of
this experiment, the people of the surrounding villages also tried to establish
this kind of educational institution. Godavari district rate schools are also
known as the tailor system of education. A great change has been seen in
the Godavari region through these rated schools in the field of school
education. People started donating themselves and became the factor
behind the new trend in school rates in the Godavari region. Non-Brahmin
castes like Reddy, Naidu, etc., agricultural castes, volunteered to help
this school. In this way, they expressed their aspiration that the boys and
girls of their caste should develop academically and socially.
