2.7. Religion :

During the reign of the second Venkatapati Raya, Jesuit Christian
missionaries were known to be present in his court. One such Christian
preacher, Robert de Nobili, preached Christianity in South India around
1626 under the name Tattva Bhodananda Swami. Protestant sects of
Christianity were established in Punganur in Chittoor district and
Firangipuram in Guntur district as centres. In 1719, a German Christian
missionary named Benjamin Schultze wrote a Telugu grammar, and in
1727, he translated the Christian Bible into the Telugu language. Christian
missionaries began their work in Andhra Pradesh in the 1800s, with the
London Christian Mission commencing its activities in Visakhapatnam in
1805. In 1822, the mission extended its operations to the Kadapa region.
The Church Missionary Society also started working in Machilipatnam
under the leadership of Rev. Noble. At the same time, the American
Mission in Guntur expanded its activities.

After the Charter Act of 1813 lifted the ban on the propagation
of Christianity in India, missionary activity intensified. The Lutheran
Mission became the main centre in Guntur, while the Canadian Baptist
Mission operated as the main centre in Ongole, and the Church Mission
Society worked as the main centre in Hyderabad. The American Baptist
Missionary Society initiated its operations in the district of Guntur and
later expanded to cover the entire Guntur region. The Free Church Mission
extended its activities to the district of Nellore. By the year 1840, the
North German Missionary Society had also extended its work along the
Godavari coastline, with Rajahmundry as its main hub. Christian
missionaries extended invitations to the lower castes, particularly the
untouchables, who were subject to discrimination within the Hindu religion,
and through conversion to Christianity, they aimed to uplift their social
standing. It is important to recognise and appreciate the role of Christian
missionary societies in promoting education in the Telugu regions. These
institutions provided opportunities for education to lower castes, which
were historically discriminated against in Hinduism. As a result of this
education, many individuals from these castes were able to secure jobs
and positions of respect in society.

2.8. Impact of the Industrial Revolution on the Economy:

In the second half of the 18th century, the Industrial Revolution
began in the textile industry in England. It gradually spread all over the
world in all sectors. A new civilization was born in the world with iron
and coal as its main sources. The “industrial revolution” is the term used
to describe the sequence of complete revolutionary changes in the
production and distribution of goods. The industrial revolution saw the
use of mechanical energy instead of human and animal labour in the
production of goods. India’s connection to the world market during British
colonial rule had an impact on the country’s industrial revolution. Raw
cotton exports from India were promoted to textile mills in England,
while machine-made textiles from England flooded Indian markets with
high quality and low prices, leading to a decline in demand for Indian
textile products. As a result, the Indian textile industry lost market share
and struggled to compete with the imported goods.

In addition to the traditional cottage industries, the paper and
glass industries in the Andhra region also suffered due to foreign
competition, leading to their decline and eventual demise. This had a
ripple effect on the labourers who relied on these industries for their
livelihood, as they became unemployed and were forced to seek
employment as marginal labourers in the agricultural sector or migrate
abroad for work. On the other hand, manganese deposits in the Nellore
and Vijayanagara were exported abroad. The rising unemployment in
the region put pressure on the agricultural sector to provide employment,
leading to the division of land into small plots, which resulted in decreased
profits in agriculture.

Impact on Andhra Economy :

Although the industrial revolution had negative effects on the traditional
cottage industries in the Andhra region, it also brought about some positive
changes. These changes were mostly observed during the reign of the
Queen, after the East India Company’s rule, when there was significant
investment in infrastructure such as the construction of roads and railways
and the availability of investment capital was wide. As a result, the region
underwent modernization, and some industrialization began to take place.
Modern industries like jute, textiles, cement, and sugar were established
in the Andhra region.

--- PAGE 090 ---
The introduction of machine-made textiles in England led to a
decline in demand for Indian handloom products, as the machine-made
cloths were available at lower prices. This had a devastating impact on
the Indian textile industry, as can be seen in the decline in exports from
ports like Machilipatnam and Visakhapatnam. For instance, the average
annual exports from Machilipatnam in the 1820s were around ten lakh
rupees, but by 1843-1844, they had fallen to below three thousand.
Similarly, the annual export from Visakhapatnam, which was around seven
lakh, also fell drastically to just one lakh. The situation was made worse
by the imposition of taxes on weavers, such as “Mutoorpa”’ on looms,
which further weakened the already struggling industry. Many weavers
were forced to migrate to other countries like Burma, Mauritius, Sri Lanka,
etc., in search of employment as labourers.

The industrial revolution led to the introduction of machine-made
goods, which competed with the handicrafts in Andhra Pradesh, causing
workers in these industries to lose their jobs. Unfortunately, no other
industry emerged in the region that could accommodate these workers.

The European trading establishments in the Andhra region, which were
known as factories, had a significant role in the textile industry. For
instance, the Dutch factory in Machilipatnam employed around 5,000
weavers.

2.9.A. Agriculture :

The directors of the East India Company in London aimed to
generate more revenue from their newly acquired territories in the Andhra
Pradesh region. As a result, the company government increased the tax
burden on agriculture in the region. The coastal lands were mainly owned
by zamindars, and the company collected taxes from them under the
name Pesh Kush. The high rates of pesh kush became a significant burden
for the zamindars, who were accustomed to a luxurious lifestyle.
Consequently, these zamindars faced financial losses in districts such as
Godavari, Krishna, and Ganjam.

The British government implemented the Ryotwari system in the
Rayalaseema region, which involved collecting taxes directly from farmers.
However, this system led to problems for farmers as tax rates were set
too high. In addition, starting in 1834, farmers were required to pay their

taxes in cash, which further exacerbated their difficulties. It was only
after the British came under the rule of the Queen that land survey records
of the area became fully available and the tax collection process became
more transparent. As a result, tax collection increased. Despite agriculture
being the primary occupation in the Andhra region, the British government
did not prioritise irrigation management. In the early days of British rule,
little attention was given to the maintenance of agricultural ponds. It wasn’t
until 1819 that a separate department for irrigation was established in
Madras State with the appointment ofa superintendent engineer for pond
irrigation and repairs. From 1855 on, the Public Works Department took
over the supervision of these tasks. In 1844, Henry Mount Gomery was
appointed to investigate agricultural issues in the Andhra region, and his
report highlighted the negative impact of both heavy rainfall and drought
on the agricultural sector. In response to Gomery’s report, the British
officials asked Sir Arthur Cotton to assess the benefits of constructing
dams on the Godavari and Krishna rivers for the welfare of the people.
In response, Cotton sent the first report in 1844 and the second report in
1845 to the government. He argued that the cost of building dams could
be recouped in two years and that millions of deaths due to drought
could be avoided by diverting waste water from the sea to agricultural
land. As a result of Sir Arthur Cotton’s continuous efforts, the dams
were built on Krishna and Godavari.

The construction of the Dhavaleswaram project on the Godavari
River began in 1847 and was completed in 1852. The project comprises
three primary irrigation systems, namely the Eastern Delta Canal, the
Central Delta Canal, and the Western Delta Canal. Sir Arthur Cotton
constructed cotton barrage to control the floods. The construction ofa
dam on the Krishna River at Bejawada began in 1852 and was completed
in 1855, resulting in increased prosperity in the area. Captain C.A. Orr
led the work on the dam, there are two main canals: the East Delta Canal
and the West Delta Canal. In recognition of Sir Arthur Cotton’s efforts,
the British government awarded him the title of “Sir” in 1861. Similarly,
Indian employee Veenam Veeran, who worked with him in the
construction of Cotton Dhawaleswaram Bridge, was specially praised
and recommended to the government and promoted as Assistant Engineer,
which also gave him the title of Rao Bahudoor. Cotton was glorified as

2.9. B. Industries:

The textile industry provides employment to the second-largest
number of people after agriculture. Historically, it was a thriving
cottage industry in this region, with women predominantly responsible
for weaving and animal rearing. Entire families of weavers were
involved in textile production, yet their economic conditions have
not improved significantly. The East India Company favoured muslin
cloths from Srikakulam, glitter-dyed cloths from Machilipatnam, and
romals from Nellore for export to Europe, which had a steady demand
for these products. Silk cloths were produced in regions such as
Peddapuram, Dharmavaram, and Berhampur. The popularity of
certain types of Indian silk in Europe was largely due to the efforts
of the Portuguese, Greeks, and East India Company. However, the
emergence of Chinese and Japanese silks in the European markets
led to fierce competition, and ultimately, these foreign silks gained
dominance over Indian silk.

Generally, the silk industry was concentrated in urban areas, and
weavers in the Andhra region were primarily from castes such as
Padmashalas, Devangas, Satanis, Malas, and Muslims. Eluru carpets
and Kurnool colourful blankets have gained recognition in
international markets. The carpet industry in this region was
developed by Persian immigrants who settled here. The wool from
locally bred sheep in the Krishna Godavari districts is used to produce
these carpets and rugs. While this industry has also developed in
areas like Adoni and Bellary, it primarily serves local needs. Over
time, this industry has lost its former glory due to declining popularity
among consumers. On the other hand, the shipbuilding industry
flourished in the coastal towns from Ganjam to Nellore. It is interesting
to note that the ships built at Korinka were considered to be of high
quality. Many foreign ships anchor at Korinka for servicing and
repairs.

2.10. Famines :

Colonial Andhra experienced frequent droughts and famines
during British rule. In 1791 AD, the northern district of Madras Presidency
was hit by drought conditions due to insufficient rainfall. This led to a
shortage of food, causing numerous human and animal deaths. Law and

order deteriorated, and looting became rampant. It took a considerable
amount of time for the affected areas to return to normal. Another severe
drought hit the entire Andhra Pradesh region between 1805 and 1807,
resulting in the devastation of several villages. In 1811, Nellore district
was also hit by a severe drought. During 1823-24, a famine struck the
Machilipatnam region. Local officials of the East India Company had
imported grains from Bengal as a precautionary measure, but the quantity
was insufficient, resulting in many deaths. The ceded districts, where
rainfall is scarce, are prone to drought conditions. In 1805, 3,225 people
died due to famine, followed by 4,902 in 1806, and a staggering 17,207
in 1807. The year 1833 witnessed the most severe drought in Guntur
district, which greatly impacted the lives of the people. This drought is
also referred to as the “Dokkala’” famine. A significant number of livestock
perished, and diseases spread, affecting around 50 lakh people in the
region. It is estimated that around two lakh people lost their lives. In
1839, the region was hit by severe monsoon conditions, leading to crop
damage.

The Andhra region continued to suffer from severe droughts
throughout the 19th century. The most devastating famine, known as the
Ganjam Famine, occurred between 1865 and 1866. During the American
Civil War, the demand for cotton increased, leading to high prices and
prompting farmers in the Ganjam region to switch to cotton cultivation.
After the American Civil War, the supply of cotton increased, and as a
result, prices fell. This caused significant damage to the economic
conditions of farmers in this area. Moreover, the lands that were previously
used for rice crops were now allocated to cotton cultivation, resulting in
a shortage of food grains and leading to thousands of deaths due to
hunger. Similarly, many people migrated to distant countries like South
Africa and Mauritius as labourers to escape the dire circumstances. The
lack of proper irrigation facilities in Rayalaseema has made it a permanent
drought-prone area where agriculture is dependent on rain fed land. The
region has suffered from frequent droughts, and in the 1890s, many people
in Kadapa and Kurnool districts died due to a lack of food and while
migrating. As a result of these recurring droughts, the population in
Rayalaseema, Guntur, and Ganjam areas decreased significantly.

2.11 Contribution of Sir Thomas Munro:
Early life:

Sir Thomas Monro was born on May 27, 1761, in Glasgow,
England. Their father was Alexander Munro. In student life, Thomas
Munro was known as a talented sportsman. Arriving in India in 1779,
Monro joined the East India Company’s army in the Madras Infantry.
Served as an army officer and participated in the Mysore wars. Thomas
Monro, who was active in the Mysore wars, was selected as one of the
four generals to administer the territories captured from Tipu Sultan.
Commissioned as a Brigadier General, he achieved great success with a
small army. He had military skills. He held the position of revenue officer
for the Baramahal area in Salem district. He was appointed as an assistant
to Captain Reed. Under the supervision of Captain Reed, who was known
for his competence in administration, Munro gained valuable experience
in governance, eventually becoming a skilled administrator and an expert
in land tax management.

In 1793, Captain Reed presented the Fifth Revenue Report to
the Governor General, Lord Caron Wallis.

Thamas Munro

sir-thomas-munro-governor-of. html

much the farmer can pay but on the basis of how much is to be spent on
the government’s needs, he expressed his opinion that the country can
develop only if the surplus money is with the farmer rather than with the
government treasury. He acted as collector for the ceded (Datta Mandals)
districts granted to the East India Company from the Najam of Hyderabad
under the subsidiary alliance, and from 1800 to 1807 he oversaw the
administration of the Rayalaseema region. In these areas, the Ryotwari
system was introduced for land tax collection and improved tax
management. In 1800, when Munro took over the administration of ceded
districts, those areas were in a state of complete anarchy due to a lack of
law and order. Over 80 local armed leaders known as Poligars,” with
over 30,000 armed followers, pose a threat to law and order in the
region. He convinced the government leaders in Madras that the
administration of government is impossible without the suppression of
the Poligars in that region. He brought in an army under the leadership of
General Camp Bill and successfully suppressed the Poligars, restoring
peace and security to the region. He restored the administration in that
region.

Revenue Settlement :

In 1802, Munro initiated a comprehensive survey of cultivated
land in the ceded districts, which was completed by 1807. During this
survey, each cultivator was given a unique identification number, and the
area of their cultivated land was recorded along with their name. The
land survey system was designed based on factors such as soil fertility
and the availability of irrigation facilities. These survey records are still
considered authentic documents, even after more than 200 years since
the survey was conducted. Before the introduction of Munro’s Ryotwari
system, a village-wise system was in place where all cultivators ofa
village were collectively responsible for assessing and paying taxes. As
this village’s policy was not working properly, a replacement policy was
implemented. Munro had first introduced the Ryotwari system in the
BaraMahal area of Salem district between 1792 and 1799. In this system,
the government collects taxes directly from the cultivator of the land without
any middlemen. Munro felt that if there were 40 or 50 small landowners
instead of two or three large cultivators, the agricultural sector would
develop.

Munro provided loans for various agricultural purposes, such as
purchasing seeds, bullocks, and ploughs, building and repairing wells,
and improving irrigation canals. He took initiative and carried out the
repairs of ponds and irrigation canals without waiting for official orders
from the government. His proactive approach laid the foundation for the
prosperity of the region. In 1807, during his journey to England, Munro
submitted a report to the government known as the Munro Report. In
this report, he recommended reducing survey rates by 1/4th, advocating
for government management of irrigation ponds, granting disciplinary
exemptions when the yield decreased, and highlighting that land rights
should remain with the farmer to encourage their development of the
land. The Munro Report reflects his deep admiration for farmers and
their crucial role in the region’s prosperity.

In 1814, the Court of Directors of the East India Company
dispatched a Judicial Commission, led by Thomas Munro, to India to
examine the police and judicial systems in Madras and Bengal and to
offer recommendations for enhancing their efficiency. The commission
submitted its report in April 1816, suggesting that the district police be
brought under the supervision of the collector instead of the judge and
that a village-level officer be authorised to handle minor theft cases. In
1820, Thomas Munro was appointed Governor of Madras by the officials
of the East India Company in recognition of his distinguished services.
Munro had laid the foundations of an efficient system of administration in
the region, but unfortunately, he passed away on his retirement day on
July 6, 1827, due to a cholera infection while he was in Pattikonda among
his favourite people of the ceded districts.

Despite being a British administrator, Thomas Munro hada deep
admiration and affection for India and its people. He is remembered in
the history of Andhra Pradesh as a beloved and charismatic administrator.
Despite being a Christian, Munro showed great respect for Hindu religious
traditions and provided significant financial support to many temples. He
oversaw the renovation and restoration of several temples in the Datta
mandals, repaired the Agusteshwara and Chennakeshwara temples in
Kadapa district, and even reconstructed the Rameshwara temple in
Jammalamadugu. Munro also gifted a large vessel known as Gangalam
to the Tirumala Devasthanam. Ina speech given in the British Parliament,
Munro spoke highly of the hardworking and intelligent nature of the Indian

people. Munro had a particular fondness for rural farmers and was fluent
in Telugu, allowing him to connect with them. He formed a special bond
with the Telugu people in the Rayalaseema region and was greatly admired
in the ceded districts, where he was respectfully referred to as Mandava
Rishi. The farmers even named their sons after him, such as Mandolappa,
as a sign of their affection. Thomas Munro is remembered in the modern
history of the Andhra Pradesh region as a friend and benefactor of the
Telugu people.

2.12. Contribution of C. P. Brown:
Early life:

Charles Phillips Brown was born on November 10, 1798, in the
city of Calcutta. His father, David Brown, was the administrator of an
orphanage and an expert in several Indian languages. After his father’s
death, he went to England for civil service. Brown was selected for the
Indian Civil Service, trained at Haileybury College, and appointed as an
Indian Civil Service officer in August 1817 as a deputy to John Hanbury,
Collector of Kadapa District in Madras State. Then, when Sir Thomas
Munro was working as the Governor of Madras, he directed Brown to
learn local languages and improve administrative skills. Taking these orders
as inspiration, he acquired a good knowledge of the Telugu language to
understand the problems of the local people. Brown not only became
proficient in Telugu but also developed a special admiration for it and
became a factor in the revival of Telugu.

Charles Phillips Brown, who was appointed as Deputy Collector
in Kadapa district and then held duties in Machilipatnam, Rajahmundry,
Guntur, etc., developed a special fondness for Kadapa and made Kadapa
the field of action for his Telugu language programme. A fter retiring in
1834, Brown went to London, and in 1834 he was appointed as a
member of the board of the Madras College as a Persian translator for
the East India Company. He returned to London in 1854 and worked as
a Telugu professor at London University for some time. C. P. Brown,
who grew fond of the Telugu language, has collected old Telugu poems
and made copies of them. For this great work, he paid the salaries of
many Telugu scholars from his own money and arranged accommodation
in his home in Kadapa.

--- PAGE 108 ---
C.P. Brown

Source. https://madrascourier.com/biography/charles-philip-brown-the-
englishman-who-revived-telugu-literature/

Charles Phillips Brown reformed the Telugu language and made
it modern Telugu. Brown is credited with creating the modern alphabet
that we use today by reforming the letters of the Telugu language. By
doing so, he was glorified as the protector of Telugu and remained alive
in the hearts of the Telugu-speaking people. C.P. Brown was an active
member of the Royal Asiatic Society. Charles Phillips Brown is known
for writing the first comprehensive and authoritative Telugu-English
dictionary called ““Brownyanigantu’’. This dictionary is widely recognised
as the most significant contribution to the Telugu language and is still
popular and available in libraries even today.

He prepared and published revised copies of many Talapatra
texts. He had given it to those who came forward for publication. He
published Telugu works like Vemana Padyalu, Andhra Mahabharata,
and many others at great expense. When Mr. Brown was the Kadapa
District Collector, he provided jobs to more than a hundred Telugu
scholars in a spacious building and carried out this Telugu bashodharana
as a great work. Although C.P. Brown was not our countryman yet, he
successfully completed the great task of the revival of the Telugu language.

2.13. Impact of the 1857 Revolt in the Andhra Region :

In 1757, the British gained sovereignty over the entire Indian
subcontinent through wars and negotiations within a period ofa hundred
years. The Revolt of 1857 was the Indian response to this British
occupation. While the colonial rulers tried to play down the importance
of this mutiny by calling it a sepoy mutiny, national historians hailed it as

98


--- PAGE 109 ---
the first freedom struggle. Indians serving in the army of the East India
Company were called sepoys. Along with them, the indigenous rulers,
who were dissatisfied with the government of the East India Company,
actively participated in this rebellion. They rebelled against the British
government and declared the Mughal emperor Bahadur Shah Jafar as
the Indian emperor. The British suppressed the rebellion and restored
the British government in India. The British believed that this revolt was
due to the mismanagement of India by the East India Company. The
East India Company was exempt from the administration of India. The
administration of India was entrusted to the British Queen. A Cabinet-
level minister in the British Parliament oversees the affairs of India’s
administration on behalf of the Queen. She has promised to run the
administration without discrimination. But in reality, these promises were
not fulfilled.

The 1857 revolt was mainly done in North and Central India.
The impact of this rebellion in South India was less. Only a few sporadic
Madura incidents occurred in the Andhra region. A few riots occurred in
towns like Kadapa, Machilipatnam, and Visakhapatnam, which have a
significant Muslim population. Sheikh Peer Saheb, a Muslim religious
leader, called for a religious war of Jihad against the British in the city of
Kadapa during prayers in the mosques. Muslims in some towns offered
prayers in mosques for the victory of the Mughal Emperor. On the
occasion of Moharram, Muslims held demonstrations in Visakhapatnam
to change the name of the city to Mohammed Patnam. Savara tribesmen
of Parlakimidi area of Ganjam district rebelled against the company rule
under the leadership of Dandusena. The company government captured
and executed the garrison and easily put down the rebellion. Another
tribal revolt was led by Korukonda Subbareddy. When Subbareddy
was detained and interrogated by the British government, he said
Nanasaheb had rebelled against the British and was achieving success.
He said in the court hearing that he rebelled because he thought that he
would get a suitable reward ifhe rebelled against the British. Korukonda
Subbareddy was hanged by the company government.

The educated people of different towns organised meetings and
competed to declare their support for the British. Public meetings were
organised in important cities like Visakhapatnam, Rajahmundry,

99


--- PAGE 110 ---
Vijayawada, Guntur, Nellore, and Chittoor. In this way, the people of
this region thanked the British for establishing peace and security in the
region and providing a stable government. In Hyderabad, there is a severe,
tense situation where Nizam is ruling. Five thousand men, led by Turabuz
Khan, attacked the British Residency in Secunderabad. The Residency’s
troops, under the command of Kurnool Davidson, met the attack and
successfully repulsed it. Najam Sultan’s Prime Minister, Salar Jung, gave
full cooperation to the British and maintained law and order. Due to this,
the rebellion of 1857 did not have much impact in the telugu regions.

3.2 . Introduction :

The last decades of the 19th century were the days when the

country was under the political servitude of the government on the one
hand and social evils on the other, and society was in darkness, waiting
for a beacon of hope, a guide. In the beginning of the nineteenth century,

--- PAGE 118 ---
Indian society suffered from fanaticism and superstition. Idolatry and
polytheism helped to strengthen the Brahmins position. Caste system,
Untouchability, Parda system, Sati, Child marriages, Social inequalities,
Devadasi System, Illiteracy, etc. exist in the society. Priests were controlled
the society. People practiced idolatry, animal sacrifice, and physical
punishments. Socialising was also there in society. Female infanticide
and social boycotts of widows were all frequent practices. Polygamy
and Kulinism were common in society. The rigid caste system has
produced societal divides, making it impossible to maintain a united mass
movement. These conditions were also pervasive in Andhra society during
the Nineteenth century.

Social and religious reform movements arose among all sections
of the Indian people. Consequently, many individuals and movements
sought to bring about changes in social and religious practices in order to
reform and revitalise society. These efforts are collectively called the
Indian Renaissance.” Enlightened Indians like Raja Ram Mohan Roy,
Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar, Radha Kanta Dev, Dayanand Saraswati,
and many others fought to face the challenges of the West and tried to
bring reforms in the society. The Renaissance was first started in ‘Italy’
and spread in different countries. Bengal was the first state in India started
the Renaissance and East Godavari in Andhra was first Telugu speaking
region started social reform movement. Raja Ramohan Roy was the father
of Renaissance in India and Kandukuri Veresa Lingam was Father of
Andhra Renaissance Movement. It was precisely at that moment that
three luminaries illuminated the fields of moral education in the community
in Andhra, 1.e., Sri Kandukuri Veereshalingam Pantulu, Raghupathi
Venkataratnam Naidu, and Gurajada Appa Rao, who sanctified and
revived Andhra with their vision and consciousness. They laid the
foundations for all-round social reform, spiritual renewal, and family spirit
throughout their lives.

3.1 Causes for the Social Reform Movement :

“* There were many causes for the social and cultural awakening in the
19" century.

** The establishment of British rule and its deep influence on the political,
economic, social, and cultural life of the country created conditions
favourable to intellectual growth.

“* Bhrahma Samaj criticised social evils. The Andhra Brahma Samaj

Societies are established in Rajahmundry, Kakinada, Guntur, Bapatla,
Vijayawada, Narsaraopeta, Nellore, Chirala, Visakapatnam, and
Vijayanagaram. They encourage widow remarriages, women’s
education, and criticise social evils.

The rise of socio-political associations is established in Andhra
Pradesh, like Vignana Chandrika Mandali of Madras, Andhra
Bhashabivardini Samajam of Musulipatnam, Saraswati Gantha
Mandali of Rajamundry, Andhra Sahitya Parishad of Kakinada, etc.;
these associations have awakened the Andhra people.

There are several political organisations like the Madras Native
Associations (1852), the Madras Mahajana Sabha (1884), and the
Krishna District Association (1892) that also started a new social
and religious reform movement in Andhra.

The contributing factor was the efforts of the orient lists; they brought
India’s past. The great works of Sir William Jones, James Prinsep,
Charles Wilkin, Max Muller, and others were influenced by the
Indians. Indian scholars like Raja Ramohan Roy, Radhakanta Dev,
M.G. Ranade, R.G. Bhattacharya, etc. also contributed towards
discovering and reinterpreting India’s past.

The literary works of Andhra poets, for instance, enlightened the
people.

The growth of western education changed the mindset and attitude
of the people. There are several English schools and colleges
established in India; for instance, Kolkotta, Mumbai, and Madras
Universities were established in 1857. In 1843, an English school
and Noble College were established at Machilipatnam in Andhra
Pradesh, and in 1845, Andhra Christian College in Guntur was started.
Many other schools and colleges were established in several districts
of Andhra Pradesh. The Britishers like Sir Thomas Munro, C.P.
Brown, and Colonel Mackenzie encouraged western education in
Colonial Andhra. The educated middle- and upper-class people
raised awareness about social and religious evils. The Indians learned
critical thinking, problem solving, and how to generate new ideas.
These ideas of freedom, social and economic equality, fraternity,
democracy, and justice have had a tremendous impact on Indian
society.

The influence of modern Western culture and the sense of defeat at

--- PAGE 122 ---
the hands of a foreign power gave birth to a new awakening.

The rise and growth of journalism in Andhra Pradesh were also causes
for social awakening. The first Telugu journals like Satyadoota (1835)
and other journals like Yakshini, Hitavadi (1841), Crescent (1844),
Varthaman, Tarangini, Purushartha Pradayini (1872), Sasi Rekha
(1974), Desabi Mani (1886), Chintamani, Jayanthi, Udayani, Veena,
Jwala, Theist, Satya Samvardhini, Manovillasini, Amrutha Bhodini,
etc.

The Andhra Prakasika (1885), Andhra Patrika, Krishna Patrika,
and Telugu Janana also played a major role in enlightening the social,
political, and cultural conscience of the Andhra people.

In the last decades of the nineteenth century, the tides of nationalism
and democracy were expressed in movements to reform and
democratise the social institutions and religious outlook of the Indian
people.

The socio-religious upheavals of the nineteenth century provided
fertile ground for the formation of Indian nationalism. As a result of
the social and religious injustices faced by various sectors of the
population, many leaders and reformers emerged in Indian society.
Christian missionaries played a major role in propagating Christianity
and British trade. A church was constructed by the Portuguese at
Masilipatnam in 1670. They started English missionary schools and
established social service organisations. In the same way, the London
Missionary Society established missionaries at Jammalamadugu
(1805), Kadapa District, the American Baptist Organisation (1835),
and the Church Mission Society (1841), which established Christian
missionaries for education and social service. They uplift the poor
and inculcate discipline. In these schools, people like Raghupati
Venkataratnam Naidu, Konda Venkatappayya, and Mutnuri Krishna
Rao trained, and they denounced the worship, rituals, and beliefs of
the Hindus, the caste system, discrimination against women, etc. In
this context, Indian intellectuals felt the need for reforms in society.

Factors such as the rise of nationalistic sentiments, the emergence of
new economic powers, the spread of education, the influence of
modern Western ideas and culture, and the growing awareness of
the world strengthened the will to reform.

3.4 Social Reform Movement in Andhra :

The Nineteenth Century becomes a significant period for the social and
cultural history of the Indian subcontinent, particularly in Andhra. The
period witnessed a series of social movements that brought many changes
to society. The social reformers changed the mindset and attitude of the
people; they also changed conservative beliefs in Andhra Pradesh.
Kandukuri Veeresa Lingam Panthulu was one of the prominent social
reformers in nineteenth-century Andhra.

3.5. Kandukuri Veeresalingam:

Kandukuri Veeresalingam Pantulu (16 April 1848-27 May 1919)
was a social reformer, writer, first Telugu novelist, activist, translator,
Telugu teacher, and a multifaceted scholar. He was a pioneer of the new
Telugu literature, and he wrote the first autobiography in Telugu.
D.Anjaneyulu wrote a book on ‘Kandukuri Veeresalinganm’ in 1976;
Vakulabharanam Ramakrishna’s work “Social Reform in Andhra’,
(1983); and The Autobiography of Veeresalingam’ (1915) are the main
sources for the understanding of Kandukuri social reforms and social
reforms in Andhra Pradesh. Veeresalingam was the first to write a history
of Telugu poets, and he was the first Telugu writer to translate scientific
books. He was not only agitating for and promoting women’s education
but also started a school for girls and coeducational schools. He was
considered the ‘Father of the Renaissance Movement in Colonial
Andhra’. He was a pioneer in the social reform activities in Andhra
Pradesh; hence, he was considered the ‘Raja Ram Mohan Roy of Andhra
Pradesh.

He criticised the caste and untouchable systems. He used to
include children belonging to untouchable castes and make them sit
together with other children. Besides giving them free education, he used
to buy books for poor people. During the nineteenth century, the girls
were married at an early age in the society. Once the husbands die, the
married women become widows and face many hardships. In order to
change this, he not only propagated widow remarriage but also instituted
many difficult practices.

3.5.1 Early Life:

Veeresalingam Pantulu was born on April 16th, 1848, in
Rajahmundry to Punnamma (mother) and Subbarayadu (father). They
belonged to Niyogi’s middle-class Brahmin family. He was described as

an ‘Ekasanthagrahi’ (Quick on the uptake or a man of high I.Q). Their
ancestors migrated to Rajahmundry from Kandukuru village in present-
day Prakasam district, and hence this family name got established.
Veeresalingam’s father died when he was four years old. He grew up
under the care of his grandparents. He joined school at the age of five
and learned Balaramayanam, Andhra Nama Sangraham, Amaram,
Rukmini Kalyanam, Sumati Shatakam, and Krishna Shatakam, etc. At
the age of 12, he joined the Rajahmundry government school to study
English. From childhood, he was very active and got his first classes.
At the age of thirteen, he was married off to an eight-year-old girl named
‘Bapamma’ (Kandukuri Rajyalakshmamma). Growing up,
Veeresalingam worked hard to eradicate such evils. During his school
days, he was influenced by reading a book written by Keshab Chandra
Sen. He not only lost faith in idol worship, etc., but also came to the
conclusion that there were no ghosts. He used to go to the graveyard at
midnight to prove it to the people. After his uncle’s death in 1867, he
tried to join the government service, but knowing that he would not get
anything without bribery, he decided not to join. Although he wrote the
bar exam and thought of taking up the legal profession, he realised that
corruption was rampant in it and lying was a must, so he refrained from
doing so. Finally, he took up the teaching profession. Veeresalingam started
his career as a teacher in 1871 at the Government District School,
Rajahmundry. He later shifted to an Anglo-Vernacular school at
Dhavaleshwaram in 1874, where he worked as Headmaster.
3.5.2 Social Reform Programmes of Kandukuri:

As a teacher, Kandukuri taught children the concepts of social
reform along with lessons. He started a magazine called ‘Vivekavardhini’
in October 1874 to spread his views on the evils of society. In the first
issue, he informed that the objectives of Vivekavardhini were to “point
out the irregularities in the society, eradicate the evils, highlight the prevalent
corruption in the government system, and bring the corrupt people before
the society”. Vivekavardhini has become a lion’s dream under the rule
of corrupt people.

Kandukuri had a rivalry with Kokkonda Venkataratnam Panthlu
(1842-1915), a contemporary celebrity. After founding Kandukuri
Vivekavardhini, Kokkonda started a magazine called ‘Kandukuri
Vardhani’. To compete with that magazine, Kandukuri started a comedy

magazine called ‘Hasya Sanjeevini’. Kandukuri published the first
Farhasana in Telugu in this magazine. He published many farces and
satires in this magazine. Kandukuri and Kokkonda both addressed many
social issues at Dhavaleswaram. Veeresalingam gave two public lectures
at the Girls School in Rajahmundry on August 3rd and October 17th,
1879. In these lectures, he addressed women’s issues.

Veeresalingam established ‘Sangha Samskara Samajam’
(Social Reform Association) on September 8th, 1878, at Rajahmundry.
The main aim of this association was to carry on propaganda work by
arranging lecturers and conducting weekly meetings on various social
issues, particularly the emancipation of women. His students actively
participated in social reform activities, and they used to perform dramas
in front of the public. For community service, he founded a charitable
institution called ‘Hitakarini’ (Hitakarini Samaj in 1905) and gave his
wealth to it. He worked as a Telugu scholar in Rajahmundry for 25 years
and at Madras Presidency College for five years. He was a person who
enlightened the truth and principles he believed in and obeyed them. He
founded the Brahma Samaj in Andhra.

In 1875, the Provincial Social Club was started at Rajahmundry
with Basavaraju Gavarraju as its secretary. Veeresalingam regularly
attended club meetings. He compiled Matru Puja and Pitru Puja. He
also composed a collection of one hundred verses, “Stree Neeti Deepika’
(Lamp of Morals for Women), in 1884. Veeresalingam is a rationalist.
His life was intertwined with social reform and literary works. He was a
man who gave up his legal profession because of the corruption in the
government. He proved his desire for reforms against atrocities.
Vivekavardhani published the corrupt article in the magazine. He used
the magazine as a weapon to make people aware of other evils in society.
He girded his loins for social reform and jumped into action. In those
days, women were not considered to need education.

Veeresalingam not only agitated for and promoted women’s
education but also started a school for girls. He is self-educated and
introduced the system of co-education, where boys and girls study
together. He used to include children belonging to untouchable castes
and make them sit together with other children. Apart from educating
them for free, he used to buy books for them. He worked tirelessly
against child marriage and caste eradication. Vivekavardhani wrote essays
against it in those days when the system of prostitution was rooted.

Another major reform was Veeresalingam promoted widow
remarriage. The girls were married at an early age in their society. If their
husbands die, they become widows and face many hardships. To
overcome this social custom, the Widow Remarriage Association was
started in 1871 at Madras to promote widow remarriages, but it was
short-lived. This was revived in the 1880s by Dewan Bahadur Raghunath
Rao. He delivered his first speech on widow remarriage on August 3rd,
1879, at Rajahmundry; this was a milestone and had a tremendous impact
on widow remarriages in Andhra. Veeresalingam and his friends formed
the Widow Remarriage Association in 1879. This was the first time the
association addressed widow remarriages in Andhra. He also started
the Sree Punarvivaha Samajam (Association for the Remarriage of
Women) in 1880.

Narasimham, and Basavaraju Gavarraju stood by Veeresalingam. His
wife, Kandukuri Rajyalakshmamma, was supportive of her husband.
3.5.3 A Revolutionary Changes in Society:

Veeresalingam started a girl’s school in 1881 in Innispet and a
lower secondary school for girls at Rajahmundry. He further started
schools at Shimavaram in the west Godavari district in 1889 and the
Victoria Girls School at Rajahmundry in 1905 for women’s education.
He founded the Brahma Samaj in Andhra Pradesh. The establishment of
youth associations also started with Veeresalingam. For community
service, he founded a charitable institution called Hitakarini (Hitakarini
Samaj in 1905) and gave his wealth to it. Since childhood, he has been
accustomed to the qualities of action, adventure, and knowledge. The
teachings and writings of Raja Ramamohana Roy, Devendranath Tagore,
Kesava Chandra Sen, and Iswarachandra Vidyasagar brought a
revolutionary change to his spiritual thought. In 1887, he founded the
Sangha Reform Samaj and started a revolution against the evils of the
declining Hindu society. He fought against superstitions and traditional
customs. He also criticised the expensive rituals in marriage ceremonies.
He said that society avoids the large expenditure on marriages and the
abolition of taboos on sea voyages, and he campaigned against corruption
in public.

3.5.3 Writings of Veeresalingam:

He is widely considered the man who first brought about a
renaissance in Telugu literature. He was known as Yugakarta; he has
the title ‘Gadya Tikkana’. Veeresalingam spends the whole day working
on reforms and writes books at night. He was influenced by Kesab
Chandra Sen and the Brahmo Samaj. He wrote more than 100 books
between 1869 and 1919. He introduced the essay, biography,
autobiography, and novel into Telugu literature. His Satyavati
Charitam was the first social novel in Telugu. He wrote Rajashekhara
Charitamu, inspired by Oliver Goldsmith’s The Vicar of Wakefield.
He used literature as an instrument to fight social evils.

As much as Veeresalingam worked in community service, he
also worked in literature. Veeresalingam was a scholar of three languages,
namely Telugu, Sanskrit, and English. He wrote the first novel in the
Telugu language. He is also credited with introducing the autobiography
and the essay into Telugu literature. He also authored the first Telugu

book on modern science. Apart from these, he composed many ballads
and plays in Telugu. He also translated famous English works into Telugu.
He wrote two satakas during his studies. He used to write articles for
magazines. Vivekavardhani used to write in a simple style. He was one
of the first writers to write in colloquial language. He wrote more than

Yatra are prominent among his works. He translated many English and
Sanskrit texts into Telugu. He wrote texts for children and autobiographies.
He also published a history of Andhra poets. He wrote with concise
grammar. Chinnayasuri left the Sandhi and Vigraha parts of Nitichandrika
(Telugu Panchatantram) and completed the Veereshalingam.

3.5.4 Kandukuri Plays:

Vyavahara Dharmabodhini, Chamatkara Ratnavali, and
Shakespeare’s Comedy of Errors were written in Telugu and performed
by students. Abhignana Sakuntalam, Ratnavali-Sanskrit metaphor
Dhakshina Gograhanam, Satya Harishchandra, Malavikagni Mitramu,
and he have written 12 plays.

3.5.5 Kandukuri Novels:

Veeresalingam Pantulu wrote Rajasekara Charitra, the first
Telugu novel. In this novel, the author criticises social evils and superstitions.
He used the best Telugu proverbs and stood as a guide for the previous
generation of novels. Satya Raja Purva Desa Yatralu (The eastern
travels of the king of truth) is based on “Gulliver’s Travels,” written by
“Jonathan Swift” in English. He ridiculed the perverted happenings of the
society. It was an influential satire that dealt with the conventional practises
that existed in the society that dominates women. Satyavati Charitra,
(The History of Satyavati) (1883), a novel that preaches the importance
of women’s education, received good publicity in those days.
Chandramati Charitra, or The History of Chandramati (1884), is a
novel promoting religious themes, dharma, and women’s progress.
Veeresalingam pioneered such an experiment in entertaining and
conversational criticism of social evils. He published many farces in
“Viveka Vardhini” magazine. Rayasam Venkata Shiva” praised
Veeresalingam Prahasanam (farces). Ex: Pelli vellina Tarvata Pedda Pelli
(the big wedding after marriage). Chndragrahanam, Telu Mandu, Bahu
Baryatvam, Hindu Maha Sabha, Muncipal Natakam, etc are written by
Veerersalingam. . About 10 of the more than 50 plays written are based

--- PAGE 136 ---
on English sources. Independent works and Farhasanas (farces) were
published in three parts titled “Hasya Sanjeevani’.
3.5.6 Stories and Articles:

Most of Kandukuri’s stories promoted the advancement of
women. Some are adapted from English sources, but most are independent
works. He published mostly in the journal “Sati Hita Bodhini”. He
also brought out a collection of 158 short stories called “Neeti Katha
Manjari”.

Articles: Veeresalingam wrote up to 190 essays or lectures on
various topics. These were published in magazines like Satya Vadini,
Viveka Vardhini, Satihitabodhini, Chintamani, Satya
Samvardhini, and Telugu Janana. Ex: Domestic Congress and its
Missions, Constitutional Reform and Cultural Reforms, Profits due to
the English Government, etc.

Histories:

The writing of the History of Andhra Poets text is an important
milestone in the history of Andhra poetry. It is a first attempt at a
comprehensively researched and planned history of poets. The text was
published after examining many tablets, inscriptions, and printed and
unprinted texts. It was expanded from 1886 to 1917. It came out in
three parts. The autobiography was written in 1903-1915. This was
written in an unbiased and textual style. It was published by Vignana
Chandrika Granthamala, run by Komarraju Venkatalakshmana Rao. It
is a work that reflects the social condition of that time. This is the beginning
of textual autobiography in Telugu. He also wrote a history of the country,
ahistory of leaders, biographies of the best women, etc. The other writings
are “Sangraha Vyakaranam, Kavya Sangraham, etc.

3.5.7 Magazines run by Veeresalingam:

Kandukuri Veeresalingam Pantulu runs several magazines like
Viveka Vardini (1874), Satihita Bodhini (first periodical) (1883),
Satya Samvardhini, (Messenger of Truth), Chintamani, and Telugu
Janana, etc. As a person, a social reformer, and a writer, Veeresalingam
has many characteristics. In many respects, he is the leader and idol of
the Andhras. Kandukuri is a person who is famous as the father of modern
Andhra society. He started the first co-educational school and wrote the
first autobiography in Telugu. He wrote the first novel in Telugu. He wrote
the first farce in Telugu. The birthday of Kandukuri, the first dramatist,

3. 6 Raghupathi Venkataratnam Naidu (1862-1939)
Raghupathi Venkataratnam Naidu was an educator, social reformer,
epitome of chastity, and celibate known in the Telugu regions of India.
Raghupati Venkataratnam Naidu, along with Kandukuri Veereshalingam
Panthu, is the name that comes to mind for Social Reforms and for the
development of Brahmo Samaj in Andhra.
3.6.1 Early Life and Education:

Raghupathi Venkataratnam Naidu was born on 1* October 1862,
in Machilipatnam to a family of Telaga Naidu. Mother Seshamma was a
devotee of Vishnu. Naidu’s education began in the city of Chandrapur,
where his father, Appaiah Naidu, was working as a subedar in North
India. Therefore, he stayed in North India for a long time. Being in that
area since his childhood, Naidu was well versed in Hindi, Urdu, and
Persian. Due to the transfer of his father, Appiah Naidu, to Hyderabad,
Venkataratnam studied at Nizam High School in Chadarghat. In that
school, Sarojini Naidu was apprenticed to Naidu’s father, Aghornath
Chattopadhyay.

In 1884, he got married to Seshamamba while still a first-year
B.A. student. In 1889, she gave birth to a baby girl and died. After the
death of his wife, he never remarried and wore white for the rest of his
life. He was called ‘Shwetambara Rishi’. He used to educate poor
students and orphans. He kept a small part of his monthly income and
used the rest for poor students. He graduated from Madras Christian
College. He worked as a teacher in Eluru C.Y.MLS. and worked as a
principal in high schools in Rajahmundry and Bandar. In 1891, he has
completed M.A. in English Literature and worked for a while as Professor
of Anthropology and English at Pachiyappa College, Madras. He founded
the Social Purity Association in 1891 to train people to be honest citizens.
He worked as the principal of the Mehboob College, Secunderabad
from 1889-1904, and then of the Pitapuram Raja College, Kakinada,
between 1905 and 1919. Venkataratnam Naidu rendered great service
as the principal of the college. The Queen donated lakhs for the buildings
needed for the college and donated 35 acres of land for sports. Talking

Raghupati Venkatratnam Naidu
Source: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raghupathi_Venkataratnam_Naidu#/

In 1925, he was appointed as a Vice-Chancellor of Madras
University, holding that position until 1928. He established a fund of ten
thousand rupees in Madras University in the name of his teacher, Dr.
Miller, for the development of science. He was conferred a knighthood by
the British government in 1924. The Andhra Vishwakala Parishad Bill
was drafted and passed in the Legislative Assembly. In 1927, the
Vishwakala Parishad honoured Naidu with an honorary doctorate at its
first convocation. Raghupathi Venkataratnam Naidu died on 26" May
1939.
3.6.2 Raghupati Venkatratnam Naidu and his Social Reforms:

Kandukuri Veeresalingam Pantulu encouraged widow marriages
in Andhra society. Due to his concern for the Brahmo Samaj doctrines,
Venkataratnam became involved in global evangelism and
humanitarianism. He is merciful. Many orphaned boys and girls were
enrolled and educated. He strived for the abolition of the “Devadasi
system”. Naidu worked for the spread of women’s education. Apart
from admitting women to Pitapuram Raja College, he also arranged
accommodation and food facilities for the underprivileged and poor
students. Venkatratnam Joined the Brahmo Samaj and built an upasana
centre at Kakinada.

He worked for the abolition of the caste system, which is
important in Brahmo Samaj doctrine. He worked hard for his alcoholism.
In 1923, while a member of the Madras Legislative Council, he pressed
the government for the Prohibition Bill. He worked to eradicate
prostitution. He opposed the tradition of Bhogam Melas in auspicious
works. He edited the magazines ‘People’s Friend and Fellow
Workers’. in 1936, poor students were given 1500 rupees for their
education ina single month. A movement was organised for the prevention
of untouchability. Prominent in Brahmo Samaj doctrines, they worked to
eradicate the caste system and performed inter-caste marriages.
Venkatratnam said that “in today’s way of life, teachers are urged to be
more conscious than other people in society, and teachers are urged to
act as a ‘liberation army’. They worked to eradicate the caste system
and performed inter-caste marriages.

A social chastity society was established, and the following rules
were imposed on the members of that society: Prostitutes should not go
to meetings. Don’t encourage them. Singing and listening to obscene
songs should not be done. Efforts should be made to develop physical
and mental purity. His thoughts and writings were published in 1924,
authored by him and V. Ramkrishna Rao. Raghupathi Venkataratnam
Naidu received many awards and titles in recognition of his efforts in
various fields. Some of them are ‘Brahmarshi, Swetambara Rushi,
Apara Socrates, Kulapati, Diwan Bahadur, and Kaiser-e-Hind,
Sir. Raghupati Venkata Ratnam Naidugaru, the beloved of the people of
Andhra, passed away on 26" May 1939, after leading a full life. He was
described as the second great social reformer of Andhra.

3.7 Gurajada Apparao:

Gurjada Apparao (1862-1915) was a prominent Telugu writer
and a great poet who tried to bring about social change through his writings.
He enriched in Telugu literature and he was a rationalist. His works from
the 19th century and the first decade of the 20th century are still
appreciated by the people. He wrote in a vernacular language that could
be understood by all. Gurajada Apparao got the title ‘Father of
Advanced Poetry’. He was one of the main contributors to the
development of the common language in Telugu literature. He also has
the title ‘Kavi Shekhara’.

3.7.1Early Life and Education:

Gurajada Apparao was born on 21 September, 1862, to
Venkata Ramadasu and Kausalyamma in his maternal uncle’s house in Y.
Rayavaram, Visakhapatnam district, Andhra. He has a younger brother
named Shyama Rao. Gurjada Apparao’s family migrated to Visakha
mandal from Gurjada village in Krishna district during their grandfathers’
time. Apparao’s father worked as a Peshkar, Revenue Supervisor, and
Khiledaru in Vizianagaram. Apparao studied in Chipurupalli until he was
ten years old. Later, as their father passed away, they came to
Vijayanagaram. Here, he spent few weeks in extreme poverty and
continued his studies. At this time, M. R. College Principal C.
Chandrasekhara Sastry gave him a place to stay.

Gurajada completed his matriculation in 1882 and his F.A. in
1884. He joined the high school as a teacher. Gidugu Ramamurthy, the
leader of the vernacular language movement, helped him while he was
studying BA in Vizianagaram. Both of them are best friends. Every year,
the people of Y. Rayavaram celebrate Gurjada’s birthday. In 1884, he
joined Maharaja’s College as a teacher in their school. In 1885, Apparao
married Appala Narasamma. They have three children, two daughters
and one son. Daughter name was Oleti Lakshmi Narasamma, son
Venkata Ramadasu, and second daughter Puligadda Kondaiyamma. In
1886, he held the post of Head Clerk in the Deputy Collector’s office,
and in 1887, he held the post of lecturer in the college. In 1886, the Raja
joined their court. In 1897, when Maharaja Ananda Gajapati succumbed
to a minor illness, Apparao was appointed as the personal secretary to
Maharani Appala Kondamamba of Rewa. In 1911, he became a member
of the Madras University Board of Studies.

3.7. 2 Literature and Social Service:

Apparao lived in Vijayanagaram, which was then known as the
Kalinga kingdom. He had good relations with the Gajapati kings of
Vijayanagara. He first addressed the Vizianagaram Congress Party
meeting in 1887. At the same time, he joined Visakha Volunteer Service
for social service. In 1889, Ananda Gajapati was elected as the Vice
President of the Debating Club. At the same time, he wrote poems in
English with his younger brother, Shyamala Rao. ‘Sarangdhara’, an
English poem written by them, was appreciated by everyone when it
was read in the Indian leisure hour. It was then that Mr. Sambhuchandra

Mukherjee, a publisher of Reis and Rayyet in Calcutta, heard and
encouraged Apparao to write in Telugu.

He said that no matter how well they write in English, they can
write even better in their mother tongue. Indian Leisure Hour editor
Gundukurthi Venkata Ramanaiah also encouraged him in the same pursuit.
In 1891, Gurajada was appointed as the Institutional Legislative
Researcher at the Vijayanagara Institution. In the early days of the 20th
century, Gurajada A pparao fought with his colleague Gidugu Ramamurthy
Panthulu. Both of them tirelessly confronted the classical linguists in the
press, in meetings, and at the University of Madras. Gidugu was used for
argumentative power, and Gurajada’s writing power was used for practical
rhetoric.

3.7.3 Literary Works of Gurajada:

The world-famous Novel play Kanyashulakamu was written in
vernacular around 1890 (the exact year is not known). The story of this
play is a criticism of the evils of virginity and prostitution prevalent in
those days. His famous work Kanyashulkam play has a unique place in
the world of literature. The roles he created in this drama, like Girisham,
Madhuravani, Ramappa Pantulu, etc., became famous. In 1892,
Gurajada’s play Kanyashulkamu was performed for the first time took
place in 1892. The very first show got a lot of fame. With this, it can be
said that the use of language in literature has begun as a kind of experiment.
The play proved that vernacular could be used for social use as well as
for the pleasure of connoisseurs.

In 1897, Kanyashulkamu’s first composition was dedicated to
Maharaja Anandagajapati. Even Kasibhatta Brahmaya Shastry, who was
against the use of vernacular, praised the literary values of this play, which
made Apparao famous. Prakasika magazine was started in 1896. In
1897, Kanyashulkam was published by Vavilla Ramaswamy Sastrulu
and Sons, Madras. Kanyashulkam (the play) is the foremost among
Gurajada’s works. This drama by Gurjada is one of the best written in
Indian languages, criticising the abuse of virginity. He made many changes
to the first composition published in 1892 and published the second
composition in 1909. Written in vernacular, the dialect of the Vijayanagara
region, this play continues to entertain readers even after 100 years. The
play has been translated into Kannada, French, Russian, English (2 times),

Kanyashulkamu, the second composition we have today, was
written in 1909. In 1909, Kanyashulkam was written while resting in the
Nilgiri Hills due to poor health. A patriotic song written in 1910, called
‘Deshamunu Premicnhumanna’ (Do you Love the country), became
very popular. In 1911, he was appointed to the Board of Studies at
Madras University. In the same year, he started the Andhra Sahitya
Parishad with friends. Another of his compositions is the well-known
song ‘Puttadi Bomma Poornamma’. Its theme is also virginity. This is
the last verse of this lyrical poem of compassion.

Gurajada Apparao
3.7.4 Other Works of Gurajada Apparao:

Sarangadhara (English poetry, published in Indian Leisure Hour
(Vijayanagar) and Rees and Rayyat (Bengal), Pumamma, Kondubhattiam,
Nilgiri songs, String of pearls (Muthyala Saralu), Kanyaka, Satyavrati
Shatakam, Bilhantyam (imperfect), Subhadra, Langarethu, Dinchulangaru,
Lavanaraj’s dream (Lavanaraju Kala), Kasulu, Soudamini (a prototype
of a novel), Stories, (Devudu Chesina Manusulu), Correction
(Diddubatu), Matilda, A Reformer’s Heart (Samskartha Hrudayam),
Matamu Vimathamu, Pushapala Vikalu, etc. Gurajada’s detractors were
mainly some on his sentiments, others on his literary language. While he
advocated reforms, those who opposed his views on social matters
opposed them on sentiments, while the advocates of literary language
opposed him on the language of usage. Some of the Telugu poets were
praised about Gurajada.

“There is no other work in Indian literature that can compare to

3.8 Komarraju Venkata Lakshmana Rao:

The twentieth century was a great era of Telugu literary and social
development. Around the same period, four great men (Kandukuri
veeresalingam, Gurajada Apparao, Gidugu Rammurthy Panthulu, and
Kommarraju) led the Telugu language and the Telugu race towards the
modern era. They are the pioneers of the new age who have given the
Telugu people enough progress for ten generations in one generation.
Lakshmana Rao is one of the great men who awakened the Telugu nation.
Komarraju Venkata Lakshmana Rao (1877-1923) was a great literary
figure, multi talented personality, historian, social scientist, poet, inspirer,
scientist, the first creator of ‘Vigyan Sarvasvam’ (Encyclopedia) in Telugu
and the founder of ‘Vignana Chandrika Mandali’. He is the best
scientist who introduced history research to Telugu people and took the
initiative to provide high standards of history and science works in Telugu.
Sahitya Krishiwala (Worker of Literature), who died at the age of 46,
accomplished enough work for an institution in his short lifetime. Moreover,
he was a companion, encourager, and inspiration to many literary figures.
3.8.1 Early Life of Komarraju Venkata Lakshmana Rao:

Lakshmana Rao was born on 18" May1877, at Penuganchiprolu
in Krishna district. The famous writer Bandaru Acchamamba is his elder
sister. Lakshmana Rao’s father died when he was at the age of three
years. Lakshmana Rao completed his primary education in Bhuvangiri
under the patronage of his stepbrother Shankara Rao. Lakshmana Rao’s
uncle Bandaru Madhavrao, was a government employee in Nagpur. His
second wife is Achamamba. So Lakshmana Rao stayed in Nagpur and
learned Marathi. He graduated with a BA in 1900, then studied privately
and passed an MA in 1902. He wrote essays and poems in Marathi.
Apart from Telugu, Marathi, and English, he also acquired proficiency in
Sanskrit, Bengali, Urdu, and Hindi. After his education in Maharashtra,
he gota job at the institution of Munagala Raja Nayani Venkata Ranga
Rao. Raja is progressive and Telugu language lover. Lakshmana Rao
provided adequate leisure and financial support to continue his literary

work while working. Due to his association, Komarraju got good
encouragement for the development of Telugu. Lakshmana Rao came in
contact with many scholars while he was in Maharashtra.

In 1899, Lakshmana Rao rejected the claim that ‘Parnashala’
was located at Nasika Triambakam in Maharashtra, as mentioned in
Balagangadhara Tilak’s “Ramayana’’. Sources prove that Parnashala is
near the Godavari. He was a follower of Tilak. At that time, Lakshmana
Rao was 22 years old. In 1901, Komarraju Lakshmana Rao played a
leading role in establishing ‘Shrikrishna Devarayandhrabhasha Nilayam’
and, in 1906, ‘Vignana Chandrika Mandali’. He started the great task of
preparing a complete scientific corpus in Telugu.

3.8.2 Writings of Lakhsmana Rao:

He edited ‘Samachar’ and various ‘ Vigyan Vistar’ magazines
in Maharashtra. He used to write articles for magazines like Kesari and
Marati. The ancient Maharashtrian poet Moropant wrote
Mahabharatha. Lakhsmana Rao corrected it, prepared a
Suddhaprati(Xerox), and announced Karnaparva. This is the first book
edited by him. However, he did not lose touch with the Andhra language
or the affairs of Andhra. He used to write articles in Telugu magazines
while staying in Nagpur. At that time, Acchamamba and Lakshmana Rao
used to write articles in “7elugu Janana”’, amagazine run by Rayasam
Venkatashivudu, a teacher at Bejawada Christian School, for the spread
of women’s education. “History of Shivaji” was his first Telugu book.
His essays like “Hindu Maha Yugam” and “Muslim Maha Yuga” were
later published under the title “Lakshmanaraya Vyasavali”. Biographies
of Great Men and Biography of Ravichettu Ranga Rao are books written
by Komarraju Venkatalakshmana Rao.

Komarraju Venkata Lakshmana Rao

3.8.2 Srikrishna Devaraya Andhra Bhasha Nilayam:

Komarraju Lakshmana Rao, Nayani Venkataranga Rao,
Ravichettu Ranga Rao, Adipudi Somanatha Rao, Mylavarapu Narasimha
Shastri, and others established Sri Krishna Devaraya Andhrabhasha
Nilayam on 1“ September 1901, at Ravichettu Ranga Rao’s residence
at the then Residency Bazar in Hyderabad. The main objective of
establishing this library is to improve the status of Telugu in the Telangana
region. This is the first library started using advanced methods of Telugu
speaking region. The Telugu language has been served well by this
institution. Dignitaries like Adiraju Veerabhadra Rao served as its
secretaries.

3.8.3 Vignan Chandrika Granthamandali:

Recognising that development in science is essential for society
to move forward, Lakshmana Rao, Nayani Venkatarangarao, Gadicharla
Harisarvothamrao, Ayyadevara Kaleswara Rao, and Ravichettu Ranga
Rao established Vignana Chandrika Granthamandali in Hyderabad in
1906. Until then, work in Telugu was mostly limited to literature. Their
aim is to publish books on subjects like science and history in Telugu to
provide modern knowledge to all. “Abraham Lincoln” by Gadicharla
Hari Sarvottam Rao was the first book presented by Vignana Chandrika
Grantha Mandali to Telugu Desam. Lakshmana Rao handled much of
the burden for its publication, from proofreading to writing the first few
sentence. Between 1906 and 1910, the council published more than 30
books. It is said that Lakshmana Rao’s hand as an editor in all the books
is not infected. In 1908, this institution was shifted to Madras.

3.8.4 Andhra Council of Research:

On 27" December 1922, Lakshmana Rao, Adiraju Veerabhadra
Rao, etc. established the Andhra Research Council in Hyderabad. The
objectives of this organisation are historical research, publication of
legislative texts, and publication of unpublished texts. This institution
published books like Telangana inscriptions, Shitabukhan Charitra, etc.
Later, it was changed to Lakshmanaraya Research Council. The
organisation is now nominally located in the Hyderabad office of the
Andhra Saraswati Parishad.

3.8.5 Andhra Saraswati Parishad:

Lakshmana Rao was one of the founders of the Andhra Saraswati
Parishad in Kovvur in 1916. Apart from being a life member from the
beginning, he was also its secretary for some time.

3.8.6 Andhra Vignana Sarvaswam: (Andhra science is everything):

Lakshmana is the Omniscient. He researches everything
thoroughly and writes it as a comprehensive, independent essay.
Everything else in Lakshmana Rao’s literary life is one, and everything in
science is one. He wanted to spread world knowledge to all Telugus.
His ardent desire was to bring out the whole of Andhra science along the
lines of a British encyclopaedia. During 1912-13, he undertook this great
work. He was not only the chief editor but also the chief writer. Gadicharla
Harisarvottam Rao, Achanta Lakshmipathy, Mallampalli Somesekhara
Sharma, Raiprolu Subbarao, and others stood with him. Lakshmana Rao
and Harisarvottama Rao used to go to the Kannemera Library in Madras
without missing a single day, stay till it closed, and collect information
from the files of books.

At first, it was published as ‘A’ at the rate of one hundred pages
per month. The day and night worked hard and published three volumes.
In this, he himself composed 40 articles out of 100 on various subjects
like science, language, astronomy, history, and art. He wrote essays on
various subjects like “Atharvaveda, Advaita, Abhignana
Shakuntalam, Alankaras, Ashtadasa Mahapuranas, Atta Baindu,
and Ashtadhyayi”. Lakshmana Rao died on 12" July 1923, while
considering the inscriptions for writing the Andhra volume. Lakshmana
Rao spent his days and nights researching inscriptions and other texts. At
that time, he developed asthma. He rested for some time in Madanapalle
and came back to Madras.

Thus Andhra Vigyan Sarvasvam “A” word stopped with three
volumes. Later, Kashinadhuni Nageswara Rao published it in two volumes
with the collaboration of more scholars. Later, the Telugu Language
Committee, formed under the chairmanship of Dr. Bejawada
Gopalareddy, published fourteen volumes in order of subject matter. In
1986, this institution merged with Potti Sriramulu Telugu University. After
that, Urlam Zamindar (Narasannapeta, Srikakulam) published “Andhra
Vignanam’ in five volumes during 1938-1941. Among Lakshmana Rao’s
writings, the essay “Science Reading in Vernaculars” should be specially
mentioned. Lakshmana Rao encouraged patriotism in his writings. He
was participated in the Calcutta Congress Mahasabha in 1906. The 1907,
Krishna District Congress Mahasabha Secretary invited prominent people
to the meeting. In 1908, his associate Gadicharla Harisarvottam Rao
was arrested, and Lakshmana Rao supported their family.

3.8.7 Social Reformer:

Lakshmana Rao strongly opposed child marriages. He
encouraged widow marriage, post-menstrual marriage, and prohibition
of Bhogam Melas, prevention of obscurity, seafaring, and intermarriage.
He, along with his sister Atchamamba, tried to promote education among
women. He used to participate in the programme of teaching Hariyans at
night. Kuruganti Sitaramaiah said in his article that Lakshmana Rao Pantulu
is nota person but an organisation. Famous historians and various scientists
of Andhra Pradesh were attracted by him and became known to the
country through ‘Vignanachandrika Granthamandali’. Adi Raju
Veerabhadra Rao, who carried out historical research in Telangana as
the secretary of the Laxmana Raya Research Council, was trained by
Laxman Rao. Lakshmana Rao used many new terms in Telugu, for
instance, university, Rastiya etc. Lakshmana Rao died at the age of 46,
in the same room where Kandukuri Veeresalingam died, while examining
inscriptions to write the Andhra volume. There are many other social
reformers contributed their services to Andhra society.

3.9 New Literary Movements

Telugu Language is one of the oldest languages in India;
it has rich cultural and traditional history of South India. The
Telugu Literature is consists of a rich preserve of stories, dramas,
epics, novels, song lyrics and poems. The Telugu literature is a
vast store house of literary products by eminent telugu poets.
Telugu literature is the collection of works written in the Telugu language.
There are some indications that Telugu literature dates back to at least
the middle of the first millennium, with the first works dating from the 11
century when Nannaya translated Sanskrit into Telugu.

The language enjoyed a golden age under Vijayanagara king-
poet Krishnadevaraya. Early Telugu literature is predominantly religious
in subject matter. In pre-Nannaya period (Before 1020 AD), Telugu
literature existed in the form of inscriptions. The Age of Puranas period
between 1020-1400 CE, trinity of poets, popularly known as Kavi trayam
i.e ‘Nannnayya, Tikkana, Errana’ developed literature in the form of
their writings. The Prabhanda (Stories in verse form with a tight metrical
structure) period between 1400-1600 CE, Srinatha, Vemana, Potana,
Annamayya, Thimmana, Dhurjati, and Tenali Ramakrishna etc developed
Prabhanda style of composition in their writings. The poets of post

Prabhanda Period form 1600-1850 composing literary works in telugu
consisting of one hundred poetic stanzas, popularly known as Satakas.
They also wrote Champu, Kavyam, poetry, stories, songs, lyrics, Chatu
(Charming utterance), Padam, (Lyric poems), Dwipada (couplet with a
specific rhyme scheme), and poetic works etc. In the Modern period,
the writing styles of telugu literature is changed. The period of modern
Telugu poetry began with Gurajada Apparao, who changed the contours
of Telugu poetry with his pearl extracts and was perfected by later writers
in the era of Romanticism, including Raiprolu and Devulapalli
Krishnasastry. Kandukuri, and Gurujada’s attempt to reform Telugu poetry
by removing the old rules and styles reached its pinnacle with Sri Sri. His
famous work “Maha Prasthanam” is the role model style of many
writers. They followed and continue to enrich literature style. The poets
like Rayaprolu Subba Rao, Viswanatha Satyanarayana, Gurram Jhashuva,
Boyi Bheemanna and many other poets started new literary movement
in the field of Modern Telugu Literature.

3.10 Rayaprolu Subba Rao:

Rayaprolu Subba Rao (1892-1984) was among the pioneers of
modern Telugu literature. He was inspired by the Western literary
movement and brought romanticism into Telugu literature by breaking
away from the traditional translations of Sanskrit literature. Raiprolu was
a great nationalist. His patriotic songs are very inspiring. Personally,
Raiprolu was the person who was accused of acting against the fact that
patriotism is written in poetry.

He is known as ‘Abhinava Nannaya’. He was born in
Garlapadu village, Kakumandu mandal, on 13 March 1892 in Baptla,
Guntur District, Andhra Pradesh. He was a poet, lyricist, translator, and
author of several books and novels. He was a role model of New Literary
movements. His maternal uncle, Avvari Subramanya Sastry, initially taught
him Sanskrit and Telugu. He attended the Board High School
in Bapatla and was later educated in Kakinada, Rajahmundry, and
Hyderabad. Chilukuri Veerabhadra Rao and Attili Satya Narayana
cooperated with Rayaprolu. He was became a Professor and Head at
Osmania University, Hyderabad. He was nominated as an executive
member of Andhra Sahitya Academy. He was died in 30" June, 1984.
He was the recipient of the Sahitya Akademi Award for Telugu Writers
for his poetic work, ‘Misra Manjari’ in 1965. He introduced the concept
of ‘Amalina Shringara Tatvamu’ into Telugu literature.

3.10.1 Style of Poetry and Literature:

Rayprolu Subbarao impresses the viewers at first glance.
Rayaprolu Subbarao, known as the Father of Modern Poetry, he was
the pioneer of Telugu poetry. According to him, there is no poetry without
feeling. Where there is poetry, there is emotion. Subbarao’s literature is
amixture of Telugu Indian culture and Western ideas. He brought Western
romanticism, especially English lyric style, to Telugu literature. He followed
classical Sanskrit and Telugu in his writings. His literary themes include
sensual material, love of nature, patriotism, spirituality, humanitarianism,
and social reform. Rayaprolu preferred separation in his writings.
Rayaprolu was a great patriot and Telugu nationalist. He praised the
(past) greatness of Telugu warriors to awaken the sleeping Telugu pride.
Rayaprolu Subbarao defined Telugu romanticism and began a new era
in Telugu literature. His romantic literature is considered the watershed in
Telugu literature for its modernity.

It is said that a new era in Telugu poetry started with
‘Trikankana’, which he wrote in 1913. In this way, he invented the
philosophy of romance. Emotionalism is an art form that gives priority to
the artist’s imagination, feelings, and creativity. This art form, which
flourished in Germany and France in the 18th century, deeply influenced
painters, writers, sculptors, and poets. Rayaprolu unleashed this sentiment
in the western countries in Telugu. He diverted Telugu poetry, which was
heavily dependent on Sanskrit works, towards independent works. From
1921, he worked as a lecturer, reader, and professor in the Telugu
Department of Osmania University, Hyderabad and Venkateswara
University, Tirupati and continued as Principal of Warangal College for
some time. Arudra explains that the poets of that time undoubtedly felt
that modern poetry, especially sentimental poetry, was encouraged by
the introduction of English literature. He initiated the Khandakavya
process with the theme that young couples whose love did not lead to
marriage decided to remain friends. He was fluent in Telugu and Sanskrit
with the theme that young couples whose love did not lead to marriage
decided to remain friends. He has added new charms to Telugu poetry
by interweaving sentimentality according to our society.

He assisted Komarraju Venkata Lakshmana Rao in the

preparation of ‘Andhra Vignana Sarvasvamu’. He was the chief editor

of ‘Andhra Maha Bhagavatam’, published by Telugu
Academy, Hyderabad. He worked at the Department of Telugu at
Osmania University, Hyderabad, as a professor and head of this
department for 25 years. Khandavalli Lakshmi Ranjanam, Divakarla
Venkata Avadhani, B. Rama Raju, and C. Narayana Reddy were
associated with him as professors.

His translations are: Anumati (Permission), Bhaja
Govindamu (Prayer to Govinda) (Telugu translation of
Shankaracharya’s Bhaja Govindham), Soundarya Lahari (Below of
Beauty) (Telugu translation of Shankaracharya’s Soundarya Lahari),
Sundara Kanda (Beauty Section) (Telugu translation of
Valmiki Ramayana), Dootha Mattebhamu (Messenger Elephant),
Lalitha (Telugu translation of The Hermit of [Oliver Goldsmith],
Madhukala (Telugu) etc. He wrote many independent works like
Trunakankanamu (Grass Bracelet), Andhravali (Andhra Row),
Kashta Kamala (Kamala in Distress), Ramyalokamu (Beautiful Sight),
Swapnakumaramu, 7e/ugutoata (Telugu Garden), Vanamala (Forest
Necklace), Misra Manjari (Mixed Bouquet), Kannepatalu (Virgin
Songs), Jadakutchulu (Braid Tassels), His works are mainly Khanda
kavyas. He was died on 1984 June 30 at the age of 92. One patriotic
verse by Subba Rao, “Edesamegina Endukalidina” (also known as
“Janmabhum?’), is a perennial favourite, being regularly sung and recited.
It was particularly popular for public events in the pre-Independence
Andhra region. The verse has been adapted many times into a song for
use in Telugu films.

‘Edesamegina endukalidina
epeetamekkina evaremanina
Pogadara Neetalli Bhoomi Bharatini
Nilupara neejaati nindu gauravamu’
English translation by A. Viswam

To whichever country you go,
And wherever you place your feet
Whatever honour you get
And whatever people say,
Praise Bharati, your motherland.
Hold aloft your nations (full) honour.

Sri Rayaprolu Subba Rao was awarded the ‘Kalaprapoorna’
from Andhra University in 1977, and he was given the Sahitya Akademi
Award in 1965. For those who have studied the sentimental poetry and
beauty of the Rayaprolu with interest, the Rayaprolu pulses, heartbeats,
and readings can be perceived. Raiprolu are the pioneers of the era,
