3.11 Visawanatha Satyanarayana:

Viswanatha Satyanarayana (1895-1976) was a 20th-century
Telugu writer entitled “Kavi Samrat”. His works include poetry, novels,
dramatic drama, short stories, and speeches. History, philosophy, religion,
sociology, political science, linguistics, psychology, consciousness studies,
epistemology, aesthetics, and spirituality are many different subjects.
Vishwanatha wrote in a modern, classical style, in complex ways. He
wrote many poems, novels, plays, poems, essays, criticisms, essays,
stories, and histories; he had erudition and talents.

3.11.1 Early Life:

Vishwanatha was born on 10" September 1895, in Nandamuru
(Unguturu mandal) of Krishna district. Father Sobhanadri and mother
Parvati. He belongs to a Telugu- Vedic Brahmin family. He enjoyed a
comfortable in his childhood. Viswanatha Satyanarayana has said many
times that his father Sobhanadri’s influence was tremendous on him.
Vishwanatha claimed that his virtues like generosity and devotion came
from his father. He did his primary education in Nandamuru, Indupalli,
and Pedapadu villages. Chellapilla Venkata Sastri was his teacher at
Bandaru High School.

While studying at Viswanatha Satyanarayana College in 1921,
he left the college to participate in the non-cooperation movement from
the call of Mahatma Gandhi. From 1921 to 1926, he worked as a teacher
at Andhra National College, Bandar. After completing his B.A. in 1926-
27, he joined the Bandar Hindu College as a lecturer. He has worked in
several colleges, and he has served in various capacities. He faced a lot
of financial problems after his father’s death, but he became famous as a
poet during this period. In 1957, Viswanatha served as the Vice President
of the Andhra Pradesh Sahitya Akademi, and in 1958, he was a nominated
member of the Legislative Council. Vishwanatha Satyanarayana’s first

wife’s name was Varalakshmi. They had a son named Achyutadevaraya.
His first wife, Varalakshmi, who had turned him into an alchemist, died of
illness. That separation left a deep impression on Viswanatha’s life and
thoughts. His marriage to Varalakshmi and her untimely death had a
profound impact on his life and literature. The period from 1932 to 1938
was the most difficult in his life. During this time, he suffered financial
hardships as he had to survive without a steady job.

3.11.2 Literary Works of Viswanatha:

Vishwanatha’s writing career began in 1916 with “Vishvesvara
Shatakam”. He wrote “Andhra Pourushamu” under the influence
of the Indian National movement. Already in 1920, he had gained
recognition as a famous poet in Telugu. His works have been translated
into several other languages. He has produced plays and speeches for
radio. After retiring as principal of Karimnagar Government College in
1961, he devoted his full time to writing Telugu literature. Vishwanatha
gave the name to portray a winsome and unique character. He analyses
the mentality of the characters depending on the situation and the scene.
In his poems, Self-eloquent characters, self-luminous characters,
magnificent and beautiful sculptures were made. Their portrayal of female
characters is amazing.

Viswanatha’s literary works include 30 poems, 20 plays, 60
novels, 10 critical estimates, 200 Khanda kavyas, 35 short stories, three
play lets, 70 essays, 50 radio plays, 10 essays in English, 10 works in
Sanskrit, three translations, 100 introductions and forewords, as well as
radio talks. Some of his poems and novels have been translated into
English, Hindi, Tamil, Malayalam, Urdu, and Sanskrit. Veyipadagalu
was later translated into Hindi by former Prime Minister PV Narasimha
Rao as Sahasraphan. He wrote many more articles and lectures.

Some of Viswanatha’s works worth mentioning are:
Andhrapaurusham (the Andhra valour), Andhra Prashasti (the fame
of the Andhras), Ramayana Kalpavriksham, Eka Veera, Veyipadagalu,
Kinnerasani songs, Puranavaira Granthmala, Nepali Rajavamsa
Charita, Kashmiri historical novels, Viswanatha Madhakkars, Nannaya
Prasanna Katha Kalitarthayukti, etc. Viswanatha Satyanarayana’s many
works have become key texts in many literary theories and arguments in
20th-century Telugu literature.

In Viswanatha’s writings, not only his erudition but also his
penmanship, characterization, and historical understanding are excellent.

Each novel impresses the readers with its intriguing story, unique style,
and remarkable characters. Shatakams play a prominent role in
Viswanatha Satyanarayana’s major works of literature like Srigiri
satakam, Kalahasthi satakam, Bhadragiri, Kulaswami, Sheshadri,
Draksharama, Nandamuru, etc. Vishwanatha’s personality was also
bound by ancient practices and rituals. He loved Indianness and the Telugu
language. He expressed his opinions and preferences honestly. Because
of this, there were many people who opposed Vishwanatha. He had
respect for western literature. In the course of time, he studied the works
of poets like Shakespeare, Milton, and Shelley. Vishwanatha has a unique
style. His world experience hangs in his writings. These words about him
describe his style: “‘All my poetry is based on my life experiences. I have
become a poet of the world”.

3.11.3 Awards:

The Andhra people honoured him with the title “Kavi Samrat”
in accordance with their traditions. In 1964, Andhra Vishwa
Kalaparishad honoured him with the title “Kalaprapurna”. In 1942
for Sankranthi, he was honoured with “Gajarohanam’”’ in the temple.
In 1956, his celebrations were also held in Gudivada. Sri Venkateswara
University, Tirupati awarded the D.Litt. In 1962, he was awarded the

‘Kendra Sahitya Akademi Prize for his work “Vishvanatha
Madhakkarlu”. In 1970, the Andhra Pradesh government honoured
him as a court poet. In 1970, the government of India honoured him with
the Padma Bhushan award. In 1971, he received the Jnana Peetha
award for the “Ramayana Kalpavriksha’. He was the first Telugu
writer to win the Jnanpeetha Award. Postage stamp bearing Viswanatha
Satyanarayana’s figure were released on 26" April, 2017. Along with
Raiprolu Subbarao and Devulapalli Krishna Sastri, Viswanatha
Satyanarayana is also known as a prominent figure in the field of
sentimental poetry. Vishwanatha’s poems like Ritusamharam and Telugu
Ritulu are famous for his subtle descriptions of the seasons in Telugu
drama. Devotions are important among his many poetic works.
3.12.Gurrram Jashua (1895-1971)

Guram Jashua (1895-1971) was a Telugu poet who ranks among
the modern Telugu poets. He moved away from the sentimental poetic
style of contemporary poetry and wrote with the expectation of social
benefit. He was born into a lower caste that was considered low, and he

--- PAGE 172 ---
faced many humiliations. But Joshua turned against these superstitions
using poetry as a weapon; he got rewards even though he was met with
slander.

3.12.1 Early Life:

Jashua was born on 28" September 1895, to Gurram Veeraya
and Lingamma in Chatragadpadu, Vinukonda Mandal, Guntur District,
Andhra Pradesh. Parents belong to different castes. His father was a
Yadav, his mother was of a scheduled caste, and his father worked as a
Fastor. This one thing is enough to face insults and slander in a society
full of superstitions. Childhood passed comfortably in the green fields of
Vinukonda village. A fter entering school to study, Joshua began to struggle.
He faced many insults from teachers and fellow children. If the upper-
caste children taunted them in the name of caste, he turned around and
beat them. He married Mary in 1910. He used to work in a missionary
school ona salary of three rupees per month. After losing that job, he
went to Rajahmundry and worked there as a film reader in 1915-16. He
worked for 10 years as a teacher in the Lutheran Church in Guntur.
Later, from 1928 to 1942, he worked as a Telugu scholar at a high
school in Guntur. He also worked as a war propagandist during World
War-II. Between 1957 and 1959, he worked as a programme producer
at the Madras Radio Centre. Joshua worked many jobs for a living, got
membership in the Andhra Pradesh Legislative Council in 1964. Gurram
Jashua died on 24" July, 1971 at Guntur.

3.12.2 Literary Works of Jashua:

From childhood, Jashua had creative energy. He used to draw
pictures and sing songs. He developed an interest in poetry in the
company of his childhood friend Deepala Picchaiah Shastry. Jashua
learned Meghasandesam, Raghuvamsam, and Kumara Sambhavam from
Hanumanchshastry. Jashua wrote 36 texts and many other poetic
passages. The Gabbilam (1941) is one of his best works. Kalidasa
follows the pattern of Meghasandesh. In this novel, the poet sends a
message with a Gabbilam (a bat) to Kasi Viswanath about his problems.
The way the protagonist’s agony is depicted is heart-wrenching.
‘Firdausi’, Muntaj Mahal, and ‘Christu’ in 1932 were other major
works of Jashua. He wrote ‘Andhra Bhija’ (1934), Kandhi Siku
(1945), ‘Bapuji, Netaji’ (1948), and Christu Charitra in (1958).
The last work was ‘Nagarjuna Sagaram and ‘Na Kadha’ in 1966.

He wrote many texts, novels, and poetic works. Visalandhra Publishing
House published Jashua’s works in four volumes.
3.12.3 Titles and Awards:

Jashua received many titles and awards during his lifetime.
Chellapilla Venkata Shastri, one of the Venkata poets of Tirupati, put a
Gandapenderam on the feet of Jashua and said that he blessed his birth
by touching the feet of this poet. Jashua considered it the highest award
he had ever received. He was called the “Poet of the Millennium” for his
timeless pieces of poetry and literature. He received many titles and
honours. ‘Kavitha Visarada, Kavikokila, Kavi Diggaja, Navayuga
Kavichakravarti, Mathura Srinatha, and Vishvakavi Samrat’ became
famous. The Central Sahitya Akademi Award for his writing ‘History
of Christ’ in 1964. He was appointed a member of the Andhra Pradesh
Legislative Council in the same year. In 1970, Andhra University honoured
him with the Kalaprapurna’. In 1970, he was awarded the Padma
Bhushan by the Government of India.

3.12.4 Jashua Memorial Awards:

The Jashua Foundation, established by Jashua’s daughter
Hemalatha Lavanam, gives the Jashua Sahitya Award to writers in Indian
languages who have written works with human values. The Jashua
Research Centre at the Telugu Academy presented the Poets and Writers
Awards on September 28 (Jashua’s birthday). Joshua Sahitya Vishita
Award for outstanding contribution to Dalit literature.

3.13 Boyi Bhimanna (1911-2005):

Boyi Bhimanna was a poet and writer who enriched Telugu
literature by writing works with the aspirations of social consciousness.
Boyi Bhimanna was born ina remote village to a Dalit family. He is a
poet who has received many honours and awards, including the Central
Sahitya Akademi Award and the Padma Bhushan. Along with poverty,
Bheemanna experienced evils like untouchability from childhood.
Dr.B.R.Ambedkar’s book on caste eradication was translated into Telugu.
3.13.1 Early Life of Bhimanna:

Bhimanna was born on 19" September 1911in Mamidikuduru
village of East Godavari district in a Haryan family. Nagamma and Pullaiah
are his parents. Five sons and one daughter were born to them. Bhimanna
graduated in 1935 with a B.A. B.Ed in 1937. After passing the
examination, he dedicated his life to telugu literature. Boyi Bhimanna is a

famous writer. He wrote about 70 books. Besides poetry, he wrote lyrical
works and dramas. An investigative focus is evident in the prefaces written
to various books. He wrote many sentimental songs for radio broadcasts.
Along with all this, he translated some of Ambedkar’s works into Telugu
and wrote in praise of his life and personality. Dr. Darla Venkateswara
Rao commented, Jaya Jaya Ambedkara! ‘, written by them, is the national
anthem of Dalits and is still sung as a prayer song.

3.13.2 Literary Service:

Potti Sri Ramulu Telugu University Sahitya Peetha named Boyi
Bhimanna is being established, and all his works are being published,
including Boyibhimanna from ‘Paleru to Padmashri,” written by
Haimavati. It is like a rudder that expresses their literary outlook. Bhimanna
remained a nationalist throughout his life. Nationalism involves the
aspiration to live with self-respect while conforming to the domestic reality
of one’s birth and upbringing. Boyi Bhimanna is a writer who continued
Ambedkar’s ambition in literature. Telugu University released the first
volume, titled ‘Boyi Bhimanna Aggregasahitya’. It contains
‘Ambedkar in Songs’ (Pages 306-462). It contains 118 songs describing
Ambedkar’s personality and ideology. It is in this context that Bhimanna
used the term ‘Dalit’. However, he declared in his last days that he did
not want to use the word Dalit. According to him, Dharma is religion;
Buddhism is Dharma.

He wrote that Dharma that does not uplift the Dalits is lame.
Dalits are described as ‘Avarnas, Panchamas, and Harijans’. He
was a dramatist who was strongly opposed to the abolition of caste. A
distinctive feature of Bhimanna’s literature is that not all people in the
society are bad but there are also good people. In the 1973 poem
‘Gudiselu Kalipapanayi’, the ambition of Dalits to create awareness
among Dalits about why Dalit huts are burning every year and why only
new huts are coming up again is seen. In the play ‘Kuliraju’ (1946), he
touched upon caste as well as economics. Bhimanna is a staunch
opponent of violence. He opposed them, saying that all Dalits are workers.
He dedicated the play ‘Paleru’ to Konada Suryaprakasarao, who
worked in the Communist Party and advocated the separation of caste
and class. In the play, Paleru Bhimanna clearly declared his views on
class and caste. There is no caste system in India. Varna means caste
system. “Untouchability must be removed first for all the labourers to

3.13.3 Writings and Awards:

He wrote fifteen poems. Kendra Sahitya Akademi Award for
the book ‘Gudiselu Kalipothunnai’ . In 2001, the Government of
India honoured Bhimanna with the ‘Padma Bhushan’. He wrote several
plays like ‘Paleru, Kuliraju, Asuya, Pragathi, Veda Vyasa,
Navajevan Patrika, Balayogi’, etc. He also wrote text works like
Ambedkarism, Edigo Edi Bhagavadgita, Dharmam Kosam Poratam, etc.
He has conferred several awards in his account, i.e., Kala Prapurna by
Andhra Vishwa Kala Parishad (1971), Padma Shri Award (1973),
Central Sahitya Akademi Award (1975), Kashi Vidyapeeth Honorary
Doctorate (1976), Nagarjuna University has given an honorary doctorate,
Distinguished Award in Literature from Telugu University (1991), Padma
Bhushan Award (2001), and Tangirala Krishnaprasad Memorial Award
(2004), etc.

3.14 Srirangam Srinivasa Rao:

Srirangam Srinivasa Rao (1910-1983), popularly known as

Sri Sri, was a prominent Telugu poet. He is famous as a revolutionary
poet, a critic of traditional and rhythmic poetry, the president of the
Progressive Writers Association, the founder president of the
Revolutionary Writers Association, and a film lyricist. He is a rationalist
and an atheist. ‘Mahaprasthanam’ is one of his famous works. Mahakavi
Sri Sri ruled the Telugu literature of the twentieth century. He was a
rationalist and an atheist. Sri Sri is widely recognized as a great poet.
3.14.1 Sri Sri’s Early Life and Career:
Sri Sri was born on 30" April 1910, to Pudipeddi Venkataramaniah and
Appalakonda. Srirangam was adopted by Suryanarayana, and his
surname changed to Srirangam. He did his primary education in
Visakhapatnam. He was passed out SSLC in 1925. In the same year, he
got married to Ramanamma. He completed his BA (Zoology) at Madras
University in 1931. In 1935, he joined Mrs. AVS College in
Visakhapatnam as a demonstrator. In 1938, he joined the Madras Andhra
Prabha as a sub-editor. After that, he did various jobs in Akashavani,
Delhi. He was also worked with Nizam Nawab, and in ‘Andhra Vani’
magazine.

From 1933 to 1940, he compiled his great poems like Mahaprasthanam,
Jagannath’s chariot wheels, and Roaring Russia and published them as a
book called Mahaprasthanam. It was a book that changed the phase
and direction of Telugu literature. He returned to Madras in 1947 and
settled there. He continued his writing career and wrote many more great
works. He wrote songs and lyrics for many movies. He adopted a girl in
1949 due to childlessness. In 1956, he married Saroja for the second
time. He had three daughters and one son by his second wife. Sri Sri
actively campaigned on behalf of the Communists in the general elections
of 1955. In 1969, when the separate Telangana movement was at its
height, Sri Sri Vavilala Gopalakrishnaiah held a demonstration in
Khammam, calling for unity. He travelled many times to different countries.
He received many awards. ‘Kendra Sahitya Akademi Award’ and
the First “Raja Lakshmi Foundation Award” is some of these. He
served as the president of the Abhyudaya Writers’ Association (Arasam).
In 1970, his Shastipurthi Utsav was held in Visakhapatnam. It was on
that occasion that the Revolutionary Writers’ Association (Virasam) was
formed under his chairmanship. Sri Sri died on 15" June 1983, after
suffering from cancer for some time.

3.14.2 Literary Works of Sri Sri:

Sri Sri started his writing career in his seventh year. His first
book of poems was published in the eighth year. In 1928, at the age of
18, he published a volume of poetry titled “Prabhava”. He wrote this
work in the traditional way. In the later period, he wrote poetry in
vernacular prose. Mahaprasthanam Kavyam was published for the first
time in 1950. It is a collection of poems like ‘Mahaprasthanam,
Jagannath’s Rathachakras, Batasari, Bhikshuvarshiyasi’, etc. This
poem stands at the highest place in modern Telugu literature and makes
Sri Sri a great poet. Later he wrote ‘Maroprasthanam, Khadga
Srishti’, poetry collections, Charamaratri, a collection of stories, and
radio dramas. Mahaprasthanam was printed in Sri Sri’s own handwriting
and published in London in 1981. Mahaprasthanam is one of his famous
poems. This book was written to inform people of other languages in
India especially. His works are reflected Marxism, but he himself
expressed that he don’t know about Marxism. Sri Sri is the name given
to rhyme and fun. He showed innovation in his sound experiment. He
edited a column titled Questions and Answers (Praja) in ‘Pragathi

Weekly’. It is a title that answers the reader’s questions. The title with
quatrains and funs became very popular.
3.14.3 Service to Telugu Cinema:

As he was in Madras and a modern poet, he was well known to
the filmmakers. He wrote the lyrics and songs for the first Telugu dubbed
movie ‘Aahuti’. He worked for Hindi and Kannada dubbing films. He
wrote 12 songs in one day in Mysore. This is a record in the world of
cinema. He established himselfas a dubbing writer and song writer. He
wrote songs and lyrics for dubbed movies. He also wrote songs for
ordinary films. He wrote all kinds of songs, especially passionate and
exciting ones.

He has composed many movie songs that have thrilled Telugu
people. “Telugu Veera Levara,” written by him for the movie Alluri
Sita Rama Raju, is one of Sri Sri’s hits. He himself made a dubbing film
called ‘Chevilo Rahasyam’, but he lost financially. Later, he established
a company called ‘Ushasree Pictures’ and tried to make a film called
‘Rukmini Kalyanam’, but it was not successful. Along with his second
wife, Saroja, he wrote the lyrics for the films. There are many conflicting
feelings and strange conflicts in Sri Sri’s personality. The rivalry between
Sri Sri and Jnana Peetha awardee Vishwanatha Satyanarayana
is famous.

Sri Sri’s words used to have some kind of sabdalankaras (Sound
Phonetics) and witticisms. Sri Sri is famous for his witty dialogue and use
of puns. His chants are spreading in many parts of the world. Biographer,
famous writer, and linguist Budaraju Radhakrishna wrote: “There is
no other poet in the history of Telugu literature of at least a thousand
years who has defined poetry in this way, described it so poetically,
and created such a wonderful creation. This puts him at the pinnacle
of literature”.

4.0. Learning Outcomes : After reading this unit, the student should be
able to
> Know the National Movement in Andhra and the factors that led to
the outbreak of the movement.
> Understand the causes of the rise of the Vandemataram and Home
Rule movements and important incidents.
> Learn about the non-cooperation, civil disobedience, and Quit India
movements.
> Understand the Rampa Rebellion and the role of Alluri Sita Rama
Raju.
> Learn a comprehensive understanding of the Andhra Freedom
Movement’s history.
4.1. Introduction
The Indian National Movement was undoubtedly one of the
biggest mass movements modern society has ever seen. It was a
movement that galvanised millions of people of all classes and ideologies
into political action. The Andhra Pradesh region also played a remarkable
role in the nation’s struggle for political emancipation from British
imperialism. The sense of nationalism that developed among the Indians
in the 19th century took vivid forms in the 20th century. After the Sepoy
Mutiny of 1857, the British policies of racial superiority, alienation, and
others towards the Indians spoiled the relationship between the rulers
and the ruled. A sense of nationalism gradually emerged among the Indians
due to various reasons, as stated below.
4.2. Factors for the Growth of Political Consciousness in Andhra
Many factors contributed to the political consciousness in Andhra.
They are
4.2.1. The Exploitative British Rule
Though India got united under English rule, the colonial British
masters exploited the resources of India for the benefit of their country.
All three sectors of the economy suffered. The primary sector, namely
agriculture, got affected by the exorbitant demand for land tax in the
Permanent Settlement and Ryotwari systems introduced in Andhra. The
vagaries of monsoons, frequent famines, the commercialization of
agriculture, and rural indebtedness broke the backs of the agriculturists.
The secondary sector, namely the industrial sector, was no better. The
small-scale and cottage industries shattered as they were unable to

--- PAGE 194 ---
compete with the cheap, mill-made products of England distributed by a
network of railways and roadways. In the third sector, which is the
exchange sector, Indian merchants and traders lost their market. The
wealth of India was drained into England. Further, the British adopted
racial discrimination in India. The educated Andhras could not get any
employment. Hence, the Andhra people hated the misrule of the English.
4.2.2. Western Education

The British introduced Western education in India with English
as the medium of instruction for imperial needs. Under British rule, many
educational institutions were established. The Christian missionaries
started schools and colleges, which reaped the benefits of the grant-in-
aid scheme implemented by the Wood’s Dispatch. The government
established Madras University in 1857, and higher education in Andhra
Pradesh made much progress with it. The Madras government and
Christian missionaries founded colleges at Machilipatnam, Rajahmundry,
Visakhapatnam, Guntur, Kakinada, and other important towns in the
Andhra region. In spite of the defects of western education, it brought
new ideas to young minds by studying western intellectuals and liberation
movements. With modern knowledge, they were able to analyse the
imperialist and exploitative nature of British rule. Soon they organised
anti-imperialistic political movements for the liberation of India from alien
tule.
4.2.3. Influence of the Cultural Renaissance Movement

Due to the criticism of the Christian missionaries on Hinduism
and the influence of western education, some socio-religious movements
like the Brahma Samaj, Arya Samaj, Theosophical Society, and
Ramakrishna Mission were founded in the 19" century. They started
their branches in Andhra Pradesh. Social reformers like Kandukuri
Veeresalingam, Gurajada Appa Rao, Raghupathi Venkataratnam Naidu,
etc. played a major role in enlightening the people of Andhra Pradesh.
4.2.4. Press and News Papers

The press was an important agency for spreading the spirit of
nationalism. There were as many as 20 newspapers circulated in the
Andhra region. Important among them were The Hindu, founded by
Subramania Aiyer, Andhra Prakasika, edited by Pardhasaradhi Naidu,
Vivekavardhini of Veerasalingam, and The Crescent of Gajula. Lakshmi
Narasu Chetty, Purushartha Pradhayani, Loka Ranjani, Sesirekha,

--- PAGE 196 ---
Goutami, Desabhimani, Sujana Manollasini, Krishna Patrika started by
Konda Venkatappaiah, and Andhra Patrika founded by Kasinadhuni
Nageswara Rao in 1908. These papers exposed and severely criticised
British policies and their exploitation. The vernacular newspapers
published the views of the intelligentsia with regard to political, economic,
social, religious, and other matters. The newspapers were devoted to
almost all branches of humanity, and some of them created whirlwinds in
the literary and political spheres. Thus, the press contributed to a great
extent to the political awakening of the people.

The cause of nationalism in India was also greatly furthered by
the emergence of national literature. A significant contribution in the field
of national literature was made by Bankim Chandra Chatterjee, the author
of Ananda Mutt, which contains the national hymn Vandemataram. During
the long struggle for freedom from 1905 to 1947, it was the rallying cry
of the patriotic Indians. It served as an inspiration, radiating the spirit of
resistance to foreign rule in all its forms, until India got freedom. In Telugu,
Chilakarnarti Lakshminarasimham took the lead in writing patriotic poetry,
stories, and novels of Rajput chivalry. Chilakamarti composed a popular
Telugu song during the visit of Bipin Chandra Pal, which runs as follows:

“India is a gentle milch cow,

And the starved calves are Indians.

The subtle cowherds muzzle them.

To snatch the entire store of milk”.

Chilakamarti was followed by several other eminent writers like
Guruzada Appa Rao, Rayaprolu Subba Rao, Duvvuri Rami Reddy,
Mangipudi Venkata Sarma, Tummala Sitarama Murty, and others. In
1910, Chilukuri Veerabhadra Rao published the first volume of Andhrula
Charitra. It instilled self-confidence among the Telugu people after
knowing about their past glory.

4.2.5. Development of Means of Communication

The colonial masters developed the means of communication in
the 19" century to cater to imperialistic needs, especially to transport the
raw materials used in the factories of England from the hinterland to the
ports, to carry the imported British goods from the ports to the interior,
and to facilitate troop movement. The trunk road that connected Madras
and Calcutta passed through the whole length of Andhra Pradesh from
south to north. The East Coast Railway that traversed through Andhra

was opened to traffic in 1899. They considerably enhanced the nationalistic
spirit among the people as it became easier for Telugus to travel and
share their feelings with fellow Indians.

4.2.6. Establishment of Public Associations

Some enlightened Indians and educated intelligentsia recognised
the need for public associations to redress the grievances of the people.
Gajula Lakshmi Narasu Chetty, a prominent merchant in Madras, started
the Madras Native Association in 1852. In 1852 and 1861, the
Association submitted petitions to the government, raising its voice against
the British and championing the cause of poor peasants and the religious
policy of the British. The Madras Mahajanasabha was started by
prominent Telugu people like Rangaiah Naidu and Anandacharyulu in
1884. The aim of the Madras Mahajana Sabha was to give proper
direction to the growing national consciousness among the people. Though
many prominent leaders thought of starting an all-India body, A.O. Hume,
a retired Indian civil servant, organised the first session of the Indian
National Congress at Bombay in December 1885, which was presided
over by W.C. Benerjee. Some prominent Telugu leaders like Rangaiah
Naidu, Anandacharyulu, and Nyapati Subba Rao As many as 21
delegates from Andhra Pradesh attended the second session held in
Calcutta in 1886. Anandacharyulu rose to the position of President of
the Congress by 1891.

The formation of the Indian National Congress gave a boost to
the district associations. Andhra leaders took the lead in forming district
associations in Andhra on the models of the National Congress. The first
district association to be formed in the Madras Presidency was the Krishna
District Association in 1892. The other district associations started were
Godavari in 1895, Vizagapatam in 1897, Guntur in 1904, Ganjam in
1906, Anantapur in 1907, Cuddapah, Nellore, and Kurnool in 1908,
and Chittor in 1911.

4.2.7. Impact of the Ruso-Japanese War

The defeat of Russia, the European giant, at the hands of Japan,
the Asian dwarf, in 1905 gave a great stimulus to the growth of nationalism
all over Asia. It helped the Indians cast away their inferiority complex
and regain self-confidence. Japan’s victory profoundly influenced the
Andhra people too. Adipudi Somanadha Rao wrote a history of Japan
in Telugu and dedicated it to the Raja of Munagala. On his part, the Raja

named his two sons Togo and Nogi after the famed military officers.
Veerabrahmam, a native of Machilipatnam, wrote a play entitled
Japaniyam. It should be noted that Japan became a role model for the
Telugus. During the Swadeshi movement, students were sent to Japan to
learn industrial arts.

4.2.8. Partition of Bengal-Vandemataram Movement

The partition of Bengal caused by Lord Curzon in 1905 fostered
the nationalist spirit among the Indians. Though Curzon announced that
partition of Bengal had been contemplated on administrative grounds, it
was deliberately meant to drive a wedge between Hindus and Muslims
and to weaken the national movement in Bengal. The agitation for the
annulment of Bengal was popularly known as the Vandemataram
Movement, as the Vandemataram song was widely used during the
course, almost like a war cry. Since then, it has become a formality to
greet by saying Vandemataram whenever two people meet. It became a
customary habit to also wear Vandemataram badges. The Swadeshi
Movement, the constructive side of the Vandemataram Movement, was
an integral part of the agitation as the leaders appealed to Indians to
boycott foreign goods and use native products. With the advent of the
Vandemataram movement, the era of moderate nationalism ended and
ushered in extremist nationalism. At the national level, the movement was
spearheaded by Lala Lajapathi Rai, Bala Gangadhara Tilak, and
Bipinchandra Pal, known in history as Lal, Bal, and Pal.

4.3. Vandemataram Movement in Andhra

In Andhra, Krishna Patrika played a significant role in spreading
the ideals of the Vandemataram movement and the cause of Swadeshi.
An organisation known as Balabharathi Samiti was formed at
Rajahmundry in 1907, and it began to work for the spread of the Swarayya
and Swadeshi movements. In Andhra, Kauta Srirama Sastri used to sing
the Vandemataram song passionately and make people excited.

4.3.1. Bipin Chandrapal Tour (April, 1907)

Bipinchandra Pal’s tour of Andhra towns in April 1907, arranged
by Mutnuri Krishna Rao, editor of Krishna Patrika, resulted in inculcating
burning faith in Swarajya and Swadeshi. Asa part of his tour, Pal visited
Andhra towns like Vizainagaram, Visakhapatnam, Kakinada,
Rajahmundry, and Bezwada and delivered a series of lectures. He
elaborated on the need for gaining Swarajya and the importance of the

Swadeshi. Due to the untiring efforts of the leaders of Andhra, the students
and youth gave up their fear of the British and expressed their latent
feelings by wearing Vandemataram badges and by greeting one another
with the Vandemataram slogan. As a consequence of all these intense
political activities, some major incidents occurred in Andhra too. They
were the Rajahmundry College incident, the Kakinada riot case, the
Kotappakonda incident, and the Tenali bomb case.

4.3.2. Rajamundry College Incident: (April 24", 1907)

Bipinchandra Pal’s lectures were the immediate cause of an
incident in Rajahmundry. Mark Hunter was the principal of the two
colleges at Rajahmundry, viz., the teacher training college and the
Rajahmundry Government Arts College. Harisarvothama Rao presented
an address on Bipanchandra Pal during his visit to Rajahmundry. Hunter
convened a joint session of both the colleges under his control on March
19, 1907, and ordered them not to utter the word Vandemataram or
wear such badges. He banned slogans or placards of Vandemataram on
the college campus. Hunter also ordered that no single college student
should be amember of BalaBharathi. Under the leadership of Gadicherla
Harisarvothama Rao, the students of the training college attended the
college wearing Vandemataram badges. Mark Hunter wanted to teach a
lesson to the students for defying his orders. Out ofa total of 222 students,
he barred 110 from taking examinations for two years. Harisarvothama
Rao, the leader of the students, was barred from employment as a teacher
in any government-aided institution. Another student, namely
Ramachandra Rao, was also declared ineligible for government jobs.
The Rajahmundry College incident marked the beginning of the students’
participation in the freedom struggle.

Later, Harisarvothama Rao became the editor of an English
magazine being published by Bodi Narayana Rao. Harisarvothamas
severely criticised the brutality of this English in the incidents that took
place in Tinnelveli. As a result, both the editor and publisher were sentenced
to prison in 1908. Harisarvotthama Rao and Bodi Narayana Rao became
famous as the first freedom fighters from Andhra to go to jail during the
Vandemataram movement.

4.3.3. Kakinada Riot Case (1907)

The Kakinada students who studied in Rajahmundry set up an

organisation called the Vandemataram Protective League and began to

propagate the ideals of the Vandemataram movement. In an incident that
took place on May 31, 1907, Captain Kemp, the Medical Officer of the
district, became enraged when a boy by the name of Kopalle Krishna
Rao cried Vandemataram. He got enraged and beat one of the boys by
the name of Kopalle Krishna Rao. He was badly hurt and left unattended.
Knowing this, the people were outraged and attacked the European
Club, where Kemp hid himself to protect them. On hearing this, J.A.
Cunning, the Collector, reached there with force and dispersed the crowd.
Knowing the grave situation, Kemp left Kakinada secretly. Later, the
police arrested 20 people. The accused were sentenced to a fine and
imprisonment. The Kakinada riots case was the first of its kind in Andhra.
4.3.4. Kotappakonda Incident (1909)

The Kotappakonda incident took place in 1909. Kotappakonda
is ahillock on which a temple of Siva is situated, with open plain ground
on all sides. Decorated cars known as Prabhas, drawn by bullocks to
Kotappakonda, were the norm, followed by the nearby villagers in all
pomp and splendour. On February 18, 1909, on Sivratri day, Gade
Chinnapa Reddy of Reddypalem brought a 60-foot Prabha to
Kotappakonda, drawn by his bullocks. That year, many people attended
the festival beyond their expectations. The police were excessively severe
in controlling the mass and started beating the people in the name of
maintaining discipline. The bulls of Chinnapa Reddy became unruly, and
the police opened fire on them and killed them. One of the constables
tried to attack Chinnappa Reddy when the latter threw him down on the
ground. The police thought that the constable had been killed.
Immediately, about five or six constables rushed to the spot, arrested
Chinnappa Reddy, and took him to the police station. The infuriated
mob demanded the release of Chinnappa Reddy, and when it was refused,
they set fire to the palmyra leaves of the shed that constituted the police
station. Even women were so infuriated that they started attacking the
police. The cries of Vandemataram filled the air, and the police, seeing
the infuriated condition of the mob, ran away. In the course of the riot, a
constable and a peon attached to the Salt Department were killed, and
several police officers were seriously injured. On the side of the people,
two Reddy boys died. The government arrested 45 people in this
connection, of whom 21 were convicted. Chinnappa Reddy was
sentenced to death, while four others were sentenced to life, and the

4.3.5.Tenali Bomb Case: (1909)

Another incident that took place in Andhra Pradesh during the
Vandemataram movement was the Tenali bomb case. In 1909, a Dalit
boy by the name of Chennugudu was killed in a bomb explosion at
Kancharla Palem. The bomb was planted by three patriots, namely
Chukkapalli Ramaiah, Kotamraju Venkatarayudu, and Lakkaraju
Basavayya. They were arrested on April 6, 1909. Chukkapalli Ramaiah
was sentenced to 10 years transportation, whereas Venkatarayudu and
Basavayya were sentenced to two years transportation.

4.4. Swadeshi Movement

Swadeshi was the constructive aspect of the Vandemataram
Movement. The Indian National Congress, which met in Calcutta in 1906,
framed the Swadeshi movement as a means of countering the economic
loss of Britain by encouraging indigenous industries and boycotting foreign
goods. Receiving the call of the National Congress, the Andhras jumped
into the Swadeshi movement. Swadeshi spread too many parts of the
country, including Andhra. Bipin Chandrapal’s visit to Andhra Pradesh in
1907 gave a good impetus to the Swadeshi movement.

The important features of it were

1. To boycott foreign goods and use Swadeshi goods.

2. To boycott the government educational institutions and establish

national institutions instead.

To boycott the British courts and set up panchayat courts.

To discard government jobs and government titles

5. Todisobey the government’s laws peacefully, non-payment of taxes,
etc.

A Swadeshi League was formed in Madras in 1906 to promote
the cause of the Swadeshi movement and encourage the sale of Swadeshi
goods. On behalf of it, Nyapati Subbarao and K. Venkata Ramana Rao
organised the Swadeshi movement campaign in Andhra Pradesh.
Vernacular presses like Deshamata, published in Rajahmundry, Krishna
Patrika, published in Machilipatnam, and Swarajya, published in
Vijayawada, significantly contributed to the propagation of the movement.
As aresult, organisations such as Balabharati Samiti in Rajahmundry,
Yuvajana Swarajya Samiti in Machilipatnam, Mahila Bharati Sangam in

Sattenapally, Lajapatirai Vidyabhivardhani Sangam in
Visakhapatnam,Prodduturu Taluk Sangham, Modukuru Grama Sangham,
etc. were established.

Several Swadeshi stores and national educational institutions
came into existence during this period. In starting the national educational
institutions at Rajahmundry, Gunneswara Rao, the Karnam of the place,
played a prominent part. Apart from founding the Bala Bharati Samiti, he
also started the Vandemataram Night School at Rajahmundry. The
National High School at Rajahmundry was also started during this period,
for which one Perraju donated a landed property. In Masulipatam, the
National College was started through the efforts of Mutnuri Krishna Rao.
In Guntur, the Swadeshi movement was led by Konda Venkattappayya
and Vinjamuri Bhavanachari. National institutions were also opened at
places like Ongole, Cuddapah, etc. In Nellore, a Swadeshi society was
started in 1908 by Mahankali Srinivasa Sastrulu, and a night school was
also started in the house of Vennalakanti Narasiah. The Swadeshi
movement spread among the merchant class and the enlightened public
too. Several leading merchants in Rajahmundry responded to the
Swadeshi movement by agreeing to sell only Swadeshi goods. In
Cuddapah, a Swadeshi cloth shop was started. Swadeshi fairs (Santalu)
were conducted to encourage the use and sale of Swadeshi goods.
The boycott of foreign goods and the use of native products resulted in
the necessity of establishing native industries. Bhavanachari of Guntur
suggested the idea of sending young men to Japan for industrial training
in order to start industries at home after their return. The appeals of the
leaders to the patriotic elite to send the young men at their expense to
Japan were properly responded to. Certain organisations also came
forward to fund such people. Malladi Venkata Subba Rao of Kakinada
was sent to Japan in 1906 to learn pencil making. S. Rama Rao, a student
of Bellary, was sent in 1906 to learn glass and watch making.Mamidi
Devendram of Rajahmundry was sent to learn electroplating and its
ancillary industries.

The Swadeshi movement also touched literature and drama.
Several books in Telugu were written during this period in support of the
Swadeshi movement. Attili Suryanarayana wrote Hindu Daridryamu, and
Mangapudi Venkata Sarma and Venkata Subbarayudu authored Bharata
Matru Satakamu. The people of Krishna district promoted cultural

institutions like the National Theatre to instill nationalism through plays.
The Vandemataram Movement in Andhra Pradesh ceased with the
annulment of the partition of Bengal in 1911. However, the movement
played a major role in creating a sense of nationalism among the Andhras
in an unprecedented manner. Thus, between 1905 and 1911, Andhra
people were in the grip of Vandemataram spirit, which accelerated the
process of anti-British sentiment.

4.5. Home Rule Movement

There was a comparative lull in political activity in India after the
annulment of the Partition of Bengal and the shifting of the capital from
Calcutta to Delhi. Another important stage in Indians fight for freedom
was the Home Rule Movement. During this period, political leaders
started constitutional agitation against the British government, demanding
self-rule. The First World War began on August 14, 1914. Britain
depended on her colonies for material help during the war. Above all, the
British relied on the vast Indian army and Indian material resources. The
British Government, in principle, expressed that her goal was responsible
government for India. Annie Besant, the leader of the Theosophical
Society, who had joined the Indian National Congress earlier, saw India’s
opportunity in England’s need. She started the Home Rule Movement in
1916.

There emerged two home rule leagues, not in opposition but in
close cooperation with each other. One was started by Bala Gangadhara
Tilak, and another, as stated earlier, by Annie Besant. Tilak confined his
League’s activities to Maharastra, Central Province, and Berar, whereas
Annie Besant covered the rest of India. The object of the Home Rule
Movement was to attain self-government for India as an integral part of
the British Empire by all constitutional means and to educate and organise
public opinion in the country towards the attainment of the same. Annie
Besant carried on intense propaganda for home rule through two of her
journals, The New India and The Common Wheel.

The Theosophical Society branches in the Andhra region prepared
the ground for the Home Rule Movement. The Andhra people
overwhelmingly supported the home rule movement. The Andhra Branch
of the Home Rule League was formed with Gadicherla Harisarvothama
Rao as its secretary. Prominent leaders of Andhra Pradesh like Konda
Venkatappayya, Unnava Lakshmi Narayana, and Gollapudi Sitarama

Sastry joined the League. All these leaders strove hard to educate the
masses during the course of the Home Rule movement. Pamphlets were
released to create public opinion in favour of home rule in the Telugu
districts. Important of them were Paramoddesamu (The Ideal of Home
Rule), Swatantra Varadana Patramu (The Magna Carta of India),
Swarajya Sabdamu-Bhavamu (Swaraj, the word and meaning),
Swarajyamu Korutaku Karanamu (Reason for Home Rule), and
Nootana Haindava Matru Geethamu (Modern Hindu National Hymn).
Besant toured Andhra towns for the propagation of home rule ideals.
She addressed meetings at Kakinada, Rajahmundry, Eluru, and Chittoor
on home rule and explained the various aspects of the movement in a
convincing manner. By the middle of 1917, as many as fifty-two Home
Rule League branches were functioning satisfactorily in Andhra districts.
Watching the momentum of the Home Rule Movement, the government
resorted to oppressive measures. Besant was barred from entering several
provinces of British India, and those orders were resented by the Indians.
The activities of Annie Besant alarmed the government. On June 16,
1917, the Governor of Madras, Lord Pentland, ordered the internment
of Besant and her two colleagues, Wadia and Arundale. The repressive
measures hardened the attitude of the national leaders and strengthened
their resolve to resist the government. The action of the Madras
government added strength to the movement. Meetings protesting against
the internment were held at Tenali, Bapatla, and Guntur. Slowly, home
rule became a mantra in every village. When pressure was mounting
against the government, Besant and her colleagues were released on
September 17, 1917. Thus, the Home Rule movement prepared the
people with the rightful methods of how to face the British Government
and the organising agitation methods, and as a result, the people actively
participated in the movements that were launched in due course of time.
4.6. Entry of Mahatma into Indian Politics

The year 1919 was important in the freedom struggle of India,
as it marked the entry of Mahatma Gandhi into Indian politics. From
then on, Gandhi dominated the political scene until the country attained
independence in 1947. The Indian National Congress had until then
confined itself mostly to the educated middle class. But when Gandhi
entered the scene, the Congress attained the stature ofa fighting force
with a mass base. Gandhi earned a great name for successfully leading

Satyagraha movements in South Africa and India, at Champaran and
Kheda for the peasants and Ahmadabad for the mill workers.

Gandhi reacted sharply to the Rowlatt Bills passed in the first week of
March 1919. Gandhi asked the country to observe April 6, 1919, asa
day of fasting, prayer, and national humiliation against the Rowlatt Bills
and to observe a complete hartal. The Andhra people responded to
Gandhi’s call by observing fasting, a peaceful hartal, and holding public
meetings on April 6 in all important towns. The incidents in Punjab added
fuel to the fire. General Dyer fired at a peaceful meeting at Jallianwala
Bagh in Amritsar on April 13, 1919, killing nearly 400 people and injuring
thousands. After the Jallianwala Bagh massacre, martial law was
proclaimed in several cities in Punjab. The Guntur Bar Association
demanded the impeachment of General Dyer, who was responsible for
the Jallianwala Bagh atrocities. Meanwhile, the Ali brothers started the
Khilafat Movement as a protest against the treatment meted out to the
Caliph, the religious head of the Muslims of the world, after the First
World War. The Jallianwala Bagh tragedy and the Khilafat question
brought the Hindus and the Muslims together in their opposition to the
British. Gandhi wished to support the Khilafat Movement to get the
support of Muslims for the ongoing national movement.

4.7. Non-Cooperation Movement (1920-1922)

In the backdrop of the Punjab wrongs, the Khilafat, and the
dissatisfaction generated by the Montagu-Chelmsford Reforms of 1919,
the Special Session of the Congress met at Calcutta in 1920. It passed
the non-cooperation resolution moved by Mahatma Gandhi. In the
programme of non-cooperation were included the surrender of titles,
honorary offices, and nominated seats on local bodies; the boycott of
British courts by lawyers and litigants; the establishment of private
arbitration courts; the boycott of elections that were to be held soon;
and the boycott of foreign goods. Adhering to the general pattern of the
1921-22 non-cooperation movement, the movement in Andhra also

followed the following:

1. Boycott of elections

2. Surrender of titles and honorary offices

3. Boycott of educational institutions

4. Boycott of law courts

5. Extension of non-cooperation to municipalities

--- PAGE 216 ---
6. Defiance of forest rules

7. Encouragement of Swadeshi and boycott of foreign cloth

8. Promotion of temperance by advocating the prohibition of intoxicating
liquors,

Resignation of village officers, and last but not least,

10. The no-tax campaign In Andhra Pradesh, Unnava Laxminarayana,
Gollapudi Sitarama Sastry, Narasimha Charyulu, N.V.L. Narasimha
Rao, and Polisetty Hanumaiah Gupta gave up their legal practises.
Konda Venkatappayya, Bhupathiraju Venkatapati Raju, C.
Narasimha Raju, and Narasimha Iyyer resigned for membership in
the Legislative Council. Educational institutions were also boycotted,
and student demonstrations were arranged at every centre. The
boycott of foreign clothing was also taken up with great zeal by
Andhra Pradesh. Some of the Congress workers, mostly young
student volunteers, used to go about the countryside, asking people
to give up their foreign clothes and wear Swadeshi clothes. On
account of the activities of temperance workers, the anti-drink
campaign made a tremendous impact in Guntur, Nellore, Bellary,
Anantapur, Kurnool, and the Godavari districts.

The All India Congress Committee met at Bezawada on March
31 and April 1, 1921. It had a great impact on the pace of the freedom
struggle in Andhra Pradesh. At this session, it was decided to collect one
crore rupees towards the Tilak Swaraj Fund, enlist one crore Congress
members, and introduce twenty lakh Charkas. It was in this meeting
that Gandhiji approved the design of the Congress tricolour, designed by
Pingali Venkaiah, with some changes. Later, it became the national flag
of India, with some more changes.

After the Vijayawada Congress meeting, Gandhiji toured
Kakinada, Rajahmundry, Eluru, Machilipatnam, Guntur, Nellore, Bapatla,
Cheerala, and Vetapalem and encouraged people to participate in the
non-cooperation movement. Wherever Gandhi went, people came in
droves to visit him and make contributions to the Tilak Swaraj Fund. At
the behest of the leaders of Andhra Pradesh, women in large numbers
came out of their houses and gave all their gold ornaments to the Tilak
Swarayya Fund. The famous actress Yamina Poorna Tilakam offered her
jewels and everything to the Mahatma, renounced her profession, and
dedicated her life to the non-cooperation movement. Andhra districts,

--- PAGE 218 ---
especially Guntur District, became the laboratories of Gandhian techniques
of satyagraha. In this connection, mention should be made of the people
of Chirala and Perala, who waged an epic struggle against the imposed
decision of a municipality, the Palnad Forest Satyagraha, and the No-
Tax Campaign in the Pedanandipadu.

4.7.1. Chirala-Perala Movement

Chirala, a small town in the former Guntur district, earned
nationwide fame with its non-violent agitation during the Non-Cooperation
Movement, when a municipality was imposed by the Government of
Madras much against the wishes of the people. The boycott of the
municipality famed as the Chirala-Perala Struggle was marked by its
uniqueness not only in the history of Andhra but also in the history of the
freedom struggle in India.

The villages, namely Chirala, Old Chirala, Perala, Jandrapeta,
and Veeraraghavapet, were under the administration of a union called
the Chirala Union. After frequent attacks of the plague, the chairman of
the union and the sanitary officer examined the conditions and submitted
a proposal for the conversion of the union into a municipality to provide
better sanitary facilities. The taluq and district boards endorsed the
proposal. The Government of Madras constituted Chirala and Perala
into a municipality with effect from April 1, 1920, excluding the remaining
villages, which continued to be under a union. The Chirala Municipal
Council was constituted with the Revenue Divisional Officer as Chairman
and eleven nominated councillors to carry out the administration of the
municipality. When Chirala and Perala were made into municipalities,
the tax burden increased. The people had to pay about six to seven
times more than they were paying under the union, and they felt the taxation
was oppressive.

Duggirala Gopalakrishnayya, a native of Penugranchiprolu
entered the arena; he became the leader of the dissatisfied public.
Duggirala started the anti-municipal movement, linking it with the non-
cooperation movement that was ongoing in his own style with bhajans,
songs, and puranic renderings with his melodious tone. He appealed to
the people not to pay municipal taxes. The boycott of the municipality
and withholding of the payment of taxes were near total. He personally
met with the municipal councillors and requested that they resign their
memberships. In February 1921, all councillors resigned.

--- PAGE 220 ---
The government initiated steps to collect taxes by force and prosecuted
those who refused to pay taxes under the criminal procedure code. In
March 1921, twelve of those who refused to pay the taxes, including an
elderly lady by the name of Ravuri Alamelumangamma, were arrested
and sentenced to imprisonment. Ravuri Alamelumangamma, perhaps,
was the first woman arrested in the entire country to be sent to prison for
a political offence. Gandhi visited Chirala on April 6, 1921, as a part of
his tour of Andhra Pradesh, soon after the Bezawada Congress meeting,
and appreciated the popular agitation in Chirala.

Gopalakrishnayya put forward a plan to evacuate the
municipality’s area. The plan was that if people left the area and settled
outside the municipality for more than six months, they would forego
their vote in that area, and the population of the municipality would come
down below 15000, which would force the government to abolish the
municipality. He convinced everyone. The people evacuated the municipal
limits and constructed hundreds of thatched sheds to accommodate the
people outside Chirala and Perala. He named the village Ramnagar. The
evacuation started at midnight on April 25th, 1921, with the beat of drums
and cries of ‘Mahatma Ganghi ki jai’. 13,572 villagers out of a total
population of 15,326 evacuated their homes from the municipality. The
bulk of them were sheltered by the ‘parnasalas,” also known as
‘swarajyam pakalu,” built of bamboo and palmyra. The evacuated Chiral
and Perala villages fell into dilapidated condition with the mass exodus.
Gopalakrishnayya started a volunteer corps known as Ramadandu. He
instilled discipline and patriotism among the volunteers of Ramadandu.
With its smart, rigid discipline and patriotic fervour, Ramadandu impressed
the delegates who attended the Bezawada special session of the Indian
National Congress. The volunteers of Ramadandu played a prominent
part in the construction of huts, the digging of wells, and transfer work,
and they also became the guardians of law and order as a police force.
The Ramadandu took on the responsibility of protecting the huts from
fire and theft and avoiding disputes. Gopalakrishnayya kept up the morale
of the people with his sankirtans and bhajans.

The people of Chirala spent nearly eleven months in those palmyra
huts amidst sun and rain; despite threats and governmental persecutions,
they stayed at Ramnagar in total loyalty to the chief, Duggiralayya.
Gopalakrishnayya tried to establish an idealistic state based on the

--- PAGE 222 ---
concepts of swaraj and swadharma. He practically established and ran
a popular, idealistic parallel government. Gopalakrishnayya was the
president of it. There was a parliament with a couple hundred members
to manage affairs, with representatives from all the communities.
Gopalakrishnayya passed the necessary legislative enactments, issued
administrative orders, and acted as a judicial tribunal, deciding all disputes.
They established a panchyat court to dispense justice. Law and order
were under complete control. People in the ‘parnasalas’ opened libraries,
and in the new areas they continued their daily business, functions,
marriages, and festivals. For the success of a parallel government, it
needed a lot of funds. For this, Gopalakrishnayya, with his Ramadandu,
went round the villages and collected contributions, and many Congress
leaders donated money for the cause.

The success of the movement was immensely recognised by other
areas far and near. Many Congress leaders from Madras visited Chirala
and delivered speeches to keep the morale of the people alive. The
government took the movement seriously. The publicity bureau of the
government of Madras issued a statement flouting false allegations against
the people of Chirala and Perala. The government officials and the paid
chairman resorted to repressive measures and devised various methods
to break people’s determination. The revenue authorities levied a penal
cess on the pretext that the thatched houses of Ramnagar were pitched
on government lands. The inmates of about 200 huts were evicted, alleging
that they were encroachments on government poramboke lands, and
about 100 more hut owners were served with similar notices. The people
of Chirala refused to pay municipal taxes, and consequently, some were
prosecuted and fined. They refused even to pay the fines and underwent
imprisonment in default of payment of the fine. Their movable properties,
consisting of household utensils, were attached and offered for sale by
public auction, but as a result of the preaching of the non-co-operators,
the sale fetched no bids. The Chirala property was purchased for low
prices, mostly by reserved constables, office clerks, and other officials.
The Chirala-Perala issue was raised in the Madras Legislative Council.
The members who asked the government to abolish the municipality were
countered by Justice Party members. On November 17, 1921, a motion
for the abolition of Chirala Municipality was proposed. The council
debates clearly revealed that the government was strongly determined to
suppress the movement while continuing the municipality in Chirala.

--- PAGE 224 ---
Duggirala was busy collecting funds for the relief activities for the Chirala-
Peralamovement through his tours and delivering speeches in important
towns like Guntur, Ellore, Bezawada, and Ananthapur. He spoke on the
Chirala Perala Movement, apart from various other aspects, and appealed
to them to liberally contribute funds for his struggle. The fiery and sarcastic
speeches earned the ire of the police and the government.
Gopalakrishnayya delivered fiery speeches at Berhampur in September
1921, condemning the government for the continued enforcement of the
municipality in Chirala against the will of the people. Gopalakrishnayya
was arrested at the Berhampore railway station and sentenced to one
year of simple imprisonment.

In February 1922, Gandhiji suspended the Non-Cooperation
Movement. But the people of Chirala Perala continued the movement
and did not leave their new habitat. However, nearly 200 hunters were
burned to ashes in a fire accident in Ramnagar. No one from the provincial
leadership came forward to reconstruct the huts. Lack of adequate funds
and a leader were other causes for the failure of the movement. Further,
the evacuation considerably dislocated their economic lives. The
demoralised people were left with no alternative except to go back to
their old homes. The Chirala and Perala struggle came to an unsuccessful
end not only because Gopalakrishnayya was imprisoned, but also
because the people were coerced into submission by prosecutions,
threats, the burning of cottages, and inducements through diplomatic offers,
which all resulted in the return of the people to their old town without
achieving their main object, viz. the abolition of the municipality. They
became demoralised, went back upon their resolve, and accepted the
very municipality on which they had waged such a bitter struggle for so
long.

4.7.2. Forest Satyagraha

Palnad Taluqwas a dry eco zone in the Guntur District. As the
place abounded in forests, the people were heavily dependent upon the
forests for means of livelihood like grazing cattle, making agriculture
implements, collecting minor forest produce, and removing earth from
the forests. But most of the forests were declared “reserved” as per the
Forest Act of 1882. The people were barred from entering the reserved
forests. An exorbitant grazing tax called pullari was levied. The people
demanded a reduced grazing tax, the removal of the compounding fee, a

--- PAGE 226 ---
single permit for the entire forest of the district, etc. The non-cooperation
movement provided an opportunity for the people to oppose the
unpopular forest regulations.

The people of the Palnad taluq, particularly those belonging to
the villages of Macherla, Veldurti, Ottipalem, Srigiripadu, and
Rentachintala, came under the influence of the ongoing national movement.
On several occasions, they led large herds of cattle into the reserve forest
in contravention of the forest rules. When the government forest guards
tried to impound the cattle, the villagers used to go in organised parties
and rescue the cattle. At Nadipalem, when the forest guards wanted to
impound about 100 buffaloes, the villagers, led by a young woman,
attacked the ranger and his party and brought back the cattle. In
Srigiripadu, about 700 cattle were impounded. The enraged villagers
decided to organise a social boycott of the officials belonging to the
Forest and Revenue Departments. In Macherla, a social boycott of the
officials was organised on a wider scale. Shopkeepers refused to sell
any commodity to the officials, and the servicing communities, like
washermen and barbers, denied serving them. The social boycott was a
total success. The situation was so serious that Deputy Tahsildar could
not even get milk for his infant daughter. On receiving the reports of the
boycott, the Collector of the Guntur District visited the area. He was
also subjected to a social boycott. The collector could get neither supplies
nor conveyance for himself or for the superintendent of police. When he
wanted to make his orders known by the beat of drums, the village Munsif
stated that all the drums in the village were damaged.

The Congress Committee deputed Unnava Lakshminarayana and
Madabushi Vedantam Narasimhacharyulu to make inquiries about the
grievances of the people. The Collector summoned the two leaders to
appear before him and to show because why they should not furnish
security for good behaviour. The people took them to the collector’s
camp in procession on horseback with the beat of drums, the very drums
that the collector was unable to secure. When Lakshminarayana and
Narasimha Charyulu refused to furnish security, both of them were
arrested and sentenced to one year’s imprisonment. The people of the
district protested the arrests and expressed their resentment through all
peaceful means.

On September 23, 1921, the forest officials, assisted by the
armed reserve police, conducted a raid in Mutpur forest reserve, near
Minchalapadu, and captured 300 cattle. But the villagers, consisting of
200 to 300 men and women, tried to rescue the cattle by hurling stones
at the police guards and by shouting and whistling so that the cattle might
be frightened away. The police had to open fire and disperse the crowd,
during which their leader, Kanneganti Hanumanthu, and a few others
were shot dead. The next day, the collector and the superintendent of
police visited the village and arrested 28 men and 9 women. The death
of their leader, the large number of arrests, and the continued use of
force by the police demoralised the people. By March 1922, normalcy
was restored in Palnad. However, the sacrificing of three lives in
Minchalapadu in the police firing was the first of its kind in Andhra Pradesh
for the nationalist cause. Similarly, forest laws were defied at Kondur,
near Vijayawada. In the Rayachoti taluq of Cuddapah district, the
Satyagraha was extremely serious, and the Collector had to call the
punitive police to restore normalcy.

4.7.3. Pedanandipadu No-Tax Campaign

The Guntur district, and more particularly the Pedanandipadu
firka, achieved great name and fame in the annals of the freedom struggle
in Andhra Pradesh by carrying ona no-tax campaign even before Gandhiji
experimented with it in Bardoli. The Guntur District Congress Committee
met at Ponnur on January 12", 1922, under the leadership of Konda
Venkatappayya and Gollapudi Sitarama Sastry, and decided that January
kist should not be paid throughout the district except in some of the
firkas of Tenali, Guntur, Ongole, and Repalle taluqs. On account of the
efforts of the leaders, several village Munsifs (headmen) and Karanams
(accountants) from different places in Guntur district resigned. The leader
of the movement was Machiraju Ramamurthy, the Karanam of
Chandavolu. Machiraju Ramamurthy and Parvataneni Veeraiah
Chowdary, who spearheaded the no-tax campaign in Pedanandipadu
firka, toured villages and were responsible for the wholesome resignations
of the village officers in Pedanandipadu /firka of the Bapatla taluq.

The government did all it could to break the solidarity of the
village officers and withhold their resignations. The government took strong
measures to stifle the campaign when their efforts failed. The village officers
were informed that their resignations would not be accepted and that

those who did not withdraw their resignations would be dismissed from
service. They were also warned that the government contemplated
legislation by which the families of the dismissed officers would be deprived
of hereditary rights. The second measure contemplated was the quartering
of punitive police forces. The government tried to fill the vacancies that
arose on account of the resignations. Under the leadership of Parvataneni
Veeraiah Chowdary, popularly known as Andhra Sivaji, a volunteer corps
called Santisena was formed with a view to preparing the people for the
withholding of kist and instilling confidence in the agriculturists against
the government’s strong-arm tactics. When the people refused to pay
taxes, the government tried to seize and auction their property, but when
it tried to do so, it was found that the house numbers were wiped off so
that the government servants would not know to whom the house
belonged. Finding that even the auctioning of household articles had no
effect on the people, the government, in order to create fear among them,
wanted to seize their lands and cattle and sell and distribute them among
the depressed classes. But even these repressive measures by the
government did not deter the ryots. Soldiers with machine guns conducted
route marches in the various villages of Pedanandipadu Firka. Armoured
cars also appeared on the scene. The police force was considerably
increased. The government also resorted to the destruction and attachment
of both movable and immovable properties. The rules in force were
amended for this purpose. Land was brought to auction. The government
officials themselves had to take up the onerous task of measuring grain,
tying it up into bags, and writing attachment lists, which took considerable
time. Such was the effectiveness and efficiency of the non-co-operators
that no coolies were made available to carry the baggage.

The Andhra leaders were divided on the advisability of carrying
on the no-tax campaign. They also appointed a committee to look into
the agitation. The committee was afraid that the masses might ultimately
get out of control if the government were to employ inhuman oppression.
Hence, they wanted the movement to be suspended. While matters were
in such a state, Mahatma Gandhi himself wrote a letter to Konda
Venkatappayya, asking him to suspend the Pedanandipadu No-Tax
campaign till he tried a similar No-Tax campaign at Bardoli. It dampened
the enthusiasm of the people. Obeying the orders of Gandhiji, Andhra
Congress leaders called off the No-Tax Campaign.

4.7.4: The Rampa Rebellion

Another important episode of the freedom struggle in Andhra
was the rebellion of Alluri Sitarama Raju. Alluri Sitarama Raju adopted
violence as a means of driving away the British from India. It was popularly
called the Rampa Rebellion. The rebellions of Sita Rama Raju were
confined to the tribal region of East Godavari and the Visakhapatnam
districts of Andhra Pradesh. It lasted for a period of two years and five
months, i.e., from January 1922 to May 1924. The Rampa Rebellion
and its young hero, Sitarama Raju, have left behind a glowing memory in
the hearts of three crore Andhras of his heroism.

Rama Raju belonged to Mogallu village in the west Godavari
district. He was born on July 4, 1897, in a Kshatriya family. He acquired
knowledge in palmistry, astrology, and ayurvedic medicine. He was also
proficient in horse riding, shooting, throwing arrows, and spear-throwing
feats with a sword. He read the Ramayana, Mahabharata, and Bhagavad
Gita. He was proficient in Sanskrit, English, and Hindi. He was thoroughly
influenced by the prevailing political atmosphere.

Sitarama Raju went to Chittagong in July 1921 and participated
in the secret meetings of the revolutionary committees. After returning
from Chittagong, he toured the Gudem hills and tribal villages, preached
temperance among the people, and also asked them not to go to court
but to settle their disputes through the indigenous panchayats. Rumours
were ripe of Raju’s fituri (rebellion) in January 1922. So, he was allotted
30 acres of land at Pydiputli village and was kept under police surveillance.
However, he got permission to visit Nepal. Instead, he left for the tribal
areas and started the rebellion. Many reasons are attributed to the
outbreak of the Rampa Rebellion, such as the oppression of the local
taluq officer, Mr. Bastian, the Muttadari system, and the forest reservation
policy of the British Government. The people in the agency tracts were
dissatisfied with the restrictions placed on jungle clearance and podu
cultivation. The repressive policies of Bastian, the Deputy Tahsildar of
Gudem, and his corrupt overseer, Santanam Pillai, added to the
discontent. The dissatisfied Gam brothers acted as the able lieutenants
of Sitarama Raju. Gam Mallu Dora was dismissed from the office of the
village munsiff of Battapankalu, and his brother Gam Mallu Dora was
deprived of his lands. Another tribal leader, Yendu Padal, was disgruntled
with the British as he was not made the Muttadar of Peddavalasa. The

--- PAGE 234 ---
persecuted ex-Muttadar of Gudem, namely Muttadam Veeraiah Dora,
also joined in the rebellion. They played a considerable part in leading
the tribal people against the British.

The rebels under Raju adopted a policy of raiding police stations
to lay their hands on arms and ammunition. The first police station to be
attacked by 200 rebels was Chintapalli on August 22, 1922, and looted
for arms and ammunition. The police stations at Krishnadevipeta and
Rajavommangi were the next targets. Raju and his followers obtained
27 carbines, 38 bayonets, 20 swords, and hundreds of cartridges from
these raids. The government got reinforcements from various places.
The rebels under Rama Raju adopted the guerrilla method of fighting
with the English. The first encounter between the rebels and the police
took place at Onjeri Ghat on September 3. The police retreated as
Tremenhere, the commanding officer, was severely wounded. On
September 23, the rebels had another success at Damanapalli. In a fight
against the police, the rebels killed two police officers, namely Scot
Coward and Heyter, and four other policemen. The police withdrew,
leaving behind a great deal of ammunition and six 303 rifles.

The government sent special Malabar police forces to deal with
the situation. The government resumed the offensive against Raju after
the arrival of the Malabar police. A surprise attack was made on the
rebels on December 6 at Peddagaddapalem, where Sitarama Raju and
his men received, for the first time, a reverse. The government did not
know his movements for months, though he and his forces were passing
through the hilly regions. The government also proclaimed an award of
Rs. 1,500 for the capture of Raju, Rs. 1,000 each for the capture of the
Gam brothers and Veeraiah Dora, and Rs. 50 each for other rebels.

On September 18, Gam Mallu Dora was captured at Nadimpalli
village. Despite the loss, Raju resumed his raids and attacked Gangaraju
Madgole and Pedduru police stations. The police retaliated by opening
fire on them. Raju decided to attack the Gudem military camp on October
26, 1923, to procure arms. But the police offered stiff resistance, and
Raju had to make a retreat with several of his followers wounded and
one killed. The government sent two companies of Assam Rifles and
appointed Rutherford as the Special Commissioner in charge of operations
against Sitarama Raju. Rutherford created fear among the tribal’s by
issuing 238 warrants against the near relatives of the rebels. Raju

continued his movements as before. In an encounter on May 6, 1924,
near the Madderu River, a prominent associate of Raju, namely Pericherla
Suryanarayana, popularly known as Aggiraju, was captured. On May 7,
Rama Raju was surprised in one of his hideouts by Jameder Kanchu
Menon of the East Coast Special Police and was brought before Major
Goodall. According to the government report, Raju made an attempt to
escape, but he was shot dead. His body was cremated at Krishnadevipeta.
Later, on May 26, Padal was killed by two villagers, while on June 7",
Gantam Dora lost his life in a skirmish with the police. Thus, by the
middle of 1924, the Rampa Rebellion had come to an end.

4.8.Simon Commission

There was a comparative lull in political activity in India after the
suspension of the Non-Cooperation Movement in February 1922. In
1927, the British provided a chance to break the slumber and resurrect
political activity in India. As per the provisions of the Government of
India Act of 1919, a commission would be appointed and sent to India
after ten years to examine and review the effects of constitutional reforms
and to suggest the necessary changes. As promised, the British
Government appointed a statutory commission, popularly known as the
Simon Commission, to examine the state of constitutional reforms in India
and suggest needed changes. It was composed of seven members of the
British Parliament under the chairmanship of John Simon. All its members
were white men, and not even a single Indian was accommodated in it.
Hence, the Indian National Congress decided to boycott the Simon
Commission and organise demonstrations and observe hartal on the
day of the arrival of the Simon Commission in India. The commission
arrived in India on February 3, 1928 A.D. A nationwide hartal was
observed. There was a deserted look to almost all the places in India.
The Commission was greeted with black flags as a protest with the slogan
“Simon Go Back,” which reverberated in all the cities, towns, and even
villages.

In Andhra, Guntur and Ongole were selected for the commission’s
visit. The Commission arrived in Guntur on February 23, 1928. There
were hartals, processions, black flag demonstrations with placards
reading ‘Simon Go back,” and protest meetings were the order of the
day in Guntur. The students of Guntur belonging to various educational
institutions enthusiastically took part in the boycott programme. It is worth

recalling the heroic behaviour of Tanguturi Prakasam Panthulu when the
Simon Commission visited Madras. During the agitation against the
Commission, there was a police firing in which a person by the name of
Parthasaradhi was killed. Prakasam was proceeding with a batch of
followers towards the spot of firing. The police blocked his movement,
upon which he defiantly bared his chest and told them that “we are
determined to move on and have a look at the dead body of our comrade;
shoot me if you want”. The police were taken aback by his boldness and
courage and allowed him to proceed. The courageous behaviour of
Tanguturi Prakasam earned him the title Andhra Kesari.
4.9.Poorna Swaraj

Annoyed with the protests against the Simon Commission, the
British challenged India to prepare a constitution acceptable to all sections
of Indians. The Congress accepted the challenge and assigned the work
to Motilal Nehru. After working hard, the Nehru Report was prepared
in 1928. Citing the objections of Mohammad Ali Jinnah, the British
rejected the Nehru Report. After the denial of the Nehru Report by the
British, the Congress met at Lahore in 1929 on the banks of the River
Ravi under the leadership of Jawaharlal Nehru. The session was
momentous as the Indian National Congress Party had declared that
Poorna Swarajya, or complete independence, was the sole aim of the
freedom struggle. It was also decided to observe January 26 as Poorna
Swarajya Day every year. The ripples of the call could be seen even in
small towns and villages.
4.10.Salt Satyagraha

On March 12, 1930, Gandhi started the famous Dandi March
from his ashram at Sabarmati in Ahmadabad with 78 satyagrahis to
Dandi. They were joined by people as they proceeded. The procession
became longer and longer as more people joined the march. The March
reached Dandi on April 5. On April 6, Gandhi broke the salt law by
raising salt from the sea. The breaking of salt laws was a deliberate act of
defiance against the mighty British Government. It attracted enormous
publicity and attention from the entire country and even worldwide through
the press. At Darasana, 2,500 satyagrahis raided a salt depot under the
leadership of Sarojini Naidu. The police resorted to merciless repression.
The movement gained momentum by June 1930. Gandhi was arrested
on May 4 at 1.10. a.m. before he could offer satyagraha. He was taken
to Boriwal Station and then to Yerawada Jail.
