--- PAGE 240 ---
The Andhra Provincial Congress Working Committee met at
Guntur on March 29, 1930. The Andhra Provincial Working Committee
appointed Konda Venkatappayya as the Provincial Dictator of Andhra
Province. As per the suggestion of Gandhiji, it was decided to offer
satyagraha simultaneously at different centres in each district. Before
the actual course of action was initiated, training was given to satyagrahis
in the Congress sibirams (centres) to break the salt laws non-violently
at places like Pallipadu Ashram in Nellore District, Guntur, Bezawada,
Machilipatnam, Eluru in West Godavari, Sitanagaram Ashram in East
Godavari District, Vizainagaram in Visakhapatnam District, and Bellary
for the ceded districts. When the Satyagraha volunteers went marching
to the Sibirams, they were normally received with music and songs by
the local people. Meetings were held praising them for their patriotic
sense, wishing them all success in their venture, and appealing to the
youth at large to come forward and volunteer to serve the nation. It was
during this 1930 Salt Satyagraha movement that the famous lyric was
written. Veera Gandhamu Tecchinaramu Veerulevvaro Telupudi (Tell us
who the heroes are. We have come to anoint them.) of Tripuraneni
Ramaswamy Choudhary was composed. As the Rayalaseema had no
coastal region, other programmes like the anti-drink and Swadeshi
movements were successfully resorted to.

When the Salt Satyagraha gained momentum, the Gandhi-Irwin
Pact was signed on March 5, 1931. Congress discontinued the Civil
Disobedience Movement and participated in the Round Table Conference
as per the Pact. Slowly, the tempo waned, and the movement was unable
to regain momentum when the civil disobedience movement was
relaunched after the Round Table Conference. Mahatma Gandhi issued
a statement recommending the suspension of the Civil Disobedience
Movement after his release on May 8, 1933. The Congress suspended
the movementat first for six months and extended it for another six weeks.
Ultimately, mass civil disobedience was discontinued, and individual civil
disobedience was permitted.

After the three Round Table Conferences, the British Government
issued a White Paper in 1933, which became the basis of the Government
of India Act of 1935. Elections were held as per the Act in 1937, and
Congress Ministries were formed in seven out of eleven provinces. In
the Madras Presidency, Congress swept the elections. The popular

Congress Ministry was formed in Madras on July 14, 1937, with C.
Rajagopalachari as the Chief Minister. Andhra leaders like Tanguturi
Prakasam Panthulu, V.V. Giri, and Bezawada Gopal Reddy got ministerial
berths in the new government. The Congress ministries resigned when
the English unilaterally announced that India was at war with the Allies.
4.11: Quit India Movement

The Congress Working Committee met in 1940 and declared
that nothing short of complete independence was acceptable to the Indian
people and decided not to contribute in any way to the war efforts. The
British Government came out with proposals through the Viceroy in 1940
knownas the “August Offer’. But the Congress turned down the ‘August
Offer’ and insisted on the establishment of a provisional national
government immediately. After that, Gandhi started the Individual
Satyagraha on October 17, 1940, and it continued till the end of 1941.
Vinoba Bhave was the first to offer individual satyagraha at Pallanar village
near Wardha on October 17, 1940.

England sent Stafford Cripps in March 1942 with a draught
declaration to win over Indian support during its critical period. The Indian
National Congress as well as the Muslim League rejected the proposals
on various grounds. The failure of the Cripps Mission, the success of the
Japanese in Southeast Asia, and the presence of the Japanese forces at
the very gate of India brought about a great change in the attitude of the
Congress. The Congress Working Committee met in July 1942 and
passed the resolution calling upon Britain to withdraw from India.

The Quit India Movement, also known as the ‘August
Revolution,” was the last great campaign launched by Gandhi in 1942.
The AICC met in Bombay and adopted the historic Quit India Resolution
with the mantra “do or die’. On the next day, Gandhiji and other prominent
Congress leaders were arrested. However, the appeal of Gandhi evoked
a nationwide upsurge in which millions of people took part. The patriotic
emotion rose high when all the leaders were arrested in no time. The
masses directed their efforts towards the disruption of communications
and public utility services and organising strikes.

In Andhra, the Provincial Congress Committee had issued a
circular popularly known as the Kurnool Circular since the full text of the
circular was available in the Kurnool District Committee office, and it is
also popularly known as the Andhra Circular. Later, copies of the circular

--- PAGE 244 ---
issued by Kala Venkata Rao also came to the attention of the government
in Nellore, Krishna, and East Godavari Districts of Andhra Pradesh.
Copies of an identical programme were issued to some of the Tamil
Nadu Provincial Congress Committees. The Kurnool Circular formulated
a six-phase programme to lead the movement. They included disobeying
all prohibitory orders, preparing salt freely, boycotting educational
institutions, quitting government posts, organising labour strikes, picketing
shops selling foreign goods, toddy shops, etc., stopping the trains, travelling
without tickets, forest satyagraha, withdrawing all money from all the
banks, disconnecting telegraph and telephone wires, non-payment of all
taxes, hoisting Congress flags on all the government buildings, etc.

The Madras Government arrested important leaders of Andhra
at midnight on August 8, 1942. With the sudden removal of the leaders,
the people grew desperate and were left without any responsible person
to guide them. The whole movement was spontaneous, incidental, and
leaderless. Though all categories of people participated in the movement,
the youth and students took the lead with a burning passion and zeal.
Acts of sabotage like cutting telephone and telegraph wires, tampering
with railway tracks and road transport, and causing damage to railway
stations, police stations, offices where administrative work was carried
out, and to government property in general, formed part of the Quit
India Movement in Andhra Pradesh. The important events in connection
with the Quit India Movement in Andhra were as follows:

At Tenali, the crowd attacked the railway station and destroyed
the signal post. The crowd set fire to the railway station and the booking
office, along with books, records, and currency. The Puri passenger from
Madras was stopped by destroying the signals and assaulting the engine
driver. They burned five compartments of the Puri passenger after asking
passengers to detrain by using kerosene brought from the railway station.
The mob pelted stones at the police, resulting in injuries to some of the
police personnel. Later, the police opened fire, and six people were killed
and eleven injured. However, it was said that nine people were killed.
At Guntur, a crowd of 2,000 consisting mostly of students began to pelt
stones at the cars and at the police. To disperse the crowd, the police
resorted to lathi charges, but this produced little effect. A number of
police officers received injuries on account of stone-throwing. The police
opened fire on the mob. Though the official information mentions that

--- PAGE 246 ---
only two people died in the incident, the Andhra Patrika stated that four
people died and nine more were injured.

At Bhimavaram, 2,000 people attacked the Revenue Divisional
Office and broke open the office. They went and set fire to the office of
the divisional officer as well as the building of the Deputy Inspector of
Schools. From there, they went to the Collector’s Office and insisted
that the Collector resign from his office and also made him hold a Congress
flag. The Congress flag was hoisted at the Collector’s Office. The records
in the office were burned. From there, they proceeded towards the police
station and set fire to the police lines. The police opened fire, kill four
people. Then a section of the crowd marched towards the railway station
and set fire to a railway trolley, while others attacked the Sivaraopet
station and burned it down.

Extensive damage was done to the railways during the Quit India
Movement in Andhra. The people attacked the railway stations and, on
occasion, burned them. In fact, the Tenali incidents ignited these attacks.
At many places, the railroad records were deliberately burned. In many
incidents, the rails and fish plates were removed so as to sabotage the
railway network, causing derailments on occasion. At places with railway
signals, communication systems were disrupted. As a result, many trains
were running late, and many more got cancelled.

One of the programmes, as stated in the Andhra Circular, was
disrupting the lines of communication by cutting telephone and telegraph
wires. Obstructing the flow of information from one government office to
another was the main agenda of such deliberate acts. Many such incidents
were reported in Andhra Pradesh. Many cases of attacks on post offices
were reported in Andhra Pradesh during the Quit India movement, with
incidents of burning the offices, records, and postal bags. Many
government offices and assets became the target of attacks during the
Quit India Movements, damaging property and records.

The Ongole Bomb case was one of the important episodes of
the Quit India Movement in Andhra Pradesh. The brain behind the incident
was Chimakurthi Seshagiri Rao, belonging to the Prakasam District. A
major explosion to destroy the record room of the District Munsif Court
was planned at Ongole. That many incendiary bombs were bundled
together for that explosion. While manufacturing the bomb, due to some
problems with gelatin tissue placed on the acid bulb, it exploded. Three
people involved in the making of the bomb were injured.

--- PAGE 248 ---
Railway stations, telephone and telegraph lines, and other government
installations became the special targets of mob fury, bringing the
government to naught. Protest meetings, processions, boycotts, strikes,
hartals, and defying the government became the order of the day. In its
communiqué, the government stated that “the damage done was so
extensive as to make it incredible that it could have been perpetrated on
the spur of the movement without special implements and previous
preparation, and in many instances the manner in which it was done
displayed a great deal of technical knowledge’. The leaderless movement
resorted to a number of acts that deviated from Gandhi’s principle of
non-violence. Perhaps the people were convinced that radical and
revolutionary methods were inevitable to achieve freedom.

The Madras Government took stern measures to suppress the
Quit India Movement in Andhra Pradesh by employing various repressive
methods. There were a number of arrests of leaders, along with those
involved in sabotage and arson, which were put behind bars. Congress
and student and youth organisations like the National Students’
Organisation and the National Youth League were declared unlawful.
The police frequently resorted to lathi charges against the people to
disperse the mobs on occasions of meetings, processions, and attacks
on government properties. The police opened fire at three places in Andhra
Pradesh. At many places, Section 144 was clamped, prohibiting the
unlawful assembly of people at public places. A curfew was clamped at
Chirala, Ponnuru, Nidubrolu, and Bapatla, ranging from one to two
months. It was intimated to the public by Jom Tom that no one should
come out of their houses from 6 p.m. to 5 a.m. punitive fines were collected
from the supposedly erring places. Such fines were collected from seven
Andhra districts, namely Ananthapur, Cuddapah, West Godavari, Guntur,
Krishna, Kurnool, and Nellore, indicating the intensity of the movement.
The highest amount of Rs. 3, 21,681 was collected from Guntur, and
West Godavari was the second in the list with Rs. 2,48,285.

The Colonial government claimed that it had largely been able to
suppress the movement in India within a month and crush it completely
by the end of the year. For this, it arrested 60,000 people, detained
18,000 without trial, and resorted to firing 533 times, killing 940 and
injuring 1,630. These figures were bound to be far less than the actual
number of arrests and casualties. As per another official estimate, the

number of people killed was 1,028 and the number of wounded was
3,200. However, the unofficial figure of the dead was stated to be 25,000.
4.12. Declaration of Freedom

England became weak after the Second World War, even though
England was on the victorious side. In such a situation, the British
government felt that it would be difficult to keep India under its control
for long and decided to hand over the responsible government to the
Indians. Hence, the newly elected Atlee Government declared that a
Cabinet Mission would visit India to make recommendations regarding
constitutional reforms to be introduced in India. The mission envisaged
the establishment of a constituent assembly to frame the constitution as
well as an interim government. It consisted of Lord Pethic Lawrence, the
Secretary of State for India; Sir Stafford Cripps, President of the Board
of Trade; and A. V. Alexander, the First Lord of the Admiralty. The
Mission arrived on March 24, 1946. After extensive discussions with
the Congress and the Muslim League, the Cabinet Mission put forward
its own proposal on May 16, 1946. In 1946, an Indian government was
formed with Jawaharlal Nehru as its leader. The British Government
handed over all the departments, including defense, to the Indian
Government.

On March 22, 1947, Lord Mountbatten arrived as the last
Viceroy of India. He announced that power would be transferred from
British to Indian hands by June 1948. Lord Mountbatten entered into a
series of talks with the Congress and the Muslim League. In the end, he
came to the conclusion that partitioning the country was the only solution
to end the impasse. Mountbatten proposed the division of the country
and announced his June 3 plan. The Congress accepted the proposal in
view of the deteriorating law and order in the country due to communal
riots. Immediately after that, Mountbatten took steps for the partition of
the country into India and Pakistan. On August 15, 1947, India became
independent from British rule, and on that day, the Union Jack of the
British was hauled down, and in its place, Nehru hoisted the tri-colour
Indian National Flag. That brought an end to the fascinating saga of India’s
independence movement.

4.13. Conclusion

The Telugu area of Madras Presidency, which included Andhra

and Rayalaseema, played a prominent role in the Freedom Movement

of India. Though the region entered into political agitation in the first
decade of the 20" century, it made its mark in all the major movements.
Vandemataram became a mantra during the Partition of Bengal movement,
in which people belonging to all sections of society participated. The
Chirala Perala movement was unique in its own right, and a woman
became the first political prisoner during the movement. The effective
social boycott during the forest satyagraha, the no tax campaign in
Pedananadipadu even before that of Bardoli, and the preparation of the
future national flag were the other important episodes of the non-
cooperation movement in Andhra Pradesh. During the Salt Satyagraha
and Quit India Movement, the Andhra people contributed much to the
cause of the nation. The cult of bomb, as reported in Tenali and Ongole,
and the Rampa Rebellion were the testimonies of the violent path taken
by the Andhra people.

5.1 Introduction :

At the beginning of the modern era, the Telugu people were
divided under two different administrations. The East India Company
occupied the territories of coastal Andhra and Rayalaseema and made
those areas part of Madras presidency. The Telangana region remained
in the native state of the Nizam of Hyderabad. The Madras presidency
consisted of Tamil and Telugu-speaking people. The Andhra, who were
part of the Madras presidency, started the movement for a separate
Andhra state. The area of the Andhra Pradesh region was about 58
percent of Madras state. Telugu people make up about 40 percent of
their population. However, compared to the Tamils, the Telugus were
backward. Andhra Pradesh did not have a distinct identity in Madras
State. Andhra Pradesh did not have equal opportunities in education,
jobs, agriculture, or industry. Non-Andhras used to work as officials in
high schools and colleges in the Andhra region. Andhra people have
been appointed as headmasters, principals, and high school teachers in
colleges in Guntur, Machilipatnam, Bapatla, Rajahmundry,
Visakhapatnam, etc. By 1915, there were 31 colleges in Madras state,
while only eight were established in the Andhra region. There was not a
single engineering, medical, or law college in Andhra Pradesh. Even in
the construction of railways, Tamil regions were given priority, and thus
the Andhra region was very backward economically. The Tamilians
illtreated and insulted the Andhras. They were humiliated in all fields. The
British officials also encouraged the Tamil people and ignored the
Andhras. The northern Indians called them “Madrasis,” which lowered
the identity of the Andhra.

In the 19th century, social and cultural revival movements started
in Andhra Pradesh, and the Telugu people were awakened. Due to the
influence of the National Movement, political consciousness started
among the Telugu people. The Vandemataram and Swadeshi movements
inspired the Andhras to protect their culture, language, and literature. In
1910, the book ‘“Andhrula Charitra,” published by Vignanachandrika

--- PAGE 262 ---
Mandali, glorified the history and culture of the Andhras. It enhanced the
self-respect of the people of Andhra Pradesh. Therefore, the Andhra
people wanted a separate Andhra state to protect their interests. Some
opined that the demand for the separate state was a clash for employment.
But it is not true. Pattabhi Sitaramayya rightly said that the Andhra
Movement was a semi-national movement. In the history of India, the
Andhra people were the first to demand a linguistic state.

The modern education system has shaped public opinion towards
Andhrodyama. This feeling gradually gained momentum due to the
conferences of the district associations. The district associations are
actively working and organising activities like discussing local needs,
seeking solutions to local problems, and submitting petitions to the
authorities. Thus contributing to the awareness of political consciousness
among the Andhras, Krishna Zilla Sangam was started in 1892, the first
of which started the struggle mainly on local socio-economic issues.
Gradually, it introduced people to political issues and proved to be a
powerful organisation. Next to this, the Godavari District Conference
was held in Kakinada in 1895. Gradually, the rest of the districts also
organised annual conferences.

Imaginary Phase (1904-1922) :

The first phase was the initiation of the concept of the Andhra
Movement. A Young Men’s Literary Association was established in Guntur
between 1903 and 1904. Jonnavittula Gurunath is a prominent member
of it. He was appointed as a lecturer at Guntur Christian College and a
member of the Central Legislative Council, where the secretary of
Kurupam Raja worked. The concept of Andhrodyam started when
Jonnavittula Gurunatham, a member of the Young Men’s Literary
Association, wrote articles on the problems of the Telugu people in the
Hindu paper. Challa Seshagiri Rao, Gollapudi Sitarama Shastri, Unnava
Lakshminarayana, Duggirala Gopalakrishnaiah, etc. were other prominent
members of this association. In 1905, political consciousness started in
Andhra Pradesh with the Vandemataram Movement. As part of the
Swadeshi Movement, the Andhras wanted to preserve their history and
culture. The abolition of the partition of Bengal in 1911 as a result of the
Vande Mataram movement inspired the separate state movement among
the Andhras. As Biharis got a separate state, the aspiration for a separate
state for Andhra also got stronger.

Role of News Papers :

Journals and literature in Andhra Pradesh contributed to the
concept of the Andhra Movement. The people of Andhra were inspired
by “Andhrula Charitra”’, written by Chilukuri Veerabhadrarao. Vigana
Chandrika Mandali published a number of books to enlighten the Telugu
people. The newspapers also published articles and editorials supporting
the concept of Andhra. They also suggested some measures for the
formation ofa separate state. The Hindustan Review, the Modern Review
of Calcutta, and the London-based Review of Reviews also emphasised
the need for the Andhra movement. The Indian Patriot magazine from
Madras not only published articles in support of the movement but also
actively supported the Andhras in the campaign of the movement.

The newspapers that supported the Andhra Movement in its initial
stages were Deshabhimani, Darbar, Carlielian, Hithakarini, Navayuga,
and Andhra from Guntur; Krishna Patrika from Machilipatnam; Andhra
Patrika weekly from Bombay; United India Native States; Sampad
Abhyudaya; Sadhvi; and Mysore State from Mysore; Indian Patriot;
Sasilekha; Vaisya; Swadesimitran; Dravidian and Hindu Nation from
Madras; Dharma Prakashika; and Venkatesh Patrika from Tenali; Andhra
Advocate from Visakhapatnam; Desamatha; and Andhra Kesari from
Rajahmundry. The important newspapers that accelerated the Andhra
Movement by publishing articles and editorials in support of the movement
were Andhra Patrika, Krishna Patrika, Deshabhimani, Deshamata,
Darbaru, etc. Books like Andhra Charitra, published by Vigyan Chandrika
Mandal, inspired the Andhras. 1911, the Hindu paper published a series
of six articles on the topic “The present conditions of the Andhras’,
explaining the backward condition of the Andhras in the Madras state.
These articles also probed into the reasons for the backwardness of the
Andhras and advised them to summon confidence to consolidate the
concept of a separate Andhra state.

In 1911, Unnava Lakshminarayana, Jonnavittula Gurunatham,
Chatti Narasimharao, and Vemavarapu Ramadasu together prepared a
map of the Telugu regions. They combined all the Telugu-speaking areas
in the Madras state, the Nizam’s kingdom, and the Mysore kingdom to
create a portrait of Andhra Pradesh. It was expressed for the first time in
the Delhi Durbar held in 1911 that the Telugu-speaking people should
separate from the Madras state and form a separate administrative unit.
Newspapers like Andhra Kesari and Deshabhimani wanted to separate

--- PAGE 266 ---
Andhra from Madras, just as Bihar was separated from Bengal. There
was also an idea to send a delegation to the government for the
establishment of a separate Andhra Pradesh state.

A joint meeting of Krishna, Guntur, and Godavari districts was
held at Nidadavolu in May 1912 to discuss the formation of Andhra
Pradesh. The meeting was presided over by Vemavarapu Ramadasu. In
this meeting, the representatives of Guntur raised the issue of the separate
state of Andhra Pradesh. But the representatives of Godavari district did
not accept this view at first. But finally, it was agreed to organise an All
Andhra Conference for the formation of a separate Andhra state.

Unnavava Lakshminarayana, Jonnavittula Gurunatham, Challa
Seshagiri Rao, Konda Venkatappayya, Vinjamuri Bhavanachari, etc.
appointed a Standing Committee with Konda Venkatappayya as
Secretary to organise the All Andhra Conference and gather public
opinion. He published a book explaining the need for a separate state
along with Gurnaddham. There was a debate about the formation ofa
separate Andhra Pradesh, in which Pattabhi Sitaramayya argued positively
and Nyapathi Subbaarao argued against the cause. It was decided to
hold the first Andhra Mahasabha at Bapatla in 1913.

There were differences of opinion in the press about the formation
of a separate state. Many articles have been published in support and
opposition. The Tamil press questioned the capacity of the Andhra
Pradesh in forming an administrative setup. They also questioned how
the Andras could appoint the judges for the high court and members of
the executive council. They opined that if Andras were appointed to the
above posts, the dignity of the post would be decreased. This damaged
the self-respect of the Andhra people. The standing committee released
its manifesto in 1912. The manifesto is confined to non-conservational
issues like the establishment of English papers, a university for the Andhra
region, the appointment of Andhras in military services, etc. The separate
state concept was not in the manifesto, which was adopted in the Bapatla
session.

5.1.3: Propagation Phase (1912-1920) :

During this phase, the Andhra Mahasabha meetings were
organised to consolidate the popular opinion for a separate Andhra state.
Let’s discuss them briefly.

The First Session of the Andhra Maha Sabha: (Bapatla 1913) :

In the month of May 1913, the first Andhra Mahasabha was
held in Bapatla. About 800 representatives from the Telugu regions of

--- PAGE 268 ---
Madras state and observers from Nagpur, Warangal, and Hyderabad
attended this meeting. Baiya Narasimheswara Sharma, a member of the
Madras Legislative Council, presided over the meeting. In his presidential
address, he asked the Andhras to be loyal to the British government. He
opined that there are more disadvantages than advantages to forming a
separate state. Therefore, there should be a wide discussion on this topic.
There was a fierce debate on this issue in the subject committee and in
the open conference. In this meeting, Vemavarapu Ramadasu of Madras
proposed a separate Andhra state resolution. But leaders like Nyapati
Subbarao, Macharla Ramachandrarao, Ganti Venkataramaiah, etc.
opposed this resolution. It was agreed that the separate Andhra Pradesh
matter should be considered in later meetings and that public opinion
should be collected on the issue. After the meeting, the separate Andhra
issue was discussed in various district meetings. Guntur, Vijayawada,
Godavari, and Visakhapatnam districts passed resolutions in favour ofa
separate Andhra Pradesh state. Differences of opinion were expressed
in the Ganjam district meeting. Nellore district has concluded that the
time has not yet come for a separate Andhra Pradesh state. Seeded
districts did not show much interest in this movement. A delegation headed
by Konda Venkatappayya visited different parts of Andhra and
propagated the concept of special Andhra. Krishna Patrika and Andhra
Patrika propagated this concept. Pattabhi Sitaramaiah published articles
in the Hindu Patrika advocating the desire for a separate Andhra Pradesh.
The Second Session of the Andhra Maha Sabha: (Vijayawada, 1914)
The second Andhra Maha Sabha session was held at Vijayawada in
1914. The session was presided over by Nyapati Subba Rao. In his
presidential address, he did not mention the separate Andhra concept,
which did not satisfy the delegates. Mantha Suryanarayana Rao proposed
a resolution for the formation of a separate Andhra State with the areas
of Telugu-speaking people in the state of Madras. Vemavarapu Ramadasu
seconded the resolution. The majority of the delegates accepted the
resolution.
The Third Session of the Andhra Maha Sabha (Visakhapatnam, 1915) :
The third Andhra Maha Sabha session was held at
Visakhapatnam in 1915. The session was presided over by the Raja of
Panagal, Raja Rama Rayanimgar. He initially opposed the concept ofa
separate Andhra Pradesh. But he accepted to preside over the session,
and in his presidential address, he supported a separate Andhra Pradesh.

--- PAGE 270 ---
In this session, the resolution “to form a separate state with eleven districts
is necessary and eligible” was passed. Other important resolutions of the
session were that school education should be in Telugu and that the
speakers of the session should speak in Telugu. The principle of primary
education in the mother tongue was also welcomed by Tamil journals
like “Tamil Mitran.”
The Fourth Session of the Andhra Maha Sabha (Kakinada, 1916) :

The fourth Andhra Maha Sabha session was held in Kakinada in
1916. Mocharla Ramachandra Rao was the president of the session. In
this meeting, C.V. Narasimha Raju from Visakhapatnam made a proposal
for the formation ofa separate autonomous Andhra Pradesh with Telugu-
speaking districts. A resolution was accepted to request the government
for a separate Andhra state after the First World War. The delegates
from Nellore and Cuddapah opposed the resolution. The delegates from
Berhampur and Anantapur supported it. After the meeting, Bulusu Samba
Murthy met Tilak and conveyed the demands of the Andhra people. At
that moment, when the Andhras were demanding a separate state, the
British India Government announced that the youth of the Northern
Circulars of Andhra were not eligible for recruitment into the war army.
This enraged the people of Andhra Pradesh. The Andhra Mahasabha
protested the government’s decision and insisted that the government
cancel the order. The government was bound to cancel the order. This
was the first attempt by the Andhra Mahasabha to solve a problem with
the government. To involve the Telugu-speaking people living in Madras
city in this movement, an association named ‘Andhra Political
Organisation’ was formed under the presidentship of Bhaiya
Narasimheshwara Sharma in April 1917.
The Fifth Session of the Andhra Maha Sabha (Nellore, 1917) :

The fifth Andhra Maha Sabha session was held at Nellore in
1917. Konda Venkatappayya was the president of the session. In the
5th session, there was a severe debate and strong opposition over the
separate Andhra movement. The representatives of Rayalaseema
disagreed with the opinions of the Circar districts. The delegates from
Nellore district also supported Rayalaseema. Hence, the Rayalaseema
districts, along with Nellore, opposed a separate state. A.S. Krishna
Rao, Gutti Kesava Pillai, and Ekambar Ayyangar were among those
who strongly opposed the separate Andhra resolution. With the efforts
of Gadicharla Harisarvoththama Rao and B.N. Sarma, the resolution
was passed in the 5th session of the Andhra Mahasabha.

--- PAGE 272 ---
Andhra Mahasabha with Montague in 1917:

Although involved in the movement to separate Andhra Pradesh,
the Andhra people participated in the Indian National Movement and
played a significant role in national politics. P. Anandacharyulu was elected
president of the Indian National Congress session in 1891 at Nagpur.
They participated in every session of the Indian National Congress. They
also took part in the Vande Mataram and Home Rule movements with a
zeal for nationalism. The secretary of state for Indian affairs, Montague,
visited India in 1917 to propose some constitutional reforms and get the
support of Indians in the First World War. By 1917, there was a consensus
among the Andhras on the formation of a separate Andhra. A special
Andhra Mahasabha held at Vijayawada in the month of October 1917
decided that a delegation should meet Montague to explain the need for
this. At first, the Madras State Government did not give permission for
the meeting. But the persistence of the Andhra Pradesh members of the
Madras Legislature finally got permission. On December 17, 1917, a
delegation under the leadership of Nayapati Subbarao met Montague
and explained the need for the immediate formation ofa separate Andhra
Pradesh state. All districts are represented in this delegation. Important
among the members of the delegation were Konda Venkatappayya,
Kashinadhuni Nageswara Rao, Pattabhi Sitaramaiah, Gadicharla
Harisarvottam Rao, Oruganti Venkata Rangaiah, P. Pattabhirami Reddy,
Mocharla Ramachandra Rao, ete.

Formation of the Andhra Provincial Congress Committee
(January 22, 1918):

The Andhra demanded a separate circle of Congress for the
Andhra region in the sessions of the Indian National Congress many
times. Even though the Andhras attended on a large scale the sessions of
the Indian National Congress, their demand was rejected for no reason.
Annie Besent was sympathetic to the demands of the Andhra people.
The Calcutta Congress Session in 1917 was presided over by Annie
Besent. Finally, the Indian National Congress Session accepted the
demand to form a separate Andhra Provincial Congress Committee for
the Andhra region. According to the decision, the Andhra Provincial
Congress Committee was formed on January 22, 1918. Nyapathi Subba
Rao was the first president, and Konda Venkatappayya was the first
secretary. This was a milestone in the separate Andhra movement.

--- PAGE 274 ---
Resolution of B.N. Sarma (6" February, 1918) :

B.N. Sarma proposed a resolution in the Central Legislative
Council for the formation of a separate Andhra Pradesh, which was not
successful due to the lack of support from unofficial representatives.
Eminent people like Jinnah and Srinivasa Shastri also spoke against this
resolution. Surendranath Banerjee opined that the time has not come for
linguistic states. Te) Bahadur Sapru said that he would have supported
this resolution if it were limited to Andhra Pradesh alone. With this, the
resolution was defeated. Frustrated by the Andhras, the Krishna and
Visakha District Associations decided to intensify the movement of
linguistic states. The Montague-Chelmsford reforms were announced in
the month of July 1918, which left the Andhras disappointed as there
was no mention ofa separate Andhra state. A joint meeting of the Andhra
Mahasabha and the Andhra Provincial Congress Committee was held in
Guntur under the presidentship of Kashinadhuni Nageswara Rao in the
month of August 1918. In December 1918, Pattabhi Sitaramaiah proposed
organising an All India Conference for Linguistic States. But Congress
leaders did not agree. Mocharla Ramachandra Rao, on behalf of the
Andhra Sangam, submitted a petition to the British Government in London
for the formation of the Andhra State.

5.1.4. Hidden Phase during the National Movement :

From 1922 to 1935, the Andhras played a significant role in the
National Movement. Hence, the demand for a separate state became
passive to some extent. They postponed their demand for a separate
state until the achievement of Indian Independence for India.They tried
to incorporate the demand along with the demands of the national
movement. With their efforts, the Motilal Nehru Committee included the
linguistic states formula in the Nehru Report.

Important Events of this Period :

In 1922, Ramaiah Pantulu introduced a resolution in the Central
Legislative Council for the formation ofa separate Andhra Pradesh state.
But this resolution was withdrawn when the Home Department replied
that the time was not ready to consider the matter and that the Central
Government would consider it ifall the Andhra members of the Madras
Legislative Council demanded a separate state.

Formation of the Justice Party :

The Justice Party was formed in 1917 to counter Brahmin

supremacy in politics. It supported English rule from the beginning. In

--- PAGE 276 ---
1920, the Congress party did not participate in the Madras state elections.
The Justice Party participated in the elections and formed the Madras
State Cabinet. Although many prominent Andhras were also members
of the Justice Party, the Justice Party did not support a separate Andhra
movement.
Establishment of Andhra University :
Opinion between Andhra and Rayalaseema :
The Andhras, who were members of the Justice Party cabinet,
succeeded in establishing a university for the Andhra region. Andhra
University was established in Vijayawada in April 1926 with Kattamanchi
Ramalinga Reddy as its first vice chancellor. However, many controversies
arose among the people of Andhra and Rayalaseema over the choice of
the centre of the university. In the Andhra University Act, it was decided
that the administrative departments of the university should be established
in Vijayawada and the teaching departments should be started in
Rajahmundry. But the government found it inappropriate to be like this
and recommended to the university Senate that both be established in
Vijayawada. This led to controversy about the centre of the university.
Members of Rayalaseema in the Madras Legislative Council insisted
that the university should be in their area. Considering their opinion, the
Andhra University Senate accepted Anantapur as the centre of the
university. This was also accepted by the Select Committee of the Madras
Legislative Council. Chief Minister Dr.Subbaramaniyan proposed
Rajahmundry as a suitable place for the centre of the university. Finally,
the university was established in Visakhapatnam, and Rayalaseema districts
were kept under Madras University. With this, the Rayalaseema districts
expressed their anger towards the Circar districts. Many are of the opinion
that Dr. Subbarayan deliberately created a rift between Andhra and
Rayalaseema.
> In1927,55 resolutions were notified to the Madras Legislative
Council regarding the formation of a separate Andhra Pradesh state.
On March 14th, a resolution was passed in favour of the separate
Andhra Pradesh state. The Madras State Government forwarded
this resolution to the Central Government as usual. But the Madras
Government, in its submission to the Simon Commission, opposed
the formation of linguistic states.

>» When Gandhiji was going to London for the second Round Table
Conference, Pattabhi Sitaramaiah and Ayyadevara Kaleswara Rao

met Gandhiji and mentioned the formation of Andhra Pradesh. But
Gandhiji said that a separate Andhra Pradesh state could be formed
after independence.

> Andhra leaders V.V. Giri, Mocharla Ramachandrarao, Bobbili Raja,
and A.P. Patro met India’s Under Secretary of State, Lord Lutheran,
and asked him to include Andhra in the formation of new states. But
the British government did not take any action in the case of Andhra
Pradesh.

> In 1932, Bobbili Raja became the Chief Minister of Madras.
Gadicharla Harisarvothamrao proposed a resolution in the Madras
Legislative Council that the formation of Andhra Pradesh should take
place immediately. Bobbili Raja, who had so far strengthened the
separate Andhra movement, opposed the movement when he became
the chief minister. Enraged by this, the Andhra members proposed
several separate Andhra State Resolutions, which were passed by
the Madras Legislative Council in November 1933. The Madras
government sent a copy of this resolution to the Centre without any
recommendations.

» The Andhra Congress Swaraj Party was formed in 1934 to achieve
Andhra Pradesh, but it could not achieve its goal.

> During the civil disobedience movement, the Andhra movement faded
into obscurity. After the civil disobedience movement, the Session
of the Andhra Mahasabha in 1934 decided to set up the Andhra
Nidhi. A group of representatives from Andhra Pradesh went to
London in March 1935 to convince the British Government. The
government has not given any clear assurance.

5.1.5. Revolutionary Phase 1935-47 :

From 1935 onwards, as the National Movement became passive
for some time, the separate Andhra moment again came into the scene.
Efforts were made to demand a separate state. The most important was
the Sri Bagh Pact.

Sri Bagh Pact (1937) :

By 1935 the differences between the Andhra and Rayalaseema
leaders reached a peak. Many incidents are the reason for the differences.
Of them, the most important were the change of Andhra University to
Visakhapatnam and the lack of representation for the Rayalaseema leaders
in the ministry of the Madras Government formed in 1937. To lessen the
differences, the Rayalaseema leaders were invited to the Silver Jubilee

--- PAGE 280 ---
session of the Andhra Mahasabha held in October 1937 at Vijayawada.
H.Sita Ramireddy inaugurated the session, and Cuddapah Koti Reddy
presided over the session. The leaders of circar districts assured some
privileges to the Rayalaseema people. In 1937, an accord was signed by
the leaders of Andhra Pradesh and Rayalaseema, which was known as
the Sri Bagh Pact. As the pact was signed in the house of Bulusu Samba
Murthy in Madras, which was known as Sri Bagh, the pact is also known
as the Sri Bagh Pact.

Important aspects of the Sri Bagh Pact :

>

Andhra University: Two centers are to be developed at Waltair and
Anantapur under the supervision of Andhra University. Opportunities
for socio-cultural relations between Andhras should be created by
expanding cultural centres in different parts of Andhra. Colleges should
be established in places suitable for the respective subjects of study.
Irrigation: In order to protect the agro-economic interests of the
people of Rayalaseema, they should be given special priority in the
matter of irrigation for ten years or as long as necessary to enable
them to develop at par with those of the coastal districts. Special
attention should be paid to the use of Tungabhadra, Krishna, and
Penna river waters in the case of projects beneficial to these areas.
Legislature: All districts should have equal representation in terms of
seats in the Legislative Assembly. All major institutions of civic priority
should not be concentrated in one place. The capital and high court
should be established at different places in Rayalaseema and Circar
districts. An option is to be given to Rayalaseema to select either the
capital or high court, whichever they want to establish in the
Rayalaseema area. The university can continue where it is.

Resolutions:

>

1938 Konda Venkatappaaih proposed a resolution in the Madras
Legislative Assembly in 1938 for a separate Andhra Pradesh state.
The resolution was approved by the Madras legislative assembly.
In 1938, the Andhra Mahasabha session was held at Madras. It
was presided over by Sarvepalli Radha Krishnan. In the session, a
resolution was passed that Madras would be the capital of Andhra
Pradesh.

In 1941, the Andhra Mahasabha session was held at Visakhapatnam.
Sir Vijaya was the president of the session. Andhra Maha Sabha

collected 1 lakh rupees to provide relief measures in the Rayalaseema
area, which was hit by a severe famine.

> In1942, Sir Vijaya submitted representations to the Cripps mission
for the formation of a separate Andhra Pradesh state.

>» TheAndhra leaders Vavilala Gopala Krishnaiah, GV. Punnavayya
Sastry, Nadimpalli Venkata Lakshmi Narasimharao, and Tekumalla
Ramachandra Rao were successful in getting the attention of the
national leaders towards the separate Andhra movement.

Declaration of Jawaharlal Nehru in November 1947 :

Jawaharlal Nehru, the then prime minister of India, announced a
declaration in 1947. In his declaration, he supported the formation of
states on a linguistic basis. He also declared that the demand for the
separate state of Andhra Pradesh is a reasonable demand. And the
government is accepting the demand, and it will be included in the
constitution. With this declaration, the Andhras thought their demand would
be fulfilled.

5.2. Formation of Andhra State (194-530)
5.2.1. Introduction :

After independence, the people of Andhra hoped that their
demand for a separate state would be fulfilled. The announcement by
Jawaharlal Nehru gave strength to their demand. Tanguturi Prakasam
and Acharya N.G. Ranga met the home minister, Sardar Vallabhai Patel,
and requested him to look after the demands of the Andhras. On
November 27, 1947, Jawaharlal Nehru announced that the government
would reorganise the states on the basis of language. But the Constituent
Assembly announced that the states that were formed prior to
independence are only included in the constitution. Hence, the formation
of Andhra Pradesh could not be included in the constitution. This was
very shocking for the people of Andhra Pradesh. They were disappointed.
The Communist Party capitalised on the situation and tried to strengthen
the Andhra Pradesh area. In 1948, Nehru visited Visakhapatnam. The
opposition parties demanded a separate Andhra state. To satisfy the
feelings of Andras, Nehru announced the appointment of a committee to
look after the demand for linguistic states.

5.2.2. Dhar Commission :

Along with the Andhras, the Kannaries and Maharashtrians also
put pressure on the central government for separate states. On June 17,
1948, the Linguistic Provinces Committee was appointed by the

--- PAGE 284 ---
Constituent Assembly with a former judge of the Allahabad High Court,
S.K. Dhar, as president and Dr. Panna Lal and Jagat Narayanal as
members. The Dar Commission should submit a report on the formation
of new states, the determination of their boundaries, and the economic,
administrative, and political consequences of the formation of new states.
It means that the formation of Andhra Pradesh is linked to the formation
of other states.

In 1948, the Dhar Commission visited Madras and gathered the
views of people from all walks of life. Andhra Mahasabha representatives
demanded a separate state with all the Telugu-speaking areas.
Representatives of the Rayalaseema Mahasabha, M.N. Shastri, H.
Lingareddy, etc. wanted a separate Rayalaseema state with Madras as
the capital. Neelam Sanjiva Reddy said that it would be better to postpone
the principle of linguistic states for a few years. He requested that legal
protection be given to Rayalaseema if Andhra Pradesh is formed and the
Pact of SriBagh be included in the constitution. The Dhar Commission
submitted its report in December 1948. Taking the differences between
the leaders of Andhra Pradesh as a reason, the Dhar Commission said
that the creation of linguistic states is a hindrance to national unity and the
welfare of the country. It suggested that the restructuring of states should
be done only on the basis of administrative convenience. As soon as the
Andhras heard the contents of this report, they were outraged. To satisfy
Andhra Pradesh, the Congress appointed a three-member committee
on behalf of the Congress to look into the issue of the restructuring of
linguistic states in the 1948 National Congress Session. Jawahar Lal
Nehru, Vallabhbhai Patel, and Pattabhi Sitaramaiah were members of
this committee. The committee is named the JVP Committee after their
initials.

5.2.3. JVP Report :

The JVP Committee submitted its report to the Congress
Working Committee in April 1949 and suggested that the formation of
linguistic states should be postponed for some time. It stipulated that the
Andhra state would be formed if the Andhras gave up their desire for
Madras as their capital. The then Union Minister T.T. Krishnamachari
added that the consent of the neighbouring states was also necessary for
the formation of Andhra Pradesh. With these reports, rifts and group
politics strengthened in Andhra Pradesh. Andhra Pradesh Congress
demanded the formation of Andhra Pradesh with half of Madras town.

The Madras state government and the Tamil Nadu State Congress
Committee agreed to form Andhra Pradesh, according to the JVP report.
The Congress working committee also recommended the government
form a separate Andhra Pradesh state without Madras city. The Andhra
Mahasabha demanded the formation of Andhra Pradesh by January 26,
1950. From 1946 to 1953, the leaders of Andhra Pradesh formed groups,
and group politics dominated the cause ofa separate state. There were
clashes and differences between the leaders of Andhra Pradesh in the
Madras assembly. The prominent rivalry was between Tanguturi
Prakasam and Pattabhi Sitaramayya. The other important popular group
was N.G. Ranga versus Neelam Sanjeeva Reddy. The group politics
delayed the process of formation of Andhra.

5.2.4. Partition Committee :

The Madras State Government appointed a partition committee
to look after the formation ofa separate state. Kumaraswamy Raja, the
then chief minister of Madras State, was the chairman of the committee.
Tanguturi Prakasam, Kala Venkatrao, Bejawada Gopala Reddy, and
Neelam Sanjeeva Reddy were the members of the committee that
represented the Andhra region. The members from the non-Andhra
region were T.T. Krishnamachari, Bhaktavatsalam, and Madhava Menon.
Prakasam proposed that the Government of Andhra, the Legislature,
and the High Court of Andhra had to work from Madras City until the
new capital and the seat of the High Court were established in the Andhra
region. The non-Andhra members of the committee did not accept this
proposal. They argued that Andhra should establish the provisional capital
within the territorial limits of Andhra province. The government, the
legislature, and the judiciary should work from the provisional capital.
They agreed to establish some of the offices of Andhra Pradesh in Madras
City for a temporary period. Then Prakasam sent a decent note on the
difference of opinion on issues like temporary capital and the division of
assets. The Central Government took this opportunity to postpone the
establishment of a separate Andhra Pradesh state.

5.2.5. Hunger strike of Swami Sitaram (15.8.1951 to 19.9.51) :

The Constitution of India came into force in 1950. The proposal
for a separate Andhra Pradesh state was not included in the constitution.
The leaders of the Andhra Congress Committee were involved in internal
clashes and group politics. In April 1951, elections were conducted for
the post of president of the Andhra Congress Committee. In this election,

Pattabhi Sitaramayya supported Neelam Sanjeeva Reddy as a candidate.
At the same time, Prakasam supported N.G. Ranga. Sanjeeva Reddy
won the election with a majority of five votes. As a result, Prakasam and
N.G. Ranga left the Congress Party and founded the ‘Kisan Mazdoor
Party’. The leaders at the national level also made different statements
on the issue. Swamy Sitaram, a follower of Gandhian ideology, decided
to observe a hunger strike to achieve a separate state. He started fasting
unto death on August 15, 1951. Sitaram announced that the Andhra
state should be formed by October 2, 1951, and that Madras city should
be decided within three years; until then, neither the Andhras nor the
Tamils should claim power over that city. Nehru criticised Sitaram’s hunger
strike but said he would consider Sitaram’s wish in a positive manner.
The hunger strike led to anxieties in Andhra Pradesh and created a tense
atmosphere. Aacharya Vinobha Bhave interfered in the issue and advised
Sitaram to give up the hunger strike. Hence, Swami Sitaram gave up his
fast on September 20, 1951. The 35-day fast resulted in a peaceful end.
Nothing was achieved, and the people of Andhra lost confidence in the
Congress leaders.

5.2.6. Madras Assembly Elections 1952 :

The first general elections along with state assemblies in
independent India were held in 1952. The resentment of the Andhra
people towards the Congress Party was reflected in the Madras State
Assembly elections. The Congress got only 152 seats out of 375 in the
Madras State Assembly. In the Andhra Pradesh region, Congress won
only 43 seats out of 140. The Communist Party improved its position by
getting 41 seats in the Madras State Assembly. The prominent leaders of
the Congress Party, Kala Venkatrao, Bezawada, Gopala Reddy, and
Sanjeeva Reddy, also lost their elections.

The Kisan Mazdoor Party :

This was founded by Tanguturi Prakasam, formed an alliance
with the Communists as the United Democratic Front. The United
Democratic Front has the highest strength in the Madras Legislative
Assembly. But the Madras Governor asked Congress leader
Rajagopalachari to form the cabinet and not United Democratic Front
leader Prakasam. Rajagopala Chari had contempt for the Andhras from
the beginning. He was averse to the formation of Andhra Pradesh. He
ignored the interests of the Andhras and tried to benefit the Tamils. After
doing injustice to the Andhras, he started the Krishna Pennar project

and decided to move the waters of the Krishna River to Madras. Against
this backdrop, deep discontent erupted in Andhra Pradesh. The Kosla
Commission, appointed by the Centre, opined that the Krishna Pennar
project is not technically sound and should be replaced by the Nandikonda
project.

5.2.7. Martyrdom of Potti Sri Ramulu :

In such circumstances, Potti Sri Ramulu, who thought that the
Andhras would be treated with extreme injustice in the Madras state, felt
that there was no other way to achieve a separate state than a hunger
strike. On October 19, 1952, he started a hunger strike at Bulusu
Sambamurthy’s house in Madras. The objectives of his initiation were
the speedy establishment of Andhra Pradesh and unity among Andhra
leaders. The central government ignored this hunger strike earlier.
Gradually, the health of Potti Sriramulu deteriorated. Nehru announced
that he would look into the Andhra Pradesh problem. But Sri Ramulu
did not believe that statement. Nehru also criticised the hunger strike.
After 58 days of fasting, Potti Sri Ramulu died on the night of December
15, 1952.

Sri Ramulu’s self-immolation turned the Andhra region into a fire
pit. The emotions of the Telugu people broke free. Demonstrations, hartals,
and riots took place all over Andhra Pradesh in an unprecedented manner
for three days. Railway stations, post offices, and government offices
were destroyed. Police firing took place in many places. Seven people
died in the firing. Many were injured. The conditions of the Quit India
Movement were created throughout the Andhra region. By that time, the
central government had opened its eyes. On December 19, 1952, Nehru
announced in the Lok Sabha that Andhra Pradesh would be formed
immediately with the noncontroversial Telugu regions. With this, normal
conditions have been established in Andhra Pradesh. Potti Sriramulu’s
self-immolation won a separate state for the Andhras.

Vanchu Commission :

The Central Government has appointed Rajasthan High Court Chief
Justice Kailasanad Vanchu to examine all the matters necessary for the
formation of Andhra Pradesh and submit a report in accordance with
Nehru’s announcement in Parliament. He toured Andhra Pradesh
extensively and submitted his report on March 23, 1953. Following this
report, the Central Government announced that a new state could be
formed with Srikakulam, Visakhapatnam, Godavari, Krishna, Guntur,

Nellore, Chittoor, Kadapa, Anantapur, Kurnool districts, Aluru, Adoni,
and Rayadurgam taluks in Bellary.

At the same time, the representatives of Rayalaseema requested
to establish the capital of Andhra Pradesh in Rayalaseema as per the Sri
Bagh Agreement. But the communists insisted that the capital stay in
Vijayawada. With this, again, differences of opinion arose between the
Rayalaseema Circar districts. Their arguments broke out over the capital’s
decision. Neelam Sanjeeva Reddy, who was elected as the leader of the
Andhra Congress Party, sought the help of Tanguturi Prakasam and N.G
Ranga. The Kisan Mazdoor Party had already split into the Praja Socialist
Party under the leadership of Prakasam and the Krishikar Lok Party
under the leadership of Ranga. Fearing the communists, they decided to
join hands with the Congress again. The Andhra members of the Madras
Legislative Assembly met in Madras to decide the capital of the Andhra
state. The Congress Praja Socialist Party and Krishi Kar Lo Party
accepted Kurnool as the capital. But the Krishikar Lok Party changed
its decision in the Madras legislative session and demanded Tirupati as
the capital. The Communists proposed an amendment to have the capital
between Guntur and Vijayawada, but it was defeated by a margin of one
vote. Rajagopala Chari advised non-Andhras not to participate in this
voting. However, five non-Andhras voted in favour of Kurnool. Kurnool
became the capital of the new state. On October 1, 1953, a separate
Andhra Pradesh state was formed with Kurnool as its capital, and
Tanguturi Prakasam was the first Chief Minister. Neelam Sanjeeva Reddy
became Deputy Chief Minister. Trivedi was the first governor. In 1954,
the Andhra Pradesh Legislature passed a resolution to establish the High
Court in Guntur. Andhra Pradesh’s long wish has been fulfilled.
5.2.8.Potti Sriramulu’s Life and Achievements :

Potti Sriramulu sacrificed his life for the cause of Andhra Pradesh.
He was a true Gandhian. A patriot, humble, and selfless. Potti Sriramulu
was born on March 16, 1911, in Madras, in an Arya Vaishya family. His
father is Guruvayya. Mother Mahalakshmamma. After completion of
education, I worked in railways. After the deaths of his wife and mother,
he developed a theological tendency. Under these circumstances, he
resigned from his job and joined the Sabarmati Ashram. Through his
good conduct, he became a close disciple of Gandhiji. As a disciple of
Gandhiji, he took part in the Non-Cooperation Movement, Salt
Satyagraha, Civil Disobedience Movement, and Quit India Movements
and was jailed several times.

--- PAGE 294 ---
Apart from participating in the national movement and serving
the country, he also rendered many services to the people in the social
sector. Dharwad Village Seva Sangam was started by Shri Narayana
Das, a relative of Gandhiji. Sriramulu joined Gandhi and served the people
for two and a half years. Later, he came to Andhra Pradesh and joined
the ashrams of Gandhiji in Komaravolu and Angaluru, rendering immense
service to the Andhras. He did his best for constructive programmes like
Khaddar, Prohibition of Liquor, Harijan Development, and Hindu-Muslim
Unity. Sri Ramulu’s efforts to remove untouchability in Andhra Pradesh
are remarkable. From 1944 on, he worked for the upliftment of Harijan,
with Nellore as the centre. In 1946, he went on a fast for 23 days and
made Harijans enter the Sri Venugopala Swamy temple in Mulapeta,
Nellore town. After independence, in 1948, the Madras government
responded to his hunger strike for 29 days for the support of Harijans
and issued an order to celebrate Harijana Welfare Day on the 30th of
every month.

Potti Sriramulu was saddened by the neglect of the Central
Government for a separate Andhra state, even though it was Andhra’s
long-standing desire after independence. According to the JVP Report,
Swami Sitaram’s hunger strike did not yield any results. In 1952, Shri
Rajagopalachari, who was the Chief Minister of Madras, took steps that
were detrimental to the interests of the Andhras. Potti Sriramulu started
a fast to death on October 19, 1952, at Bulusu Sambamurthy’s residence
in Madras for the realisation of three goals. One of his wishes is that the
centre should make an announcement that the formation of Andhra Pradesh
will take place immediately; two, the differences between Andhra leaders
should be resolved; and three, Madras should be made a free state and
a chief commissioner should be appointed. Potti Sri Ramulu’s health is
deteriorating so much that the Central Government has not taken any
measures. An all-party meeting held by Tanguturi Prakasam on December
7 to save him failed. Later, as per the wish of the Andhra leaders,
Jawaharlal Nehru sent a telegram to Sriramulu Garu to give up the fasting
and the initiation so that he could look into the Andhra state formation
issue on December 9. But Shri Ramulu remembered that Nehru had not
fulfilled the promises made earlier and continued the initiation. Finally, on
December 15, 1952, Potti Sriramulu passed away. There was anarchy
in Andhra Pradesh. With this, Nehru announced the formation of Andhra
Pradesh, excluding Madras town, on December 19, 1952, in Parliament.

In this way, Andhra Pradesh was born with the self-sacrifice of Potti Sri

Ramulu.

5.3. Andhra Pradesh (1956) :

The formation of Andhra Pradesh is the consolidation of
Telangana, Coastal Andhra, and Rayalaseema as a single state. Coastal
Andhra and Rayalaseema were handed over to the East India Company
during the Asafzahi period. The Telangana region remained under the
administration of the Nizams as part of Hyderabad State. In 1948,
Hyderabad State was annexed to India by police action. The Government
of India has made Hyderabad a state. The 1956 movement for the
consolidation of the Telangana region of Hyderabad state and Andhra
state was known as the Visalandhra Movement.

5.3.1 Circumstances that Led to the Visalandhra Movement :

The movement was led by the aspiration of all Telugu-speaking
people to come under one administration. Telangana was backward during
Nizam rule. Telugu language and literature lacked encouragement. So,
people wanted all Telugu-speaking people to come to one state. The
concept of Visalandhra germinated in coastal districts.

Y In 1912, Unnavava Lakshminarayana and Jonnavittula Gurunatham
created a portrait of Andhra Pradesh combined with the Telangana,
Circar, and Seeded districts.

vY Inthe second Andhra Mahasabha held in 1914, President Nyapati
Subbarao proposed that all the people of the local Telugu kingdoms
should be brought under one rule.

Y In1931, the British Government declared its aim to form a federal
constitution with princely states and British India provinces. This
concept gradually spread as representatives of Telangana attended
Andhra Mahasabha meetings. But it did not gain prominence until
1936.

Y In 1936, the Andhra Congress Swaraj Party, in its election manifesto,
proposed that the Circars and Rayalaseema should merge into all
the Telugu-speaking areas of Telangana to form Andhra State.

Y  InDecember 1937, Acharya Mamidipudi Venkatarangaiah, Principal
of Andhra University Colleges, Visakhapatnam, said that all Telugu-
speaking areas should be formed into one state with Hyderabad as
its capital. The Andhra National Flag, designed in 1938 by the
president of the Andhra Parliament, Pandrangi Keshava Rao, clearly
represents the three Telugu regions.

Y In 1940, Kadapa Kotireddy opined that the formation of Visalandhra
was an appropriate step. Sir Vijaya, who presided over the Andhra
Mahasabha meetings held in Visakhapatnam in 1941 and Bellary in
1943, was a supporter of the Visalandhra formation. In October
1942, in the meeting of the Andhra Mahasabha working group, it
was decided that the British Andhra regions should be merged with
the Telugu regions of Hyderabad State to form Visalandhra state.
1954: The Hyderabad State Congress unanimously demanded the
separation of Hyderabad State and the integration of Telangana,
Marathwada, and Karnataka with their linguistic states.

5.3.2. Role of Communists :

The Communist Party of Hyderabad State had close relations
with the Andhra Communist Party. It actually functions as a branch of the
Andhra Communist Party, headquartered in Vijayawada. During the
Andhra Movement in British Andhra, Communists became a great force
in Telangana politics. They brought out the concept of Visalandhra on
many occasions. The important communist leaders that propagated the
cause of Visalandhra were Puchchalapalli Sundarayya, Ravi Narayana
Reddy, Baddam Ella Reddy, and Arutla Ramachandra Reddy. Other
Andhra and Telangana leaders also strengthened this movement.
Puchchalapally Sundarayya wrote a book named ‘Visalandhra
Prajarayyam’. He named the Communist Party magazine “Vilandhra.”
Mainly, Communist Party leaders worked for the Visalandhra movement.
Communists propagated the concept of Visalandhra even before the
formation of Andhra Pradesh. The press took this movement to the
masses. Since 1946, the Communists have advocated the formation of
integrated linguistic states.

5.3.3. The First Visalandhra Mahasabha :

The Visalandhra Mahasabha was founded in November 1949
at Vijayawada under the leadership of Ayyadevara Kaleswara Rao. The
first meeting of the Visalandhra MahaSabha was held in Warangal in
1950. Many leaders from the Andhra and Telangana regions participated
in this meeting. It was resolved that Visalandhra should be built as the
capital of Hyderabad. The prominent leaders that attended the session
were Kodati Rajalingam, Dr. Murthy, T.S. Hayagriva Chary, Swamy
Ramanandathirtha, D.G. Bindu, K.V. Reddy, M. Rama Koteswara Rao,
T. Prakasam, P. Thimmaa Reddy,Chilukuri Narayana Rao, B.S. Murthy,
N. Narayana Murthy, Vaman Murthy, Sardar Venkateswarlu, etc.

5.3.4. Congress Meetings :

The Hyderabad State Congress meeting was held in Nizamabad
in 1950 and in Hyderabad in 1953. It was resolved that the state of
Hyderabad should be divided on the basis of language into Vishalandhra,
United Maharashtra, and United Karnataka. A sub-committee was
appointed to prepare the necessary memorandum to be submitted to the
State Reorganisation Committee appointed by the Central Government.
In 1953, the Andhra Provincial Congress Committee resolved to form
Visalandhra Hyderabad as its capital. These two resolutions strengthened
the demand for Visalandhra.

5.3.5. The Second Visalandhra MahaSabha :

In 1954, the second Visalandhra MahaSabha was held in
Hyderabad. In this meeting, the argument of a special Telangana was
raised by some leaders. But Swami Ramananda Tirtha supported
Visalandhra. He said that a separate Telangana would be a weak state,
and democratic forces would be suppressed.

5.3.6. Role of Leaders and Newspapers :

Devulapalli Ramanuja Rao, Paga Pullareddy, Kodati Raju
Lingam, Hayagrivachari,Hayagrivachari etc. propagated the Visalandhra
Movement. Devarapalli Ramanuja Rao discussed this matter with Andhra
Congress leaders. He published a book and some pamphlets. A
delegation headed by Baga Pullar Reddy toured the Andhra and
Telangana regions and propagated the idea of Visalandhra. Leaders like
M. Narasimha Rao, Pandita Narendraji, Mehdi Nawaz Jung, Mir
Mohammad Ali Khan, Mukdum Mohinuddin, etc. explained the benefits
of Vishalandhra to the people through their announcements. Educated
Muslims also supported the concept of Visalandhra. These efforts created
a positive attitude towards Visalandhra among the people. All Telangana
newspapers like Andhra Janata, Telugu Desam, Kakatiya, etc. published
articles stating the need for Vishalandhra and took the concept of
Vishalandhra to the masses.
5.3.7.1952 Elections and Local Politics :

In 1952, elections were held in Hyderabad state. By then, the
communists had abandoned the armed struggle and participated in the
elections. They have more seats in Telangana. But the Congress party
got more seats in Kannada and Maratha areas of Hyderabad state. Thus,
the Congress Party got the majority in the Hyderabad Legislative
Assembly. Congress Party leader Burgula Ramakrishna Rao was
appointed as Chief Minister of Hyderabad State.

5.3.8. Appointment of State Reorganisation Committee :

With the formation of Andhra Pradesh in 1953, there was a
demand for the formation of linguistic states across the country. On
December 22, 1953, Prime Minister Nehru announced in the Lok Sabha
that he was appointing a State Reconstruction Committee in response to
public opinion. A committee was appointed under the chairmanship of
Syed Fazal Ali. H.N. Kunjru and K.M. Panikkar were other members
of this association. This committee was to submit its report by June 1955.
The Fazl Ali Commission was accepted by the Telugu people.

Telugu, Kannada, and Maharashtra regions of Hyderabad state
agreed to partition. With the appointment of the state reorganisation
committee, separate Telangana and Vishalandhra arguments came to the
fore again. Both are ready to showcase their strengths. In the month of
October 1953, Nehru criticised the Visalandhra movement as Andhra
imperialism. From that day on, the separate Telangana movement got
stronger. Mahadeva Singh of the Hyderabad Praja Socialist Party also
strengthened arguments for Telangana state. Konda Venkata Ranga
Reddy strengthened the Visalandhra movement until 1953. But in 1954,
after being elected president of the Telangana Congress, he declared
that he was a special Telangana man. Marri Chenna Reddy and K.V.
Ranga Reddy demanded a separate Telangana state. In June 1953, the
general meeting of the Hyderabad Congress Committee decided to divide
the state of Hyderabad into three states to form the states of Visalandhra,
United Maharashtra, and United Karnataka. But in 1954, the Hyderabad
Congress Committee rejected this resolution. It was announced that their
representatives should meet and decide about the formation of states for
the respective languages. The representatives of Maratwada decided to
join Maharashtra, while the representatives of Karnataka decided to
join Karnataka. But there were differences of opinion among the leaders
in Telangana. Telangana Communists and a few Congressmen
strengthened Visalandhra. In Andhra Pradesh, however, there is consensus
for the establishment of Visalandhra.

5.3.9. Recommendations of the Fazal Ali Commission :

The Fazal Ali Commission gathered the opinions of people from
various institutions in Hyderabad in the month of June 1954. They recetved
many petitions. The report was submitted in September 1955.

Highlights of this report were :

1.

All regions want the division of Hyderabad State. Hyderabad should
be divided, Kannada areas should be merged with Mysore, and
Marathwada areas should be merged with Bombay State.
The Visalandhra arrangement has many benefits. With the presence
of three crore Andhras, the state has access to vast water resources,
mineral wealth, electric power, and raw materials. If Hyderabad is
made the capital city, the problem of the permanent capital of Andhra
Pradesh will also be solved. Moreover, the creation of Visalandhra
would result in a unified authority over the waters of the Krishna
Godavari River. The construction of many dams will provide
opportunities for the development of the state. This will benefit both
areas. Food grains available in the Telangana region can also be
used. The merging of the two regions will also reduce administrative
costs. The majority of members of the Telangana state legislature
want Visalandhra.
But it is strange that the Fazal Ali Commission condones their desire
for a separate Telangana by saying that the establishment of
Visalandhra is exemplary. This commission was of the opinion that it
is not possible to dismiss the special claims of Telangana. K.V.
Rangareddy and Marri Chenna Reddy argued that Telangana people
are being treated unfairly in the fields of finance and education in the
new state. Therefore, this commission recommended that Telangana
be a separate state. If two-thirds of the legislators in the Telangana
Legislative Assembly to be formed in 1961 support Visalandhra,
Telangana can be merged with Andhra. Commenting on this report,
Tenneti Vishwanatham sarcastically commented that all the arguments
of the commission were in favour of Visalandhra and the decree was
in favour of a separate Telangana. Special Telangana advocates
expressed their joy at the contents of this report.

The Communists, on the other hand, announced that they would

resign from their positions and re-contest on the Vishalandhra issue. Most
of the Congress members in Telangana supported Vishalandhra. Seven
of the ten district congress unions have declared their support for the
formation of Visalandhra. When this matter was discussed in the
Hyderabad Legislative Assembly, 147 out of 174 members expressed
their views. 103 of them supported Visalandhra, and 29 supported a
separate Telangana. 15 people were neutral. During this time, the Chief

Minister of Hyderabad State, Burgula Ramakrishna Rao, acted wisely.

In favour of public opinion, he went to Delhi and informed the Congress

leadership about the Visalandhra arrangements. Finally, the Congress

leadership gave its approval to the formation of Visalandhra. But Konda

Venkata Ranga Reddy and Marri Chenna Reddy did not give up their

special Telangana cause. With that, to remove the differences of opinion

between Andhra and Telangana leaders, the Congress leadership
organised a meeting in Delhi in February 1956 with the representatives
of the two regions and concluded an agreement.

5.3.10. Gentlemen’s Agreement 1956 :

The agreement signed between the Congresses leaders of Andhra
Pradesh and Telangana is known as the Gentlemen’s Agreement. Among
these, Chief Minister of Andhra State Bejawada Gopala Reddy, Ministers
Neelam Sanjiva Reddy and Gauthu Lacchanna, and Pradesh Congress
President Alluri Satyanarayana Raju were present from the Andhra region.
From the Telangana region, Hyderabad Chief Minister Burgula
Ramakrishna Rao, Ministers Konda Venkata Reddy and Marri Chenna
Reddy, and Hyderabad Congress President J.V. Narasimha Rao
participated in this meeting. Discussions were held at the Hyderabad
guest house in Delhi. Later, the leaders of both sides signed an agreement
agreeing to the creation of Visalandhra and providing protection to
Telangana, which is known as the Gentlemen’s Agreement.

Highlights of the Gentlemen’s Agreement :

1. Andhra Pradesh and Telangana shall bear the central general
administrative expenditure of the state in proportion. Surplus funds
from the Telangana region should be used only for the development
of Telangana. This method should be revised after five years, and if
the legislators of Telangana agree, it should be extended for another
five years.

2. The prohibition on liquor in Telangana should be implemented only
according to the decision of the legislators of the Telangana region.

3. Theregion should get the total educational facilities of Telangana.
Priority should be given to Telangana in educational development.
Only Telangana students should have admission in all educational
institutions, including all technical educational institutions of the
Telangana region, or they should be allotted one-third of the seats in
all educational institutions of the state.

10.

If the jobs have to be reduced due to the merger of the states, it
should be on a proportionate basis.
The recruitment should be done on the basis of the population of
both regions in the future, in the ratio of the two regions.
The position of Urdu in the administrative and legal departments of
the Telangana region should continue for five years. After that, the
matter can be looked into by the regional committee. There should
be no requirement to learn Telugu at the time of joining the job.
Within two years of joining the job, they have to pass the prescribed
Telugu examination.
A regional council should be set up for all-round development of the
region in accordance with the needs of Telangana.
The sale of agricultural lands in the Telangana region should take
place under the supervision of the regional council only.
12 years of permanent residence in the region are mandatory for
Telangana region jobs.
The regional council will have 20 members. Nine members from
MLAs. Each MLA should be elected from the district through its
district members. 6 members from the assembly or parliament elected
by Telangana; 5 members from outside elected by Telangana.
This regional body should be given statutory powers. This association
has decision-making powers in Telangana region planning,
development, irrigation facilities, industrial development, and job
placement.
Incases of conflict between this association and the state government,
the central government takes the final decision. This arrangement
should be reviewed after 10 years.
Andhra Pradesh and Telangana should have a 3:1 ratio of
representation in the cabinet. Telangana ministers must have one
Muslim.
If the chief minister is elected from Andhra Pradesh, the post of
deputy chief minister should be given to Telangana. If the Chief
Minister is elected from Telangana, the post of Deputy Chief Minister
should be given to Andhra Pradesh. Telangana should be given any
of two in the Home Department: Finance, Revenue, Planning and
Development, Commerce, and Industry.
Hyderabad Pradesh Congress presidents wanted a separate
Telangana Pradesh Congress until 1962, to which the Andhra
Congress did not object.

As soon as the Gentlemen’s Agreement was signed, the efforts to
establish Visalandhra gained momentum. The Andhra State
Legislature met in Kurnool and passed a unanimous decision seeking
the merger of Andhra and Telangana. On November 1, 1956, Neelam
Sanjiva Reddy became the first Chief Minister, and C.M. Trivedi
became the first Governor of Andhra Pradesh.
